* HOW TO USE THIS DOCUMENT

** Read this page first. It is the only page you must read.

This is long. That is on purpose — money is a subject where the missing 10% is usually the
part that costs you. But long does not mean you read it front to back in one sitting.

Here is how to actually use it:

0. *Every part begins with a box marked "IN ONE MINUTE"* — the whole chapter in plain
   English, no jargon, about a paragraph or two. Reading only those boxes, front to back,
   takes about twenty minutes and gives you the entire course in outline. If you read
   nothing else in this document, read those. Everything after each box is the detail for
   when you actually need it.
1. Read *Part 0* (the ten sentences) — five minutes. If you only ever read that, you are
   already ahead of most adults.
2. Read *Part 1* (Foundations) — this is the part that changes your life this month.
3. Read *Part 18* (The Plan) — a numbered checklist of what to do, in what order, starting
   this week.
4. Everything in between is a *reference manual*. When you hit a real situation — a job
   offer, an insurance salesman, a mortgage, a tax form, a move to Israel — come back and
   read that chapter properly.

*In Emacs:* this file opens folded (=#+STARTUP: overview=). =TAB= on a heading opens it,
=S-TAB= cycles the whole document, =C-c C-n= / =C-c C-p= jump between headings, and =C-c /=
builds a sparse tree from a search. If you are reading this outside Emacs, it is still just
a text file and the stars are just headings.

** How this document is written

Every term is defined *before* it is used, in plain words, the first time it appears. No
exceptions. If you hit a word you don't know and I didn't define it, that is a bug in the
document. There is also a full glossary in Appendix A.

Where there is a number, I show the arithmetic. Not because you can't multiply, but because
almost every bad financial decision in the world is made by someone who never did the
multiplication.

Where something is a matter of genuine dispute, I say so. Where I'm giving you the boring,
mainstream, overwhelmingly-evidence-supported answer, I say that too. Those are different
kinds of claim and you deserve to know which one you're getting.

** A warning about the numbers

Dollar figures — contribution limits, tax brackets, standard deductions — *change every
single year*. Any guide that scatters them through the text is stale within twelve months.

So: the hard numbers live in *Appendix B*, in one table, with instructions for refreshing
them in about sixty seconds. In the body I use round approximations and say "roughly."
Treat Appendix B as the source of truth, and treat even Appendix B as "verify before you
act." I will tell you exactly where to verify.

** The twenty words that block everything else

Most financial writing is impenetrable not because the ideas are hard but because twenty
ordinary-looking words are used in a special way and nobody stops to say so. Here they are,
in plain English. Learn these and the rest of the document reads like ordinary writing.

| Word            | What it actually means                                                |
|-----------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Gross           | Before anything is taken out. The number on the job offer              |
| Net             | After everything is taken out. The number that hits the bank           |
| Asset           | Something you own that has value                                       |
| Liability       | Something you owe                                                      |
| Principal       | The actual amount borrowed, separate from the interest on top          |
| Interest        | The rent you pay for using someone else's money                        |
| APR             | The interest rate for a whole year, written as a percentage            |
| Compound        | Your growth starts earning its own growth. Why starting young wins     |
| Liquid          | Easy to turn into cash quickly. A savings account is liquid; a house isn't |
| Equity          | The part of a thing you actually own. House worth $400K with a $300K loan = $100K of equity |
| Deduction       | Reduces the income you get taxed on                                    |
| Credit (tax)    | Reduces the tax bill itself, dollar for dollar. Much better            |
| Refundable      | A credit that pays you cash even if your tax bill is already zero      |
| Withholding     | Money taken out of your paycheck and sent to the government in advance |
| Pretax          | Money that goes somewhere before tax is applied to it                  |
| Premium         | What you pay every month to have insurance                             |
| Deductible      | What you pay out of your own pocket before insurance pays anything     |
| Vesting         | When money your employer put aside actually becomes yours to keep      |
| Diversify       | Own many different things, so no single failure hurts much             |
| Beneficiary     | The person who gets an account when you die. Beats your will           |

Two more that aren't words but are ideas people trip on:

- *An account is a container, not an investment.* An IRA is a box. You still have to choose
  what goes in the box. People open the box, put money in, buy nothing, and are baffled
  years later when nothing grew.
- *Rate and amount are different questions.* "It's only $300 a month" tells you nothing until
  you know for how many months and at what rate. Salespeople rely on this.

** The situation this is written around

The course is general — anyone can use it. But a guide that never commits to a specific
situation stays vague forever, so where it matters I write to one concrete household:

| Fact                        | What it changes                                          |
|-----------------------------+----------------------------------------------------------|
| Married, one year           | Filing status is *Married Filing Jointly* everywhere (§4.6) |
| One baby daughter, more expected | Sinking funds, insurance amounts, tuition math, benefits eligibility |
| Husband in kollel, studying CS | Little or no earned income now; a large income later    |
| Wife working                | The household's earned income — which unlocks the *spousal IRA* (§8.6) |
| Starting from zero          | No prior accounts, no credit history, nothing assumed     |
| Living in the US            | American track is primary                                 |
| Israel possible later       | Part 14 exists, and several decisions must be made *before* a move |

If your situation differs, the machinery is the same and only the numbers change. Where a
section is specific to this household rather than general, I say so in the text.

** What this document is not

It is not legal, tax, or investment advice from a licensed professional. It is an
*education*. The difference matters: education tells you how the machine works and which
questions to ask. Advice tells you what to do in your specific case.

After reading this you will be able to walk into a CPA's office, or sit across from an
insurance salesman, and have a real conversation instead of nodding politely. That is worth
considerably more than any single piece of advice.

There are a handful of places where I say "hire a professional." I mean it in those places
and I deliberately don't say it everywhere else.

* PART 0 — THE WHOLE COURSE IN TEN SENTENCES

If the building were on fire and you could save one page, save this one.

1. *Spend less than you bring in.* Every other idea in personal finance is a footnote to
   this one. There is no investment clever enough to outrun a spending problem.

2. *Know where the money goes.* Not approximately. You cannot fix a number you have never
   looked at.

3. *Keep a cash cushion* — money sitting in a boring bank account doing nothing, so that a
   broken transmission is an annoyance instead of a crisis that puts you on a credit card
   at 25% interest.

4. *Never carry a credit card balance.* A credit card is a free 30-day loan if you pay in
   full, and one of the most expensive loans in the legal economy if you don't.

5. *Insure the catastrophes, self-insure the annoyances.* Buy insurance for what would ruin
   you (death of an earner, a hospital stay, a lawsuit). Don't buy it for what you could
   absorb (a cracked phone, a warranty on a toaster).

6. *Get the free money first.* Employer retirement match, tax credits you qualify for, HSA
   contributions. Highest returns you will ever earn, available only by filling out a form.

7. *Invest in boring, cheap, broadly-diversified index funds, and then do nothing.* Not
   because it's exciting — because sixty years of data says the exciting version loses.

8. *Time is the actual asset.* A dollar invested at 24 is worth several dollars invested at
   44. You are 24. This is the largest financial advantage you will ever hold, and it is
   perishable.

9. *Taxes are a published set of rules*, not a mystery and not a moral failing to engage
   with. Learning them is worth thousands of dollars a year to a family like yours —
   legally, on the front of the form, in daylight.

10. *Almost nobody who wants to sell you a financial product is on your side.* Learn to ask
    "how are you paid?" and then sit quietly while they answer.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
Everything after this page is elaboration. If you internalize these ten and do nothing
else, you land in the top quarter of financial outcomes for people your age.
#+END_QUOTE

* PART 1 — FOUNDATIONS: HOW MONEY ACTUALLY MOVES

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Money comes in. Money goes out. If more goes out than comes in, nothing
clever you do later can save you.

So, in order: find out where your money actually goes — download three months of bank
statements and add them up. Most people have never once done this. Then put your money into
three separate accounts, so that bill money and grocery money don't sit in one pile looking
identical. Then save automatically, on payday, before you can spend it.

Big yearly costs like Pesach are not emergencies. They happen every year, on schedule. Take
the yearly cost, divide by twelve, and save that much every month. Pesach stops being a
crisis. That one trick removes most of the money stress in a young frum home.

Keep a pile of boring cash for actual emergencies. That's the whole part.
#+END_QUOTE

** 1.1 The only four things you can do with a dollar

A dollar that arrives in your life has exactly four possible fates. Not three, not five.

| Fate      | What it means                           | Example                              |
|-----------+-----------------------------------------+--------------------------------------|
| Spend it  | Trade it for something now              | Groceries, rent, diapers             |
| Save it   | Park it somewhere safe for later        | Emergency fund, Pesach fund          |
| Invest it | Put it to work so it makes more dollars | Index funds, retirement account      |
| Give it   | Transfer it to someone else             | Maaser, tzedakah, helping a chosson  |

That's it. Every financial decision you will ever make is a decision about which of these
four buckets a dollar goes into, and in what proportion.

The reason people feel financially lost is rarely that they chose wrong among the four.
It's that they never chose at all — the dollars arrived, drifted into "spend" by default,
and vanished. *Default is a decision.* It's just a decision made by inertia instead of you.

** 1.2 Cash flow: the picture to keep in your head

Picture a bathtub.

- Water in from the faucet — *income*.
- Water out the drain — *expenses*.
- The water level in the tub — *savings* (also called *cash*, or *liquidity*).
- Once the tub is comfortably full, you start bailing water into a separate barrel that
  slowly fills itself — *investing*.

*Income* is money coming in: paychecks, a kollel check, a side job, a gift, a tax refund.

*Expenses* are money going out: rent, food, tuition, insurance, gas.

*Cash flow* is faucet minus drain. Positive means the level rises. Negative means it falls.
Negative for long enough means the tub empties and you start borrowing — which is like
drilling a second, permanent drain, because now part of every future month leaks out as
interest before you've bought anything.

Everything in Part 1 is about making cash flow reliably positive and then doing something
intelligent with the surplus.

*** The two numbers you must know by heart

1. What comes in per month.
2. What goes out per month.

If you can't state both within about $200, you are flying a plane with the instrument panel
covered. §1.5 gets you both in under an hour.

** 1.3 Gross vs net: the first big lie

Say a job offer says =$65,000 per year=.

That is *gross pay* — the number before anything is taken out. It's the number people quote
at a vort. It is not money anyone will ever see.

What arrives in the bank account is *net pay*, also called *take-home pay*. In between,
several things get subtracted:

| Subtraction              | What it is                                       | Ballpark     |
|--------------------------+--------------------------------------------------+--------------|
| Federal income tax       | Tax to the US government                         | Varies a lot |
| Social Security tax      | 6.2% of pay, funds retirement/disability benefits| 6.2%         |
| Medicare tax             | 1.45% of pay, funds health coverage for elderly  | 1.45%        |
| State income tax         | Tax to your state (0% in some states)            | 0–7%         |
| Local/city income tax    | Some cities (NYC, Yonkers) tax you too           | 0–4%         |
| Health insurance premium | Your share of the employer health plan           | $50–600/mo   |
| Retirement contribution  | What you chose to put into a 401(k)              | Your choice  |

A $65,000 salary in Lakewood, New Jersey might land as roughly $4,100–4,500/month in the
bank. The same $65,000 in Brooklyn lands lower (New York State *plus* New York City tax).
The same $65,000 in Miami lands higher (Florida has no state income tax at all).

*The rule:* when anyone quotes you a salary, mentally cut 20–30% before planning with it.
When comparing two offers in two states, you are not comparing salaries — you are comparing
take-home pay, and that is a different comparison with a sometimes different winner.

*** Why this trips people at exactly the wrong moment

The most common young-family mistake in America: signing a lease or a mortgage against the
gross number. "We make $6,500 a month, so $2,200 rent is fine — that's a third." But you
don't make $6,500 a month. You make $4,900. And $2,200 out of $4,900 is 45%, which is a
completely different life with completely different stress levels.

Always plan against net. Always.

** 1.4 The three-account system (do this in your first week)

Most people run their whole financial life out of one checking account. Money in, money
out, the balance is some number, and "do we have money?" is answered by squinting at that
number and guessing.

The problem: that one number is a *blend* of money with totally different jobs. Rent money,
Pesach money, and genuinely spare money all sit in the same pile looking identical. So you
spend the Pesach money in Cheshvan without ever deciding to.

The fix is embarrassingly simple: *give money different rooms to sit in.*

*** Account 1 — Bills (a checking account)

- All income is deposited here.
- All fixed recurring bills auto-pay from here: rent, utilities, insurance, phone, car,
  tuition.
- No debit card attached. Ideally you barely look at it.

Its job is to be boring and to guarantee the rent never bounces.

*** Account 2 — Spending (a checking account)

- Once a month (or each payday) you move a fixed amount here.
- Groceries, gas, takeout, a sefer, clothing — everything variable.
- Your debit card is attached to *this* account.

Its job is to answer "can we afford this?" with a balance you can read without math. When
it's low, it's the end of the month and you eat what's in the freezer. That isn't
deprivation, that's the system working exactly as designed.

*** Account 3 — Savings (a high-yield savings account)

- Emergency fund (§1.8) lives here.
- Sinking funds (§1.7) live here.
- No debit card. Transfers out take a day or two, and that friction is a *feature*.

*** Optional Account 4 — Maaser

Some people keep a separate maaser account so the calculation stays visible and money that
isn't theirs never mixes with money that is. If maaser math is a recurring source of
stress, this solves it structurally. Halachic mechanics in §12.6.

*** Why this beats budgeting apps

A budget app is a *record* — it tells you afterward what you did. Separate accounts are a
*constraint* — they tell you in the moment what you can do. Constraints beat records,
because a constraint still works when you're exhausted, distracted, and holding a baby in
one arm at the checkout counter.

Cost: about forty minutes on your bank's website. Most online banks let you open several
accounts and nickname them ("PESACH", "CAR REPAIR", "SIMCHA FUND"). Some, like Ally, are
built around exactly this idea.

** 1.5 Finding out where the money actually goes

Before budgeting you must *observe*. Skipping this and going straight to a budget is how
people end up with a beautiful spreadsheet they abandon in three weeks.

*** The one-hour method

1. Log into your bank. Download the last three months of transactions as a CSV file. Every
   US bank offers this — look for "Download" or "Export" on the transactions page.
2. Open it in a spreadsheet (Excel, Google Sheets, LibreOffice — doesn't matter).
3. Add a column called =Category=.
4. Tag every line with one of about twelve categories. Don't be precious. Twelve, not
   forty. Forty categories is how you quit.
5. Sort by category and sum, or make a pivot table if you know how.

Suggested categories for a frum family:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
Housing        rent/mortgage, property tax, utilities, repairs
Food           groceries, shabbos, takeout (consider splitting groceries vs. eating out)
Tuition/School yeshiva, playgroup, babysitting, daycare
Transportation car payment, gas, insurance, repairs, transit
Insurance      health, life, disability (if not payroll-deducted)
Medical        copays, prescriptions, dental, therapy
Kids           diapers, clothing, gear, activities
Clothing       adults, yom tov clothes
Tzedakah       maaser and everything else given
Simchos        gifts, travel, sheva brachos, chasunah expenses
Yom Tov        Pesach, Sukkos, Purim — enormous and always underestimated
Debt Payments  credit card, loans, gemach
Miscellaneous  subscriptions, seforim, household, the swamp
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** What you will discover

Three things, essentially always:

1. *A subscription you forgot about.* Everyone has at least one. Cancel it today; that's
   your first win and it took four minutes.
2. *"Small purchases" total far more than you'd guess.* Fourteen dollars here and
   twenty-two there is six hundred a month and feels like nothing while it happens.
3. *Yom tov and simchos are gigantic and invisible*, because they're lumpy — nothing for
   four months, then $2,800 in three weeks. That's precisely what §1.7 exists to solve.

Do not feel bad about what you find. Everyone's first pass is ugly. The point is
information, not judgment. You cannot be ashamed of a number you didn't know you had.

** 1.6 Budgeting: four systems, pick one

A *budget* is a plan for money written before the month, instead of discovered after it.

There is no single correct system. There is only the system you will actually keep doing.
Here are the four real ones, honestly compared.

*** System A — The Two-Number Budget (simplest thing that works)

Track exactly two numbers: fixed costs, and everything else.

1. Add up all fixed monthly bills (rent, insurance, car, phone, tuition). Call it =F=.
2. Add up all savings goals you want funded monthly (§1.7, §1.8). Call it =S=.
3. Monthly net income minus =F= minus =S= is your spending money. It lives in Account 2.

That's the whole system. No categories, no app, no tracking. When Account 2 is empty,
you're done spending until next month.

- *Best for:* people who have tried budgeting three times and quit three times.
- *Weakness:* zero visibility into *why* the spending money ran out.

*** System B — Zero-Based Budgeting (the YNAB approach)

Every dollar is assigned a job before the month begins. Income minus all assignments equals
exactly zero. Overspend one category and you must explicitly move money out of another —
the tradeoff is forced into the open where you have to look at it.

- *Best for:* people who like systems, and people with irregular income.
- *Weakness:* real engagement several times a week, forever. YNAB costs money and has a
  genuine learning curve. High ceiling, high floor.

*** System C — Percentage Budgeting (50/30/20 and a frum variant)

The famous version: 50% needs, 30% wants, 20% savings.

For a frum family this is unrealistic as written, because tuition, yom tov and maaser blow
up the "needs" bucket. A more honest target:

| Bucket                               | Target                       |
|--------------------------------------+------------------------------|
| Maaser / tzedakah                    | 10%                          |
| Housing (rent + utilities)           | ≤ 30%                        |
| Food (including shabbos and yom tov) | 12–18%                       |
| Tuition                              | whatever it is (see Part 12) |
| Transportation                       | ≤ 10%                        |
| Insurance + medical                  | 5–10%                        |
| Everything else                      | 10%                          |
| Savings + investing                  | ≥ 10%, more when you can     |

If those can't sum to 100% with room to spare, the problem isn't the budget — it's the
income or the fixed costs, and you fix that in Parts 10 and 12, not here.

- *Best for:* a sanity check on whether your life is structurally affordable.
- *Weakness:* it's a diagnostic, not a daily tool.

*** System D — Pay Yourself First (what I'd actually recommend to you)

Skip micro-tracking entirely. Automate the order of operations:

1. Paycheck lands in the Bills account.
2. Automatic transfer, same day: maaser out.
3. Automatic transfer, same day: savings and investing out.
4. Automatic transfer, same day: a fixed spending allowance to the Spending account.
5. Bills auto-pay from the Bills account.
6. Whatever is in the Spending account is what you have. Spend it freely, guilt-free.

You have now guaranteed the savings rate — the only number that actually determines your
long-term outcome — and freed yourself from tracking anything else.

- *Best for:* almost everyone, and especially a busy young couple with a baby.
- *Weakness:* needs stable-ish income to set the transfer amounts. Recalibrate every six
  months, or after any income change.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*The honest truth about budgeting:* the specific system matters far less than the
literature pretends. What matters is (a) savings comes out first, automatically, before you
can see it, and (b) you look at the numbers once a month *with your wife*. A mediocre
system done every month beats a brilliant system done twice.
#+END_QUOTE

*** The monthly money meeting

Twenty minutes, once a month, same time every month (Rosh Chodesh is an easy anchor).
Coffee. Both of you. Agenda:

1. What came in last month?
2. What went out, roughly by category?
3. Anything unusual coming up in the next 60 days?
4. Are the automatic transfers still the right size?
5. Net worth number, once a quarter (§1.9).

That's it. This meeting is the highest-return twenty minutes in your calendar, and the
main reason isn't the numbers — it's that money stops being a source of surprise between
two people, which is where most of the damage in a marriage actually happens.

** 1.7 Sinking funds: the technique that fixes most frum-family money stress

This is the most practically valuable section in Part 1. Read it twice.

*** The problem

Some expenses are monthly and predictable (rent). Easy — you feel them monthly, you plan
for them monthly.

Others are *large, certain, and irregular*. You know they're coming. You just don't know
exactly when, and they don't arrive monthly, so they never show up in a monthly budget.
Then they hit at once and feel like emergencies even though nothing surprising happened.

For a frum family the list is brutal:

- *Pesach* — food, cleaning, a program or hosting, new clothing. $1,500–5,000+.
- *Sukkos* — schach, lulav and esrog, food, travel, yom tov clothing. $800–2,500.
- *Purim and Chanukah* — mishloach manos, matanos l'evyonim, gifts. $300–1,000.
- *Simchos* — you will attend a *lot* of weddings, bar mitzvahs, brissim. Gifts and travel.
- *Camp* — summer, per child, and it starts younger than you think.
- *Car* — registration, inspection, tires, and the repair that is definitely coming.
- *Insurance premiums* billed every six or twelve months.
- *Medical deductible* — resets every January and pretends it's new.
- *Seforim, a sheitel, yom tov clothes* for kids who grow at a rate that defies physics.
- *A chasunah.* Not for eighteen years. See §12.4, because eighteen years is not long.

*** The technique

For each irregular expense: estimate the annual total, divide by twelve, and set that
amount aside *every single month* into a named savings bucket.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
Pesach:             $2,400/year  ÷ 12 = $200/month
Sukkos:             $1,200/year  ÷ 12 = $100/month
Other yom tov:        $600/year  ÷ 12 =  $50/month
Simchos/gifts:      $1,800/year  ÷ 12 = $150/month
Car maintenance:    $1,200/year  ÷ 12 = $100/month
Car insurance:      $1,800/year  ÷ 12 = $150/month
Medical deductible: $1,500/year  ÷ 12 = $125/month
Clothing:           $1,200/year  ÷ 12 = $100/month
                                        --------
                               TOTAL =   $975/month
#+END_EXAMPLE

That number will look shocking the first time you compute it. Sit with it. That money was
*always* being spent — you were just spending it in panicked bursts and covering the gap
with a credit card or a loan from your father-in-law. The sinking fund doesn't create the
expense. It reveals it. Revealing it is the entire point.

*** Running it mechanically

Two options:

1. *Multiple named savings accounts.* Ally, Capital One 360, SoFi, Marcus and most credit
   unions let you open ten sub-accounts and name them. Set an automatic monthly transfer
   into each. Clearest option, and where I'd start.
2. *One savings account plus a spreadsheet.* All money in one account; a spreadsheet tracks
   which portion "belongs" to which purpose. Fewer accounts, more discipline required.

*** What changes when you do this

In late Adar, you do not have a conversation about how you're going to handle Pesach. There
is $2,400 sitting in an account labeled PESACH. You spend it. That is the whole
conversation.

The stress in most young frum households is not poverty. It is *lumpiness*. Sinking funds
convert lumpiness into a flat monthly number, and a flat monthly number is something a
human being can actually plan around.

** 1.8 The emergency fund

*** What it is

Cash — actual, boring, immediately available cash — set aside for genuine emergencies. Not
invested. Not in stocks. Sitting in a savings account earning modest interest and, far more
importantly, sitting there.

*** What counts as an emergency

- Job loss or a sharp income drop.
- A medical event.
- A car that must be fixed to keep getting to work.
- An urgent home repair — burst pipe, dead boiler in January.
- An emergency flight for a family matter.

*** What does not count

- Pesach. That's a sinking fund. It happens every year. It is not a surprise.
- A wedding you've known about for four months.
- A sale.
- An "investment opportunity." Especially an investment opportunity.

*** How much

Standard advice is 3–6 months of *expenses* (not income — expenses, which is smaller and
more achievable). Refine by circumstance:

| Situation                                    | Target                |
|----------------------------------------------+-----------------------|
| Starting from zero, carrying debt             | $1,000–2,000 first    |
| Two stable incomes, no kids                   | 3 months              |
| One income, or a baby, or self-employed       | 6 months              |
| Single income, several kids, variable income  | 6–9 months            |
| Kollel + one working spouse (your case)       | 4–6 months            |

Your household runs on one real paycheck plus a kollel check. That is more fragile than it
feels, because if her job disappears, nearly all the income disappears at once. Aim for six
months eventually. Aim for *one month* immediately — the gap between $0 and $2,000 is far
larger, practically and psychologically, than the gap between $8,000 and $10,000.

*** Where to keep it

A *high-yield savings account* (HYSA). This is an ordinary savings account, usually at an
online bank, that pays a much higher interest rate than a big brick-and-mortar bank. Chase
might pay 0.01%. An online HYSA might pay 3.5–4.5%. Same FDIC insurance (§2.3), same
safety, same access in a day or two. There is no catch and no reason not to.

On $10,000 that's roughly $400 a year for filling out one form once. Best hourly rate
you'll ever earn.

*Do not* keep the emergency fund in stocks. The whole point is that it's there on the worst
day of your year, and the worst day of your year has an annoying habit of correlating with
the worst day of the stock market — you lose the job in the same recession that dropped the
market 30%, and now you're selling at the bottom to buy groceries.

*** Order of operations

"Should I build the emergency fund before paying off debt?" The standard sequence:

1. Small starter fund ($1,000–2,000).
2. Attack high-interest debt (anything above ~7–8%) aggressively.
3. Then build the full 3–6 month fund.
4. Then invest beyond retirement matching.

Why step 1 before step 2: with no cushion, the next $600 surprise goes on a credit card and
you're back where you started, discouraged, and now you believe the system doesn't work.
The starter fund is what makes step 2 finishable.

** 1.9 Net worth: the one scoreboard that matters

*Net worth* = everything you own (*assets*) minus everything you owe (*liabilities*).

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
ASSETS                            LIABILITIES
  Checking            $2,000        Credit card         $1,200
  Savings             $4,500        Car loan            $8,000
  Retirement          $3,000        Student loan            $0
  Car (resale value)  $9,000        Gemach loan         $2,000
  ------------------------          ------------------------
  Total              $18,500        Total             $11,200

  NET WORTH = $18,500 − $11,200 = $7,300
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** Why this number and not income

Income tells you how fast the faucet runs. Net worth tells you how much water is in the
tub. High-income, negative-net-worth people are extremely common — a doctor four years out
of residency with $300K of loans and a leased BMW has a big income and a worse financial
position than a rebbe with $40K in index funds and no debt.

Net worth is also allowed to be negative early. It does not need to be impressive. It needs
to be *trending up*.

*** How to track it

Once a quarter — before each yom tov works as an anchor — open a spreadsheet, list assets
and liabilities, subtract, and write down the date and the number. Ten minutes. Do it for
thirty years and you'll own the single most useful document of your financial life.

Do not check it more often. Quarterly is frequent enough to see a trend and rare enough not
to make you crazy when markets move.

** 1.10 Inflation, and why cash is not actually safe

*Inflation* is the general rise in prices over time — equivalently, the loss of purchasing
power of a dollar.

Historically in the US, roughly 2–3% a year, with occasional ugly spikes (2021–2023 hit
7–9%). "Roughly 3%" is the planning number.

Concretely: $100 in a shoebox today buys about $74 of goods in ten years and about $55 in
twenty. The number on the bill never changed. What it buys did.

*This is why a savings account is not a long-term home for money.* If savings pays 4% and
inflation is 3%, you gain 1% a year in real terms — fine for an emergency fund, useless for
a thirty-year goal. If savings pays 0.01% and inflation is 3%, you are losing about 3% a
year, quietly, forever, while the statement reassures you that the balance went up.

*The corollary that matters for you:* money you won't need for 15+ years must be invested in
something that grows faster than inflation, or the plan silently fails. That's Part 7.

*** Real vs nominal

- *Nominal* return: the number printed on the statement. "Up 7% this year."
- *Real* return: nominal minus inflation. Up 7% with 3% inflation is 4% real.

Real is the one that feeds your family. Whenever you see a return number, ask which one it
is. Long-run US stock market: roughly 10% nominal, roughly 7% real. Plan with 7% and you
won't embarrass yourself.

** 1.11 Opportunity cost, the idea that quietly runs everything

*Opportunity cost* is what you gave up in order to do the thing you did.

The $30,000 car doesn't cost $30,000. It costs $30,000 *plus* whatever that money would
have become. At 7% over thirty years, $30,000 becomes roughly $228,000. That's the real
price tag on the nicer car — not "an extra $12,000 versus the used one," but "an extra
$12,000, or $91,000 at retirement, pick one."

This is not an argument that you must always choose the cheaper thing. Sometimes the car,
the apartment, the trip to your parents, are worth it — and a life optimized purely for the
spreadsheet is a bad life. It's an argument that you should *know the real price* before you
decide, so that when you say yes, you're saying yes to the actual trade.

*The formula to keep in your pocket:* money at 7% roughly doubles every ten years. So a
dollar you don't spend today is about $2 in ten years, $4 in twenty, $8 in thirty, $16 in
forty. You are 24. Retirement is roughly forty years away. Every $1,000 you don't spend
today is roughly $16,000 at 65.

Sixteen thousand dollars is what that $1,000 costs. That is the whole argument for saving
in your twenties, and it does not get made more persuasively at 40.
* PART 2 — BANKS AND THE PLUMBING OF MONEY

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* A bank is a business, not a friend. It makes money when your cash sits in
an account paying you nothing, and when you pay it fees.

So: keep savings at an online bank that pays real interest — about 4% instead of 0.01%. Same
government protection, same safety, hundreds of dollars a year more. Never pay a monthly fee
or an overdraft fee; both are avoidable with one phone call.

Know which ways of sending money can be undone and which cannot. A credit card charge can be
disputed. A wire transfer, a Zelle payment, or crypto is gone the moment you send it.

One habit matters more than everything else here: *before you ever send a wire, phone the
person at a number you looked up yourself* — never a number from an email. That single habit
prevents the worst financial disaster that happens to ordinary American families.
#+END_QUOTE

** 2.1 What a bank actually does

A bank takes deposits from people who have money right now, lends most of that money to
people who need it right now, and keeps the difference between the interest it charges
borrowers and the interest it pays depositors. That difference is called the *spread*, and
it is essentially the entire business model.

Two consequences worth internalizing:

1. *Your money is not in a vault with your name on it.* It's been lent out. The bank owes
   you the money; it doesn't hold the money. This is fine and legal and how banking has
   worked for centuries, but it's why deposit insurance (§2.3) exists.
2. *The bank's incentives are not your incentives.* It profits when you keep low balances in
   accounts paying nothing, overdraft occasionally, and carry a credit card balance. A bank
   is a counterparty, not a friend. Perfectly good relationship — just know which one it is.

** 2.2 Types of accounts, in plain words

*** Checking account

A *checking account* is for money that moves. You write checks against it, attach a debit
card, pay bills from it, receive direct deposit into it. It usually pays little or no
interest. That's fine — its job is motion, not growth.

*** Savings account

A *savings account* is for money that sits. It pays interest. Historically banks limited
withdrawals to six per month (an old federal rule called Regulation D, suspended in 2020);
many banks still impose it out of habit, so check.

*** High-yield savings account (HYSA)

Same as a savings account, but at a bank that actually pays a competitive rate — typically
an online bank with no branches and therefore lower costs. The rate *floats*: it moves with
interest rates set by the Federal Reserve, so it goes up and down and no rate is promised
forever.

This is where your emergency fund and sinking funds should live. Full stop.

*** Money market account (MMA)

A hybrid: savings-like interest, sometimes with check-writing or a debit card. Often
requires a higher minimum balance. Not meaningfully different from an HYSA for your
purposes — compare on rate and fees, not on the name.

Careful: a *money market account* (a bank deposit, FDIC-insured) is a different thing from
a *money market fund* (an investment held at a brokerage, not FDIC-insured, though extremely
safe in practice). The names are almost identical and that is not an accident.

*** Certificate of Deposit (CD)

You give the bank money and promise not to touch it for a fixed term — six months, a year,
five years. In exchange you get a fixed, usually higher, interest rate. Take it out early
and you pay a penalty (typically a few months of interest).

Useful for money with a known date: "we're buying a car in eighteen months." Bad for an
emergency fund, because emergencies do not consult your maturity schedule.

*** Brokerage account

Not a bank account at all. It's an account at an investment firm (Fidelity, Schwab,
Vanguard) where you buy stocks, bonds, and funds. Covered in Part 7. Mentioned here only so
you know it lives in a different category and is protected by a different scheme (SIPC, not
FDIC — see §2.3).

** 2.3 FDIC and NCUA: why your money is safe

*FDIC* — Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. A US government agency that insures bank
deposits. If your bank fails, the FDIC makes you whole up to *$250,000 per depositor, per
insured bank, per ownership category*.

*NCUA* — the identical thing for credit unions, with identical limits. Different name,
same protection.

Parsing that limit, because people get it wrong:

- "Per depositor" — you, individually.
- "Per bank" — $250K at Chase and $250K at Ally are separately insured.
- "Per ownership category" — your individual account and your joint account with your wife
  are *different categories*. A joint account is insured to $250K *per owner*, so $500K on a
  joint account between the two of you.

Practically: a young family will not come near these limits for many years. What matters
right now is simply *confirming your bank is insured* — every legitimate US bank says "Member
FDIC" on its website and in its branch, and you can verify at =fdic.gov/resources/deposit-insurance/bankfind=.

*What is NOT covered:* investments. Stocks, bonds, mutual funds, ETFs, crypto — even if you
bought them through a bank. Those are covered (partially, and against a different risk) by
*SIPC*, which protects up to $500,000 if your *brokerage* fails and your assets go missing.
SIPC does not protect you against your investments going down in value. Nothing does, and
anyone offering to is selling something (see §6.9).

** 2.4 Choosing a bank

*** The three-bank setup I'd suggest

1. *An online bank for savings* — Ally, Marcus, Capital One 360, SoFi, Discover, Amex
   Savings. High rate, no fees, easy sub-accounts for sinking funds.
2. *A local bank or credit union for checking* — you occasionally need a branch: a cashier's
   check for a closing, a notary, a wire, depositing an awkward paper check, or a safe
   deposit box.
3. *Possibly a big national bank* if you'll be traveling or moving — Chase and similar have
   branches everywhere including Israel-adjacent conveniences (though see Part 14 for what
   actually happens when you move).

*** Credit unions specifically

A *credit union* is a not-for-profit financial cooperative owned by its members instead of
by shareholders. Practically that usually means better rates on loans, better rates on
savings, lower fees, and worse technology. Membership requires eligibility — by employer,
by geography, sometimes by an affiliated organization. Many are easy to join.

If you're going to borrow (car loan, mortgage), get a quote from a credit union. They are
routinely a percentage point cheaper, and a percentage point on a mortgage is tens of
thousands of dollars.

*** What to actually compare

| Feature                        | Why it matters                                    |
|--------------------------------+---------------------------------------------------|
| Monthly maintenance fee        | Should be $0. Never pay this. Many banks waive it |
| Minimum balance requirement    | Should be $0 or trivially low                     |
| Overdraft policy               | See §2.6 — this one can cost hundreds             |
| ATM fee reimbursement          | Good online banks refund other banks' ATM fees    |
| APY on savings                 | The interest rate. Compare across banks yearly    |
| Zelle / instant transfer       | You will use this constantly                      |
| Mobile check deposit           | Photograph a check, it deposits. Table stakes now |
| Number of sub-accounts allowed | Matters for the sinking-fund system               |
| Wire transfer fee              | $15–35 typical. Matters at a closing              |
| Foreign transaction fee        | Matters if you'll be in Israel                    |

*APY* — Annual Percentage Yield. The interest rate you earn in a year, including the effect
of compounding. Use APY, not "interest rate," when comparing savings accounts; APY is the
apples-to-apples number.

** 2.5 How money actually moves between accounts

This is unglamorous plumbing that everyone is expected to already know and nobody was ever
taught.

*** ACH (Automated Clearing House)

The US system for electronic bank-to-bank transfers. Direct deposit of your paycheck is
ACH. Autopay of a bill is ACH. Moving money from checking to your online savings is ACH.

- *Speed:* 1–3 business days, though "same-day ACH" exists and is increasingly common.
- *Cost:* free, essentially always.
- *Reversible:* yes, in some circumstances — which makes it safer than a wire.
- *What you need:* the recipient's routing number and account number.

*Routing number* — a nine-digit code identifying the bank. Public information; it's printed
on your checks and on the bank's website.

*Account number* — identifies your specific account at that bank.

Together they're enough to *deposit* money into your account, and in some setups enough to
*withdraw* it. Treat them as semi-sensitive: fine to give to an employer for direct deposit,
not something to post publicly.

*** Wire transfer

A direct, near-immediate bank-to-bank transfer, usually same day.

- *Speed:* hours.
- *Cost:* $15–35 to send domestically; $35–60 internationally. Sometimes a fee to receive.
- *Reversible:* *no.* Effectively never. This is the crucial fact.
- *Used for:* real estate closings, large purchases, anything where the recipient needs
  certainty.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*The wire fraud warning — please read this one carefully.* The single most devastating
consumer fraud in America works like this: you're buying a house. Days before closing you
receive an email that looks exactly like it's from your title company or lawyer, with
updated wire instructions. You wire the down payment. The money is gone, permanently,
usually overseas, within an hour. Hundreds of families a year lose their entire down
payment this way.

*The rule:* before sending any wire, call the recipient at a phone number you looked up
*yourself* — not one from the email — and read the account details aloud to confirm. Every
time. No exceptions, even when it feels paranoid, especially when the email says it's
urgent. "Urgent" is the tell.
#+END_QUOTE

*** Zelle

A bank-to-bank instant transfer network built into most US banking apps. You send money
using a phone number or email address.

- *Speed:* minutes.
- *Cost:* free.
- *Reversible:* *no.* Zelle is treated like cash.
- *Used for:* paying a person — the babysitter, splitting a simcha gift, paying back a
  friend, tuition at some schools.

Because Zelle is instant and irreversible, it's a favorite of scammers. Never Zelle a
stranger. Never Zelle someone who called *you*. A bank will never call and ask you to Zelle
money to yourself "to protect it" — that is a script, and it is running right now, on
someone, somewhere.

*** Venmo, Cash App, PayPal

Similar to Zelle but they hold a balance inside the app. Fine for small peer payments.
Two cautions: money sitting in a Venmo balance is *not FDIC insured* the way a bank deposit
is (the protections are weaker and different), so sweep it out to your bank; and Venmo's
default social feed is public, which is a privacy setting worth changing on day one.

*** Paper checks

Still very much alive in the frum world — tuition, tzedakah, gemachs, many mosdos.

Mechanics: you write it, they deposit it, it clears in a few business days. Funds can be
withdrawn from your account days after you wrote the check, which is why "the balance looked
fine" is not the same as "the money was there."

- Never sign a blank check.
- Write the amount in words as well as digits; the words legally win in a dispute.
- Use a pen.
- A *voided check* (write VOID across it) is the standard way to give an employer your
  routing and account numbers for direct deposit.

*Check fraud is rising sharply*, mostly via mail theft — checks are stolen from mailboxes,
chemically washed, and rewritten. If you mail a check, hand it to a postal clerk or use a
blue box before the last pickup, not your home mailbox with the flag up.

*** Cashier's check and certified check

A check drawn on the *bank's* own funds after they've taken the money from your account. The
recipient knows it can't bounce. Required at most real estate closings and for many large
purchases. Costs $8–15 at your branch.

Note: cashier's checks are also heavily counterfeited in scams. If a stranger overpays you
with a cashier's check and asks you to refund the difference, it is a scam, one hundred
percent of the time.

** 2.6 Overdraft: the most expensive $5 you will ever spend

An *overdraft* happens when you spend more than the account holds.

Historically banks would "cover" the transaction and charge you an *overdraft fee* — around
$35 — per transaction. Buy coffee, a sefer, and gas on a Tuesday with $12 in the account and
you could rack up three fees: $105 of fees on about $40 of purchases. Some banks used to
reorder transactions largest-first to maximize the number of fees, which is exactly as bad
as it sounds and produced years of litigation.

Rules have improved and many banks have cut or eliminated these fees. Still:

*What to do, today:*

1. Ask your bank to *turn off overdraft coverage* on debit card purchases. Then the card is
   simply declined when there's no money — mildly embarrassing, dramatically cheaper.
2. Set up *overdraft protection linked to savings* — if checking runs dry, it pulls from your
   own savings, usually for free or a couple of dollars.
3. Set a *low-balance alert* by text at, say, $200.

*Non-sufficient funds (NSF) fee* is the cousin: the bank *declines* the payment and charges
you anyway. Same defense.

** 2.7 Fees you should never pay

| Fee                     | Typical | How to avoid it                                 |
|-------------------------+---------+-------------------------------------------------|
| Monthly maintenance     | $12     | Pick a free account or meet the waiver          |
| Overdraft / NSF         | $35     | Turn off coverage, link savings, set alerts     |
| Out-of-network ATM      | $3–6    | Use in-network; pick a bank that reimburses     |
| Paper statement         | $2–5    | Go paperless                                    |
| Minimum balance         | $10–25  | Pick an account without one                     |
| Foreign transaction     | 3%      | Use a card with no FX fee (matters for Israel)  |
| Inactivity              | varies  | Don't leave accounts dormant                    |
| Account closing         | varies  | Some banks charge if you close within 90 days   |

Add these up over a working life and it's thousands of dollars for nothing. It's also the
easiest money you will ever save, because it requires no discipline — just one afternoon of
setup.

*If you've been charged a fee, call and ask for it to be reversed.* Politely, once. Banks
reverse fees for customers in good standing far more often than people expect. The script is
"Hi, I was charged a $35 overdraft fee on the 14th. I've been a customer for two years and
this is the first time — would you be able to waive it?" Then stop talking.

** 2.8 Banking, security, and what to do about fraud

*** Basic hygiene

- A *unique* password on your bank, not reused anywhere. Use a password manager (Bitwarden
  is free, 1Password is good). This is not optional in 2026.
- Turn on *two-factor authentication* — a second code, ideally from an app like Authy or
  Google Authenticator rather than SMS. SMS 2FA is vulnerable to *SIM swapping*, where an
  attacker convinces your phone carrier to move your number to their device.
- Turn on transaction alerts. Every transaction over some amount texts you.
- Freeze your credit (§3.7). It's free, it takes fifteen minutes, and it's the single most
  effective anti-fraud step available to a consumer.

*** Debit card vs credit card fraud protection

This distinction is worth real money:

- *Credit card fraud:* the thief spent the bank's money. You dispute it, and while it's
  investigated you owe nothing. Federal law caps your liability at $50 and in practice
  every major issuer makes it $0.
- *Debit card fraud:* the thief spent *your* money — it is actually gone from your checking
  account while the investigation happens, which can take weeks. Your protection depends on
  how fast you report: report within 2 business days and you're capped at $50; within 60
  days, $500; after 60 days, potentially unlimited.

*Therefore: use a credit card for purchases, not a debit card.* The debit card should be for
ATM withdrawals and little else. This advice assumes you pay the credit card in full every
month — which is §3.3, and which is non-negotiable.

*** If you're defrauded

1. Call the bank immediately. The number on the back of the card, or from the bank's
   official site — never a number from an email or text.
2. Dispute the charges in writing as well as by phone.
3. File a report at =identitytheft.gov= (the FTC's site) — it generates a recovery plan and
   an official affidavit.
4. Freeze your credit at all three bureaus.
5. File a police report if identity theft is involved; some creditors require it.

** 2.9 A note on banking while frum

A few practical realities worth naming:

- *Shabbos and yom tov.* Automatic payments, autopay, and standing transfers keep running on
  Shabbos — which is fine and is not something you're doing. The relevant caution is
  scheduling: don't schedule something that requires *your* action on Shabbos, and know that
  a Friday-evening due date effectively means Thursday for you.
- *Erev yom tov cash.* Plan withdrawals before a three-day yom tov. Obvious until the year
  you forget.
- *Gemachs.* Interest-free loan funds run by the community. Enormously valuable, and
  discussed properly in §3.10, including the part where treating a gemach as a substitute
  for an emergency fund is a mistake.
- *Cash-heavy environments.* Many frum businesses and mosdos run heavily on checks and cash.
  Keep records anyway — for maaser accounting, for taxes, and for the simple reason that
  memory is not a filing system.

** 2.10 Where to park cash that isn't a savings account

A savings account is the right home for most of your cash. But there are three other places
worth knowing about, because for certain jobs they're better — and because nobody ever
mentions them to a young family.

*** Treasury bills

A *T-bill* is a short-term loan to the US government — 4, 8, 13, 17, 26, or 52 weeks. You
buy at a discount and receive the face value at maturity; the difference is your interest.

Why they can beat a savings account:

- *Backed by the full faith and credit of the US government.* The safest financial instrument
  in existence — safer than an FDIC-insured deposit, technically, since FDIC insurance is
  itself backed by the government.
- *Interest is exempt from state and local income tax.* If you live in New York City paying
  roughly 10% combined state and city tax, a 4.2% T-bill beats a 4.5% savings account after
  tax. This is a real and commonly missed edge for people in high-tax states.
- *No bank can cut the rate on you* mid-term the way a savings account can.

How to buy: either at =treasurydirect.gov= (free, direct from the government, slightly clunky
interface, and notoriously annoying to get money *out* of quickly) or through a brokerage
account at Fidelity/Schwab/Vanguard (easier, sellable before maturity, and my recommendation).

*** Money market funds

A *money market fund* is a mutual fund holding very short-term, very safe debt. Held at a
brokerage, not a bank.

- Rates typically track short-term interest rates closely, often beating bank savings.
- Same-day or next-day liquidity in practice.
- *Not FDIC insured.* Covered by SIPC against brokerage failure, not against loss. In
  practice, government money market funds are extremely safe — but "extremely safe" is not
  the same word as "insured," and you should know which one you're holding.
- *Treasury-only money market funds* get the same state-tax exemption as T-bills.

Fidelity's SPAXX, Vanguard's VMFXX, and Schwab's SNSXX are the common ones. If you open a
brokerage account, cash sitting in it can usually be swept into one of these automatically —
check, because some brokers default to a near-zero-interest cash account instead and
cheerfully keep the difference.

*** I bonds

*Series I Savings Bonds* are US government bonds whose interest rate has two parts: a fixed
rate set at purchase and held for the life of the bond, plus a variable rate that resets
every six months to track inflation.

- *They cannot lose value*, and they cannot pay less than 0%.
- *Interest is exempt from state and local tax*, and federal tax is deferred until you cash
  them in.
- *Limit:* $10,000 per person per calendar year, bought at =treasurydirect.gov=. A married
  couple can do $20,000 a year, and you can additionally buy in a child's name.
- *Lockup:* you cannot touch them for 12 months, and cashing in before 5 years costs you the
  last 3 months of interest.
- *Bonus:* interest can be entirely tax-free if used for qualified higher education expenses,
  subject to income limits.

*What they're actually for:* money you're certain you won't need for at least a year, that you
want protected against inflation with zero risk. A chunk of a long-horizon sinking fund. The
second tier of an emergency fund, behind the part you keep instantly available. They are not
a substitute for the first $5,000 of your emergency fund, because of the 12-month lockup.

*** How to choose

| Money for...                       | Put it in                        |
|------------------------------------+----------------------------------|
| This month's bills                 | Checking                         |
| Emergency fund, first tier         | High-yield savings               |
| Emergency fund, second tier        | HYSA, T-bills, or I bonds        |
| Sinking funds due within a year    | High-yield savings               |
| A known expense 1–3 years out      | T-bills or a CD matching the date|
| Inflation protection, 1+ years out | I bonds                          |
| Anything 5+ years out              | Invest it (Part 7)               |

*** A note if you're in a high-tax state

New York City, New York State, and New Jersey residents give up a meaningful slice of savings
interest to state and local tax. Treasury interest is exempt from that. On a $30,000
emergency fund, choosing Treasuries over a bank account can be worth a few hundred dollars a
year for identical safety. It is not life-changing money. It is free money, which is a
different and better category.

* PART 3 — CREDIT, CREDIT CARDS, AND DEBT

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* "Credit" means one thing: do strangers believe you pay people back? Three
companies keep a score on you between 300 and 850. Good score, cheap loans. Bad score,
expensive loans. On a house, that difference is well over $100,000.

You build the score by borrowing small amounts and paying them back on time, for years.
There is no shortcut, because *time* is the main ingredient. Which is exactly why you start
at 24 and not at 34.

A credit card is either a free 30-day loan or a catastrophe, with nothing in between. Pay
the whole bill every month and it costs you nothing, ever. Pay less than the whole bill and
you are borrowing at about 25% — worse than any investment you could make is good.

Do one thing today: freeze your credit at all three companies. It's free, it takes fifteen
minutes, and it stops anyone opening accounts in your name. Do it for your daughter too.
#+END_QUOTE

** 3.1 What credit is, and why a stranger's opinion of you has a dollar value

*Credit* means someone's willingness to lend you money based on their belief you'll pay it
back. Because the American financial system runs on that belief, it built an entire
industry to quantify it.

Practically, your creditworthiness determines:

- Whether you can get a mortgage, and at what rate. (Enormous. See below.)
- Whether you can get a car loan, and at what rate.
- Your car insurance premium in most states.
- Whether a landlord rents to you.
- Sometimes whether an employer hires you (varies by state and role).
- Whether you can get a cell phone plan without a deposit.

*The size of the stakes:* on a $400,000 30-year mortgage, the difference between excellent
credit and mediocre credit is often 1–1.5 percentage points of interest. Over thirty years
that's roughly $100,000–150,000. Your credit score is, quite literally, a six-figure asset,
and it is built by doing boring things consistently for years.

You are 24 and possibly have no credit history at all. That is not bad credit; it's *no*
credit, which is a different problem with an easier fix (§3.6). Start now — the single
biggest input is *time*, and time cannot be bought later.

** 3.2 Credit reports and credit scores

*** The credit report

A *credit report* is a file about your borrowing history, maintained by three private
companies called *credit bureaus*: *Equifax*, *Experian*, and *TransUnion*. Lenders report to
them voluntarily, so the three reports differ slightly.

The report contains: accounts you've opened, credit limits, balances, payment history
(on-time or late, and how late), accounts in collections, bankruptcies, and *inquiries*
(times someone checked your credit).

*You are entitled to free copies.* The official site is =annualcreditreport.com= — that is
the real one, mandated by federal law. Everything else advertising "free credit report" is
selling something. As of recent years you can pull all three weekly at no cost.

*Check them.* Roughly a fifth of consumer credit reports contain an error, and some of those
errors cost real money. Dispute errors in writing with the bureau; they have 30 days to
investigate.

*** The credit score

A *credit score* is a number, typically 300–850, computed from your credit report. The
dominant model is *FICO*; a competitor called *VantageScore* is what most free apps show you.

Rough bands:

| Score   | Rating      | What it means in practice          |
|---------+-------------+------------------------------------|
| 800–850 | Exceptional | Best rates available               |
| 740–799 | Very good   | Best rates, essentially            |
| 670–739 | Good        | Approved, slightly worse rates     |
| 580–669 | Fair        | Approved with worse terms          |
| 300–579 | Poor        | Denials, deposits, subprime rates  |

Above about 760 you've captured nearly all the benefit. Chasing 850 is a hobby, not a
strategy.

*** The five ingredients (FICO weights)

| Factor              | Weight | What it means                                      |
|---------------------+--------+----------------------------------------------------|
| Payment history     | 35%    | Do you pay on time? Late payments hurt badly       |
| Amounts owed        | 30%    | Utilization — how much of your limits you're using |
| Length of history   | 15%    | Average age of accounts. Time. Only time.          |
| Credit mix          | 10%    | Cards + installment loans looks better than one    |
| New credit          | 10%    | Recent applications and new accounts               |

Two of these deserve their own explanation.

*Payment history (35%).* One payment 30+ days late can drop a good score by 50–100 points
and stays on the report for *seven years*. This is the single most damaging routine mistake
in consumer finance. Autopay the minimum on every account — even if you plan to pay in full
manually — purely as insurance against the month you're up all night with a sick baby and
forget.

*Credit utilization (part of the 30%).* This is the percentage of your available credit
you're using. Two cards with $5,000 limits each and $1,500 of total balance is
$1,500/$10,000 = 15% utilization. Under 30% is fine; under 10% is ideal.

Crucially, utilization is measured on the balance the card *reports* to the bureaus,
typically on the statement closing date — not whether you eventually paid in full. So you
can pay every bill in full, never owe a cent of interest, and still show high utilization if
you charge a lot right before the statement closes. If you're about to apply for a mortgage,
pay the card down *before* the statement date for a couple of months.

*** What does NOT affect your score

- Your income. (Lenders see it; the score doesn't use it.)
- Your bank account balances.
- Your assets or net worth.
- Checking your own score. That's a *soft inquiry* and is harmless. Check it monthly if you
  like.
- Debit card use. It builds nothing.
- Paying rent, usually — unless you use a service that reports it.

*** Hard vs soft inquiries

- *Soft inquiry:* you checking your own score, or a pre-approval offer. No effect.
- *Hard inquiry:* you applied for credit. Small temporary ding (a few points), stays on the
  report two years, matters for about one.

Rate-shopping exception: multiple mortgage or auto-loan inquiries within a short window
(14–45 days depending on the model) count as *one* inquiry. So you can and should shop
several mortgage lenders. Do it inside a two-week window.

** 3.3 Credit cards: how they actually work

*** The mechanics, precisely

A credit card is a *revolving line of credit*. The issuer sets a *credit limit* — the maximum
you can owe at once. You buy things; the issuer pays the merchant; you now owe the issuer.

Each month you get a *statement* covering a *billing cycle* (about 30 days). The statement
shows a *statement balance* (what you owed at cycle close) and a *due date*, typically about
21–25 days later. That gap is the *grace period*.

Here is the entire game, in one sentence:

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*If you pay the full statement balance by the due date, every month, you pay zero interest —
ever. If you pay even one dollar less, interest is charged on the entire balance, often
retroactively to the purchase date, and the grace period disappears until you're back to
zero.*
#+END_QUOTE

That is why a credit card is either a free 30–55 day loan or a financial disaster, with
almost nothing in between. The card doesn't change. Your behavior does.

*** APR, and what carrying a balance actually costs

*APR* — Annual Percentage Rate. The yearly interest rate. Credit card APRs in 2026 commonly
run 20–29%.

What that means concretely. Carry $5,000 at 24% APR and pay only the minimum (usually about
2% of the balance, or ~$100):

- Payoff time: *over 20 years.*
- Total interest paid: *roughly $8,000–10,000*, more than the original purchase.

The minimum payment is not a suggestion of a reasonable pace. It is engineered to be the
slowest pace that keeps you current — because slow is how the issuer earns. Every statement
is legally required to print a box showing how long minimum payments would take. Read it
once, and let it be unpleasant.

*Compare:* the stock market returns roughly 7% real. Paying off a 24% credit card is a
*guaranteed, risk-free, tax-free 24% return*. There is no investment on earth that competes
with paying off a credit card. If you have card debt and are wondering whether to invest,
the answer is not close.

*** Other terms you'll meet

- *Cash advance* — withdrawing cash against the card. Higher APR, *no grace period* (interest
  starts immediately), plus a fee. Never do this.
- *Balance transfer* — moving debt from one card to another, often at 0% for 12–21 months
  with a 3–5% transfer fee. A legitimate tool for digging out, but only if you have a real
  payoff plan and don't re-run the old card.
- *Annual fee* — some cards charge $95–695/year for better rewards. Fine only if you compute
  that the rewards exceed the fee for *your actual spending*. Start with no-annual-fee cards.
- *Rewards* — cash back (simple, recommended) or points/miles (better ceiling, more effort).
  Typically 1–5%. Real money if you never carry a balance; a rounding error compared to
  interest if you do.
- *Introductory APR* — 0% for a period, then a high rate. Know the end date. Put it in your
  calendar the day you open the card.
- *Authorized user* — someone added to your account who gets a card. The account usually
  reports on *their* credit report too. See §3.6.

*** Rules for using a card safely

1. Pay the *full statement balance* every month. Autopay it in full. Not the minimum — full.
2. Never spend on the card what you couldn't pay in cash today.
3. Keep utilization under 30%, ideally under 10%.
4. Don't close old cards; the account age helps you (§3.4).
5. Don't chase rewards into spending you wouldn't otherwise do. A 2% reward on a purchase
   you didn't need is a 98% loss.
6. Check your statement monthly for fraud.

** 3.4 Should you close old credit cards? (and other score mechanics)

*Usually no.* Closing a card:

- Removes its credit limit from your total available credit, which *raises* your utilization
  percentage overnight.
- Eventually removes its age from your average account age.

If a card has no annual fee, keep it open, put one small recurring charge on it (a $5
subscription), autopay it in full, and forget it exists. That keeps it active — issuers do
close dormant cards — at zero cost and zero effort.

If a card has an annual fee that no longer earns its keep, call and ask to *product change*
(downgrade) it to a no-fee card from the same issuer. That preserves the account and its
age instead of closing it.

** 3.5 Debit cards, prepaid cards, and "buy now, pay later"

*Debit card:* spends money you have, directly from checking. Builds no credit. Weaker fraud
protection (§2.8). Fine for ATMs. Not your primary spending tool if you can handle a credit
card responsibly — and if you *can't*, then it absolutely is, and there's no shame in that
being the right call for a season.

*Prepaid card:* you load money on. No credit building, often fees. Occasionally useful for
budgeting a specific purpose; mostly a worse version of a checking account.

*Buy Now Pay Later (Klarna, Affirm, Afterpay):* split a purchase into four payments. Often
0% interest, which is how they get you. The dangers are real: it fragments your obligations
across apps so you lose track, some report to credit bureaus (late payments hurt), and the
whole design exists to make a purchase feel smaller than it is. If you need to split a $200
purchase into four payments, the honest read is that you cannot afford the $200 purchase
this month.

** 3.6 Building credit from absolutely nothing

You're 24, possibly with a thin or empty file. Here's the ladder, in order.

*** Step 1 — Become an authorized user

Ask a parent or in-law with old, well-managed cards to add you as an *authorized user*. The
card's full history — including the years before you were added — often lands on your report,
which can create an established credit age out of thin air.

You don't even need the physical card. Confirm with the issuer that they report authorized
users to the bureaus (most major issuers do). Only do this with someone whose credit habits
are genuinely good, since their behavior now affects your file.

*** Step 2 — A secured credit card

A *secured card* requires a cash deposit (say $300) which becomes your credit limit. You use
it, pay it in full, and it reports to the bureaus like any card. After 6–12 months of good
behavior the issuer typically refunds the deposit and converts it to a regular card.

Discover it Secured and Capital One's secured card are the usual recommendations — both
report to all three bureaus and both have a defined graduation path. Avoid any secured card
with an application fee or a monthly fee; those exist and they prey on exactly this moment.

*** Step 3 — A student or starter card

If you're enrolled in school (you're studying CS — this may apply), student cards have
lower approval bars. Discover, Capital One, and Bank of America all have them. No annual
fee, modest limits, straightforward.

*** Step 4 — Credit-builder loan

A *credit-builder loan* is a peculiar and clever product, mostly from credit unions: the bank
"lends" you, say, $1,000 but holds it in a locked savings account. You make monthly payments
for a year; the payments are reported to the bureaus; at the end you receive the $1,000. You
have effectively been forced to save while manufacturing a payment history. Self and various
credit unions offer these.

*** Step 5 — Time, and nothing but time

After the above, the recipe is: 1–3 cards, always paid in full, low utilization, never
closed, for years. There is no shortcut and anyone selling one is selling a scam (§17.2).
In about two years you should be over 700; in about five, over 760, at which point you have
extracted essentially all the available value.

*** What about "credit repair" companies?

Skip them. They charge for things you can do free: disputing errors and waiting. Accurate
negative information cannot be removed by anyone, regardless of what the ad says. If you're
in genuine distress, a *nonprofit credit counselor* (look for NFCC membership) is the real
version and is usually free or nearly so.

** 3.7 Freeze your credit. Today. It takes fifteen minutes.

A *credit freeze* (also "security freeze") blocks new lenders from pulling your credit report,
which means nobody can open an account in your name — because opening an account requires
pulling the report.

- It is *free*, by federal law.
- It does not affect your score.
- It does not affect existing accounts.
- You can *thaw* it temporarily (an hour, a day, a week) when you actually apply for
  something, then it refreezes.

You must do it at all three bureaus separately:

- Equifax: =equifax.com/personal/credit-report-services/credit-freeze/=
- Experian: =experian.com/freeze/=
- TransUnion: =transunion.com/credit-freeze=

*Do this for your children too.* Child identity theft is common precisely because nobody
checks a four-year-old's credit report for fourteen years. You can freeze a minor's credit
by mail with documentation. Do it for your daughter, and for each subsequent child. It's a
minor errand that forecloses a genuinely ugly problem.

** 3.8 Debt: which kinds, and how bad each one is

Not all debt is equal, and the moralizing on this topic ("all debt is bondage") is less
useful than the arithmetic.

*** A rough hierarchy

| Debt type              | Typical rate | Verdict                                       |
|------------------------+--------------+-----------------------------------------------|
| Mortgage               | 6–7%         | Usually fine. Buys an appreciating asset      |
| Federal student loan   | 5–8%         | Depends entirely on what it bought            |
| Car loan               | 5–10%        | Tolerable if modest; the car is depreciating  |
| Personal loan          | 8–20%        | Situational, usually a symptom                |
| Credit card            | 20–29%       | Emergency. Attack immediately                 |
| Payday loan            | 200–400%     | Never. Under any circumstances. Ever.         |
| Gemach loan            | 0%           | Different category entirely — see §3.10       |

*** The real test

Debt is defensible when *all three* hold:

1. It buys something that lasts longer than the loan (a house, an education that raises
   income, a car you'll drive for a decade).
2. The interest rate is low enough that the thing it bought outgrows the cost.
3. The payment fits comfortably in your cash flow with room for the bad month.

Debt is a problem when it funds consumption that's over before the payments are — a
vacation, a simcha beyond your means, a Pesach program on a credit card. That debt makes
next year's version of the same problem harder, which is the mechanism by which people
spiral.

*** The number that decides everything: interest rate

Simple decision rule for extra dollars:

- Above ~8%: pay it off before investing. A guaranteed 20% beats a hoped-for 7%, always.
- Around 5–8%: judgment call. Reasonable people differ. Splitting is fine.
- Below ~5%: mathematically, invest instead. Emotionally, some people still want it gone,
  and there is nothing wrong with buying peace of mind at a modest price. Just know you're
  buying peace of mind rather than maximizing dollars, and be honest that the trade is real.

Exception that overrides all of the above: *always* capture an employer 401(k) match first
(§8.3). A 50–100% instant return beats even credit card payoff.

** 3.9 Getting out of debt: two methods

*** The avalanche (mathematically optimal)

1. List all debts with their interest rates.
2. Pay minimums on everything.
3. Throw every extra dollar at the *highest interest rate*.
4. When it's gone, roll that entire payment into the next-highest.

Saves the most money. Objectively correct.

*** The snowball (psychologically optimal)

Identical, except step 3 targets the *smallest balance* regardless of rate.

Costs slightly more in interest. Wins more often in practice, because paying off an entire
debt in six weeks produces a feeling of progress that keeps people going, and a plan you
finish beats a plan you abandon.

*** Which to use

If the rate differences are large (a 27% card and a 5% car loan), use avalanche — the money
gap is too big to ignore. If the rates are similar and you've quit before, use snowball. If
you're not sure, use snowball; the research on behavior change is fairly kind to it.

*** A worked example

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
Debts:
  Card A:  $1,200 at 26%  minimum $35
  Card B:  $4,000 at 22%  minimum $95
  Car:     $8,000 at 6%   payment $260

Extra available each month: $400

AVALANCHE: minimums on B and car, $435/mo to Card A.
  Card A gone in ~3 months. Then $470/mo to Card B — gone in ~9 more.
  Then $825/mo at the car.  All clear in ~22 months.

SNOWBALL: same order here, because Card A is both smallest and highest.
  (When they diverge, snowball takes the small one first.)
#+END_EXAMPLE

Notice the acceleration: each payoff frees its payment, and the next attack is bigger. This
is why debt payoff feels impossible for six months and then suddenly fast.

** 3.10 Gemachs, family loans, and ribbis

*** What a gemach is

A *gemach* (from גמילות חסדים) is a community fund making interest-free loans. Most frum
communities have many: general cash gemachs, medical gemachs, chasunah gemachs, and gemachs
that lend objects rather than money — baby equipment, medical devices, tables and chairs,
wedding gowns.

Interest-free loans are a genuine mitzvah and a real communal asset. Use them, and pay them
back on time, and when you're able, fund them.

*** Where people go wrong with gemachs

Three failure modes worth naming plainly:

1. *Treating a gemach as an emergency fund.* It isn't one. It's borrowed money that must be
   repaid, and the repayment lands on a future month that has its own problems. Zero
   interest makes it cheaper, not free.
2. *Stacking gemach loans.* Because each is individually interest-free and socially easy,
   people accumulate several, and the combined monthly repayment quietly becomes a large
   fixed obligation invisible to any budget or lender.
3. *Not writing it down.* Interest-free does not mean informal. Both sides should have a
   written record of amount, date, and repayment schedule. This protects the relationship,
   which is worth more than the money.

*** Family loans

Money between family members is the most common form of frum-community credit and the most
common source of frum-community resentment. If you borrow from or lend to family:

- Write it down. Amount, date, repayment terms. One page. Both sign.
- Be explicit about whether it is a *loan* or a *gift*. Ambiguity here has ended
  relationships that money never should have touched.
- If it is a gift, note that US gift tax rules let anyone give any person a substantial
  amount per year (see Appendix B) with no tax and no filing. Above that, the *giver* files a
  form; almost nobody actually owes tax because of a very large lifetime exemption. Your
  parents can give you money. This is not a problem.
- Understand the effect on any means-tested benefits you receive (Part 5). A recurring gift
  can be counted as income in some programs. Ask before assuming.

*** Ribbis, in brief and non-authoritatively

*Ribbis* is the Torah prohibition on charging or paying interest between Jews. It's a real
sugya with real practical consequences, and I'm going to be careful here because this is a
halachic question and I am not a posek.

What you should know at a practical level:

- It applies to loans between Jews, in both directions — paying is a problem, not only
  charging.
- The standard mechanism for permitting interest-bearing business arrangements is a *heter
  iska*, a document restructuring the loan as a joint venture where the "interest" is a
  profit share.
- Many Jewish-owned banks, lenders, and mosdos operate with a heter iska on file; some
  post it publicly.
- Ordinary deposits and loans with non-Jewish-owned banks are generally not a ribbis issue.
- Situations that genuinely need a rav: a private loan to or from another Jew where you want
  to charge interest; a mortgage from a Jewish-owned lender; a business partnership with a
  profit split; some investment structures.
