*The practical instruction:* when a transaction involves interest and another Jew, ask a rav
before signing, not after. It's a five-minute question and the answer is usually "get a
heter iska, here's the standard one."

** 3.11 Student loans (a short chapter, because you may need it)

You're studying computer science. Whether that's self-taught, a bootcamp, a community
college, or a degree, here's the landscape.

*** Federal vs private

*Federal loans* come from the government. They have fixed rates, income-driven repayment
plans, forbearance and deferment options, forgiveness programs, and they die with the
borrower. Apply via the *FAFSA* (Free Application for Federal Student Aid) at
=studentaid.gov=, which is free — anything charging you to file a FAFSA is a scam.

*Private loans* come from banks. Usually higher rates, fewer protections, often require a
cosigner, and generally not dischargeable. Exhaust federal options first, always.

*** The single most important rule

*Do not borrow more, in total, than you expect to earn in your first year of work.* A CS
degree that costs $40,000 total and leads to a $95,000 job is a fine trade. A $180,000
degree leading to the same job is a serious problem you'll carry into your thirties.

*** Cheaper paths worth taking seriously in your situation

- Community college for the first two years, then transfer. Same degree, dramatically less
  money.
- Public in-state university over private.
- Employer tuition reimbursement — many companies pay for school; some will pay while you
  work as a junior developer.
- Self-study plus a portfolio. In software specifically, this is a real path — less
  reliable than a degree for getting the first interview, but genuinely viable, and the
  cheapest option by an order of magnitude. See Part 10.
- Look at Touro, and at frum-oriented programs generally, which often accept yeshiva
  credits and are structured around a kollel schedule. Compare their cost honestly against
  the alternatives rather than assuming convenience is free.

*** Income-driven repayment and forgiveness, briefly

Federal loans offer plans capping payments at a percentage of *discretionary income*, with
remaining balance forgiven after 20–25 years. *Public Service Loan Forgiveness* (PSLF)
forgives after 10 years of payments while working for a government or 501(c)(3) nonprofit —
and many yeshivos, day schools and mosdos are 501(c)(3)s, which makes this quietly relevant
to a lot of frum professionals.

These programs have been repeatedly rewritten by Congress and by successive administrations,
including significant changes in 2024–2025. Do not plan a decade around the current rules
without verifying them at =studentaid.gov= at the time you need them.
* PART 4 — TAXES: THE COMPLETE PICTURE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* A tax return looks frightening and is really one long subtraction problem.
Start with everything you earned. Subtract a few things. Apply the rates. Subtract credits.
Subtract what was already taken out of the paychecks. What's left is what you owe them, or
what they owe you.

Two facts here are worth more to your family than everything else in this document.

*One: earning more can never leave you with less, because of tax brackets.* When someone says
"the raise pushed me into a higher bracket," they are simply wrong. Only the extra dollars
get the higher rate. The rest of your income is untouched.

*Two: some credits pay you cash even when you owe zero tax.* A married couple filing jointly
with several children and a modest income can receive thousands of dollars a year this way.
It is deliberate policy, not a loophole, and you should claim it without a moment of
discomfort.

But you only get it if you *file a return*. Most people who miss out miss out because they
assumed they didn't need to file. File. Every year. Even when you think there's nothing in
it for you.
#+END_QUOTE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
This is the longest part of the course, and for a family in your situation it is probably
the most financially valuable. A young frum family with modest income and several children
frequently qualifies for *thousands of dollars a year* in refundable tax credits — money the
government pays you, not merely a discount on tax owed. People miss it constantly, because
nobody explained the machine to them. Here is the machine.
#+END_QUOTE

** 4.1 The mental model: how a tax return actually works

An American tax return is a single arithmetic pipeline. Every form, every worksheet, every
piece of jargon slots into one of these six steps. Learn the pipeline and the forms stop
being frightening.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
   (1)  GROSS INCOME
        everything you earned: wages, interest, self-employment, etc.
          |
          |  minus "above-the-line" deductions (a.k.a. adjustments)
          v
   (2)  ADJUSTED GROSS INCOME  (AGI)
        the single most important number on the return
          |
          |  minus the standard deduction OR itemized deductions
          v
   (3)  TAXABLE INCOME
          |
          |  apply the tax brackets
          v
   (4)  TAX OWED (before credits)
          |
          |  minus tax credits
          v
   (5)  FINAL TAX
          |
          |  minus what was already withheld or paid
          v
   (6)  REFUND  (if you overpaid)   or   BALANCE DUE  (if you underpaid)
#+END_EXAMPLE

*The plain-English version of that diagram:*

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  1. Add up everything you made.
  2. Subtract a few special things  →  this number is called your AGI, and it's the
                                       number the government uses to decide what you
                                       qualify for. Remember the letters.
  3. Subtract a standard chunk (about $32,000 for a married couple).
  4. Whatever's left, apply the tax rates to it. That's the tax.
  5. Subtract "credits" — these come straight off the tax, dollar for dollar.
  6. Subtract what was already taken out of your paychecks all year.
  7. Positive? You owe. Negative? They owe you.
#+END_EXAMPLE

Read that four times. Everything below is detail hanging off those six boxes.

Two vocabulary items to nail down immediately, because confusing them costs people money:

- A *deduction* reduces the income you're taxed on. A $1,000 deduction in the 12% bracket
  saves you $120.
- A *credit* reduces the tax itself, dollar for dollar. A $1,000 credit saves you $1,000.

*Credits are worth far more than deductions.* And a *refundable* credit is worth even more —
it can pay you money beyond your tax bill, i.e. the government sends you a check. That's
§4.9, and it is where the real money is for you.

** 4.2 Marginal vs effective tax rate — the most misunderstood idea in America

*** The brackets are not what people think

The US has a *progressive* income tax with brackets. Here is what nearly everyone gets
wrong: *moving into a higher bracket does not tax all your income at the higher rate.* Only
the dollars inside that bracket are taxed at that rate.

You will hear people say "I turned down a raise because it would push me into a higher
bracket." That is, with respect to the brackets themselves, arithmetically impossible. (It
*is* possible to lose money from a raise for a completely different reason — benefit cliffs.
That's Part 5, it's real, and it's important. But it is not about brackets.)

*** Worked example

Married filing jointly. Suppose the brackets are roughly: 10% on the first $24,000, 12% up
to $100,000, 22% above that (approximations — Appendix B has the real ones).

Taxable income of $110,000:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  First  $24,000  taxed at 10%  =  $2,400
  Next   $76,000  taxed at 12%  =  $9,120     ($24,000 -> $100,000)
  Last   $10,000  taxed at 22%  =  $2,200     ($100,000 -> $110,000)
                                   -------
  TOTAL TAX                     =  $13,720
#+END_EXAMPLE

- Your *marginal rate* is 22% — the rate on your next dollar earned.
- Your *effective rate* is $13,720 / $110,000 = *12.5%* — what you actually paid overall.

Notice how far apart those are. When someone says "I'm in the 22% bracket," they are
describing their last dollar, not their burden.

*** Why the distinction matters in real decisions

- *Marginal rate* is the number for decisions about *more*: should I take the overtime,
  should I do the side job, should I contribute to a traditional or Roth account. It tells
  you what the next dollar is worth.
- *Effective rate* is the number for *budgeting*: how much of my income actually goes to
  federal tax.

Confusing them is how people talk themselves out of raises and into bad retirement choices.

** 4.3 The W-2, box by box

A *W-2* is the form your employer sends you (and the IRS) in January, reporting what they
paid you and what they withheld. Your wife will get one; you'll get one when you start
working. Here's every box that matters.

| Box | Name                          | What it means                                       |
|-----+-------------------------------+-----------------------------------------------------|
|   1 | Wages, tips, other comp       | Taxable wages for *federal income tax*. Note this is *after* traditional 401(k) and pretax health premiums are subtracted |
|   2 | Federal income tax withheld   | What was already sent to the IRS on your behalf     |
|   3 | Social Security wages         | Wages subject to SS tax. Capped at the annual wage base. *Not* reduced by 401(k) |
|   4 | Social Security tax withheld  | Should equal 6.2% of Box 3                          |
|   5 | Medicare wages                | Like Box 3 but uncapped                             |
|   6 | Medicare tax withheld         | Should equal 1.45% of Box 5                         |
|  12 | Codes                         | The interesting box. See below                      |
|  13 | Checkboxes                    | "Retirement plan" checked affects IRA deductibility |
|  14 | Other                         | Employer's junk drawer — state disability, union dues |
|  15–20 | State/local                | State wages and withholding                         |

*** Why Box 1 is smaller than Box 3, and why that's good

If Box 1 is less than Box 3, you contributed to a *traditional* (pretax) 401(k), or paid
health premiums pretax, or funded an FSA or HSA through payroll. Those reduce income tax but
generally not Social Security and Medicare tax.

This is a tell: if Box 1 equals Box 3 exactly, your wife is contributing *nothing* pretax to
retirement. Worth checking on the next W-2 you see.

*** Box 12 codes worth recognizing

| Code | Meaning                                                     |
|------+-------------------------------------------------------------|
| D    | Traditional 401(k) contributions                            |
| AA   | Roth 401(k) contributions                                   |
| E    | 403(b) contributions (common at nonprofits, schools, mosdos)|
| BB   | Roth 403(b)                                                 |
| W    | HSA contributions (yours plus employer's)                   |
| DD   | Cost of employer health coverage — informational only, not taxable |
| C    | Taxable cost of group life insurance over $50,000           |

Code DD frightens people every year: it's often $15,000–25,000 and looks like income. It is
not taxed. It's disclosure, showing what your health coverage actually costs. Ignore it,
except as a reminder of what a benefits package is worth (§10.5).

** 4.4 The W-4: telling your employer how much to withhold

*Withholding* is your employer sending money to the IRS out of each paycheck as an estimate
of what you'll owe. The *W-4* is the form where you shape that estimate.

The form was redesigned in 2020 and no longer uses "allowances." It now asks directly about
your situation:

- *Step 1:* name, filing status (Single / Married Filing Jointly / Head of Household).
- *Step 2:* multiple jobs or a working spouse. *This is the step people skip and it's the one
  that causes surprise tax bills.* If both spouses work, each employer withholds as if their
  job is the only income, and you end up under-withheld.
- *Step 3:* dependents. This is where you claim the Child Tax Credit *in advance*, which
  lowers withholding — real cash flow relief during the year.
- *Step 4:* other income, extra deductions, or an extra flat amount per paycheck.

*Use the official IRS Tax Withholding Estimator* at =irs.gov/individuals/tax-withholding-estimator=.
It takes fifteen minutes with a recent pay stub and it's genuinely good. Run it in January,
and again after any major change: a new job, a raise, a baby, a move.

*** The refund question

A big refund feels wonderful. Mechanically, it means you lent the government money all year
at 0% interest and they gave it back.

The textbook answer: aim for a small refund or a small balance due, and keep the money
during the year.

The honest answer: for many families, the forced-savings aspect of a refund is genuinely
valuable, and the lump sum in February is what makes Pesach work. If that's you, fine —
just do it *on purpose*. Better still: set the withholding correctly and route the
difference into a sinking fund automatically (§1.7). Same lump sum, and you earn the
interest instead of Washington.

*** The under-withholding trap for two-earner households

If you both work and neither W-4 reflects the other's income, you can owe several thousand
dollars in April plus an *underpayment penalty*. The IRS generally doesn't penalize you if
you paid at least 90% of this year's tax, or 100% of last year's (110% at higher incomes) —
this is the *safe harbor*. Aim to clear it.

** 4.5 FICA: Social Security and Medicare

*FICA* — Federal Insurance Contributions Act. The payroll tax funding Social Security and
Medicare. It is separate from income tax and it is not reduced by the standard deduction, by
your children, or by traditional 401(k) contributions.

| Tax             | Your share | Employer's share | Total | Wage cap        |
|-----------------+------------+------------------+-------+-----------------|
| Social Security | 6.2%       | 6.2%             | 12.4% | Yes (Appendix B)|
| Medicare        | 1.45%      | 1.45%            | 2.9%  | No cap          |
| Additional Medicare | 0.9%   | none             | 0.9%  | Above $250K MFJ |

So a typical W-2 employee pays 7.65% off the top, and the employer pays another 7.65% you
never see. Economists will tell you the employer's half is really coming out of your wages
too, and they're mostly right.

*** Why this matters for someone in kollel

FICA is not merely a tax. It's how you *earn* Social Security and Medicare eligibility.

You need *40 credits* to qualify for Social Security retirement benefits. You earn up to 4
credits a year, each requiring a modest amount of covered earnings (see Appendix B —
roughly $1,800 per credit in recent years). So 40 credits is *ten years* of even quite small
earnings.

If you are in kollel with no reported earnings, you are earning *zero* credits in those
years. Three consequences:

1. Your eventual Social Security retirement benefit is based on your highest 35 years of
   earnings, with *zeros* filled in for missing years. Kollel years become zeros, dragging
   the average down.
2. *Social Security Disability Insurance (SSDI)* requires recent work credits. Without them
   you are not covered if you become disabled — see §6.5, because this makes disability
   insurance more urgent, not less.
3. *Survivor benefits* for your wife and children if you die also depend on your credits.

This isn't an argument against kollel. It's an argument for (a) knowing it, (b) making sure
*someone* in the household is building credits — if your wife works on the books, she is,
and her record covers the family for survivor and disability purposes to a meaningful extent
— and (c) reporting legitimate income when you have it rather than taking everything in
cash. A stipend that gets reported builds credits. One that doesn't, doesn't.

*Check your record:* create an account at =ssa.gov/myaccount=. It shows your entire reported
earnings history and projected benefits. Do it once a year; errors happen and are much
easier to fix within about three years.

** 4.6 Filing status: which box you check

| Status                          | Who it's for                                  |
|---------------------------------+-----------------------------------------------|
| Single                          | Unmarried                                     |
| Married Filing Jointly (MFJ)    | Married; one return for both. Almost always best |
| Married Filing Separately (MFS) | Married; two returns. Usually worse           |
| Head of Household (HoH)         | Unmarried with a dependent; better than Single |
| Qualifying Surviving Spouse     | Widow(er) with a child, for two years         |

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*You file Married Filing Jointly.* That is the correct choice and it is what every number in
this document assumes. Concretely, filing jointly means:

- *One return* covering both of you. Both incomes, one form, one refund.
- *The standard deduction is roughly double* the single amount (Appendix B), and the tax
  brackets are roughly twice as wide — so a household earning $80,000 split between two
  people is taxed the same as one person earning $80,000, not worse.
- *You have access to every credit that matters:* the Earned Income Credit, the education
  credits, the Saver's Credit, the student loan interest deduction. Married Filing
  Separately blocks or cripples all of these.
- *Your wife's earned income qualifies you to fund an IRA* even with no earnings of your own.
  That is the spousal IRA (§8.6), and it exists *only* because you file jointly. It is
  probably the single most valuable consequence of MFJ for your household.
- *You are jointly and severally liable* for the return — meaning each of you is legally
  responsible for the whole thing, including any error or understatement. This is the one
  real downside and it essentially never matters for an honest return. (There is relief for
  the rare bad case: "innocent spouse relief," Form 8857.)
- *Every dollar figure you look up must be the MFJ one.* Bracket tables, income phase-outs,
  Roth limits, capital gains thresholds — all published in separate columns by filing status,
  and grabbing the "single" column is a classic and expensive mistake. Appendix B gives MFJ
  figures throughout, and says so.
#+END_QUOTE

So: bigger standard deduction, wider brackets, access to credits MFS blocks, and the spousal
IRA. File jointly.

*** When Married Filing Separately might actually make sense

Rare, but real:

- One spouse has large medical expenses and a low income (medical deduction has an
  AGI-percentage floor, so a lower AGI clears it more easily).
- Student loans on an income-driven plan where payments are computed on your income alone.
- You have genuine reason not to be jointly liable for a spouse's return.

MFS blocks or limits the Earned Income Credit, education credits, the student loan interest
deduction, and more. Run the numbers both ways in software before choosing it — every tax
program will do this comparison for you in a few clicks.

*** Note on marriage timing

Marital status for the entire tax year is determined by your status on *December 31*.
Married on December 29 means you file as married for that whole year. This occasionally
matters for planning; mostly it's trivia, but it's the kind of trivia that surprises people.

** 4.7 Adjusted Gross Income (AGI) and why it rules everything

*AGI* is gross income minus "above-the-line" deductions. It is the gatekeeper number:
eligibility for a startling number of credits, deductions, and benefits is defined by AGI or
by *MAGI* (Modified AGI — AGI with certain items added back, defined slightly differently
for each program, which is exactly as annoying as it sounds).

*** Above-the-line deductions (available even if you take the standard deduction)

These are the good ones, because you get them *and* the standard deduction:

- Traditional IRA contributions (subject to income limits — §8.5)
- HSA contributions (§8.9)
- Self-employment tax deduction (half of SE tax — §4.11)
- Self-employed health insurance premiums
- Self-employed retirement plan contributions (SEP-IRA, Solo 401(k))
- Student loan interest, up to $2,500 (income-limited)
- Educator expenses (up to a few hundred dollars, for teachers — relevant if your wife
  teaches, which in the frum world is statistically likely)

*Lowering AGI is a lever.* If you're near a cliff for a credit or a benefit program, an extra
traditional IRA or HSA contribution can pull you back under, and sometimes that single move
is worth more than the contribution itself.

** 4.8 Standard deduction vs itemizing

After AGI, you subtract *either* the standard deduction *or* your itemized deductions,
whichever is larger. You don't get both.

*** The standard deduction

A flat amount based on filing status. For MFJ it is currently in the low $30,000s (Appendix
B). No receipts, no proof, no work.

Since the 2017 tax law roughly doubled it, about 90% of Americans take the standard
deduction. That includes almost certainly you, for now.

*** Itemized deductions (Schedule A)

Worth adding up only if the total might exceed the standard deduction:

- *Medical expenses* above 7.5% of AGI. That floor is high, so this usually needs a serious
  medical year.
- *State and local taxes (SALT)* — state income tax plus property tax, subject to a federal
  cap. The cap has been changed by legislation; check Appendix B. This is the big one for
  people in New York and New Jersey.
- *Mortgage interest* on up to $750,000 of debt.
- *Charitable contributions* — tzedakah, if given to a US 501(c)(3) organization.
- *Casualty losses* in a federally declared disaster.

*** The tzedakah/maaser point, said plainly

Frum families often give 10% of income, which is a large number. This makes itemizing more
plausible for you than for the average American — especially once you own a home and have
mortgage interest and property tax as well.

But two conditions:

1. *The recipient must be a US-registered 501(c)(3).* Most American yeshivos, shuls, and
   established tzedakah organizations are. Cash to an individual meshulach is *not*
   deductible, however genuine the need and however great the mitzvah. Neither is money to
   an Israeli institution unless it has a US 501(c)(3) "American Friends of" arm — which
   many do, precisely for this reason. Ask; the good ones will hand you the EIN without
   blinking.
2. *You need documentation.* Any gift of $250 or more requires a written acknowledgment from
   the organization *before you file*. Under $250, a bank record or receipt suffices. Keep
   them in one folder, physical or digital, all year. The receipts arriving in January are
   for the year that just ended — file them immediately, don't let them drift.

*A tactic worth knowing — "bunching."* If your itemized total hovers just below the standard
deduction, you can give two years of tzedakah in one calendar year (December and January of
the same tax year), itemize in that year, and take the standard deduction in the next. Over
two years you deduct more than you would have by spreading it evenly. A *donor-advised fund*
(§12.7) makes this smooth, letting you fund it in one year and distribute over several.

** 4.9 Tax credits — where the real money is for a family like yours

This section may be worth more to you than the rest of the document combined. Read slowly.

*** Refundable vs nonrefundable

- *Nonrefundable* credit: reduces tax to zero, and stops. Owe $800, have a $2,000
  nonrefundable credit → you owe $0, and the extra $1,200 evaporates.
- *Refundable* credit: reduces tax below zero and the IRS pays you the difference. Owe $800,
  have a $2,000 refundable credit → *you receive $1,200*.

Refundable credits are how a low-income family with children can have a negative federal
income tax — receiving thousands of dollars rather than paying. This is intentional policy,
it is not a loophole, and you should claim every dollar you're entitled to without an ounce
of discomfort.

*** Child Tax Credit (CTC)

- Roughly $2,200 per qualifying child under 17 (Appendix B; raised by 2025 legislation).
- Partially refundable — the refundable piece is the *Additional Child Tax Credit* (ACTC),
  currently around $1,700 per child.
- Phases out at high incomes (starting around $400,000 MFJ), so irrelevant to you for years.
- *The child needs a Social Security Number* valid for employment. Get one at birth — the
  hospital paperwork offers this, and doing it then is far easier than later.

For a family with, say, six children, this is roughly $13,200 of credit. That is not a
rounding error; that is a significant fraction of a modest household's annual budget.

*** Earned Income Tax Credit (EITC)

The most valuable and most under-claimed credit in the US system. Fully refundable.

Designed to supplement earnings for lower- and moderate-income working families. The amount
depends on earned income and number of children, and it is *large* — well over $7,000 for a
family with three or more children at the right income level (Appendix B).

Key mechanics:

- Requires *earned income* — wages or self-employment. Investment income above a modest
  threshold disqualifies you entirely.
- The credit *rises* with income up to a plateau, then *falls* as income increases. So
  there's a range where an extra dollar earned reduces the credit — an implicit marginal tax
  (see Part 5).
- Married Filing Separately generally disqualifies you.
- Roughly one in five eligible families never claims it, usually because they didn't file at
  all, believing they had no filing requirement.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*File a tax return even if you think you don't have to.* If your income is below the filing
threshold you may still be owed thousands in refundable credits — and the *only* way to get
them is to file. Every year, billions of dollars go unclaimed by people who assumed the
system had nothing for them. You cannot receive a refund you never asked for.
#+END_QUOTE

*** Child and Dependent Care Credit

For childcare expenses that let you work or look for work. Covers daycare, babysitting,
after-school care, day camp (not overnight camp) for children under 13.

- Percentage of expenses, up to a limit per child, with the percentage decreasing as income
  rises.
- Mostly nonrefundable, so its value depends on having some tax liability.
- *You need the provider's name, address, and taxpayer ID.* This is the sticking point in the
  frum world, where childcare is often an unregistered arrangement with a neighbor. If your
  provider won't give an SSN or EIN, you generally cannot claim the credit. Ask before you
  commit to a provider if this credit matters to your numbers.

A related option: a *Dependent Care FSA* through an employer, letting you pay childcare with
pretax dollars (up to a limit). You generally can't use both for the same dollars. Compare
which is better for your bracket — the FSA usually wins at higher incomes, the credit at
lower ones.

*** Education credits

- *American Opportunity Tax Credit (AOTC)* — up to $2,500 per student per year for the first
  four years of post-secondary education. 40% refundable (up to $1,000). This is the good
  one, and it is *directly relevant to you* studying computer science.
- *Lifetime Learning Credit (LLC)* — up to $2,000 per return, no year limit, covers part-time
  study and single courses. Nonrefundable. Useful for continuing education.

You cannot claim both for the same student in the same year. AOTC is usually better if you
qualify. You'll receive a *Form 1098-T* from the school reporting tuition paid.

*** Saver's Credit (Retirement Savings Contributions Credit)

A credit of 10–50% of retirement contributions (up to $2,000 per person contributed), for
lower-income taxpayers. Nonrefundable currently; legislation is scheduled to convert it into
a government matching contribution deposited into your retirement account ("Saver's Match")
later this decade — verify the current status when you use it.

*Why this is aimed squarely at you:* a household with a modest income that manages to put
$2,000 into an IRA can receive a 50% credit — $1,000 back. That is a guaranteed 50% return
on retirement savings, on top of the investment returns. It exists, most people who qualify
have never heard of it, and it evaporates as income rises.

*** Premium Tax Credit

Subsidizes health insurance bought on the ACA marketplace (§6.3). Refundable, and can be
taken in advance as a monthly reduction in your premium. Based on income relative to the
federal poverty level.

*Important:* it's reconciled at tax time. If you earned more than you estimated, you may have
to pay some back. Update the marketplace when your income changes rather than discovering it
in April.

*** Adoption credit, and others

There are dozens more, most narrow. Good tax software walks you through them. This is a
strong argument for using software rather than filling out forms by hand.

** 4.10 State and local taxes

Federal is only part of the picture, and for the tri-state frum world, often not the
expensive part.

*** The landscape

- *No state income tax:* Florida, Texas, Nevada, Tennessee, Washington, Wyoming, South
  Dakota, Alaska, New Hampshire (no wage tax).
- *Moderate:* most states, roughly 3–6%.
- *High:* California, New York, New Jersey, Massachusetts, Hawaii, Oregon.

*** New York specifics

- State income tax roughly 4–10.9%, graduated.
- *New York City* adds its own income tax, roughly 3–3.9%, if you *live* in the city. Working
  in the city while living outside it does not trigger it; residence does.
- Yonkers has a surcharge.
- So a Brooklyn resident pays federal + NY State + NYC. A Monsey resident pays federal + NY
  State but no city tax. A Lakewood resident pays federal + NJ.

*** New Jersey specifics

- Graduated income tax, roughly 1.4–10.75%.
- *Very high property taxes* — among the highest in the nation. Lakewood, Passaic, and Teaneck
  homeowners feel this acutely, and it is a large recurring cost that renters pay indirectly
  through rent.
- NJ does *not* tax contributions to a 401(k)... but it *does* tax 403(b) contributions
  (state-level quirk that surprises people who work for nonprofits). NJ also taxes some
  things federal doesn't, and vice versa. This is exactly the kind of thing tax software
  handles and a person filling out paper forms gets wrong.

*** Reciprocity and multi-state

If you live in one state and work in another, you may need to file in both, usually with a
credit in your home state for taxes paid to the work state. NJ and PA have a reciprocity
agreement; NY and NJ do not, so a NJ resident working in NYC files both. Tax software
handles this; it's a common enough situation that it's well-trodden.

*** Property tax, in brief

If you buy a home, the town taxes it annually based on assessed value. In parts of NJ this
runs 2–2.5% of value per year — on a $500,000 house, $10,000–12,500 *every year, forever*.
This dwarfs what most first-time buyers expect and it must be in the buying calculation
(Part 9).

You can *appeal* an assessment you believe is too high. Many people never do. It's a real
process with real success rates.

** 4.11 Self-employment and 1099 income

Even in kollel, side income is common — tutoring, a chavrusa stipend, freelance programming
once you're studying CS, selling something, a summer job at a camp. Here's what changes.

*** W-2 vs 1099 — the fundamental difference

| Aspect            | W-2 employee                  | 1099 contractor                     |
|-------------------+-------------------------------+-------------------------------------|
| Tax withheld?     | Yes, automatically            | No. Nothing. It's all on you        |
| FICA              | You pay 7.65%, employer 7.65% | You pay all 15.3% (SE tax)          |
| Benefits          | Often health, retirement, PTO | None                                |
| Business expenses | Generally not deductible      | Deductible against income           |
| Retirement plans  | Employer's 401(k)             | SEP-IRA or Solo 401(k) — higher limits |
| Filing            | Form 1040                     | Form 1040 + Schedule C + Schedule SE|

*The trap:* a 1099 offer of $60,000 is *not* equivalent to a W-2 offer of $60,000. You'll owe
roughly an extra 7.65% in self-employment tax, and you'll buy your own health insurance.
Rule of thumb: 1099 needs to pay roughly *25–30% more* to match a W-2 offer with benefits.

*** Self-employment tax

*SE tax* is FICA for the self-employed — 15.3% (12.4% Social Security + 2.9% Medicare) on net
self-employment earnings. You pay both halves because you're both employer and employee.

Two mitigations: it's computed on 92.35% of net earnings, and you deduct half of it
above-the-line. Still, budget for it. The classic first-year freelancer disaster is
discovering in April that "I made $30,000 and spent it" means owing about $4,000 you don't
have.

*** Quarterly estimated taxes

With no withholding, the IRS wants payment as you earn, in four installments — roughly April
15, June 15, September 15, and January 15. Use *Form 1040-ES*, or just pay online at
=irs.gov/payments= (free, takes three minutes, and generates a record).

Miss them and you owe an underpayment penalty — effectively interest. Meet the *safe
harbor* (90% of this year's tax, or 100% of last year's) and you're protected.

*Practical system:* open a separate savings account labeled TAXES. Every time you're paid,
immediately move 25–30% into it. Pay quarterlies from it. The money was never yours; don't
let it sit in checking pretending otherwise.

*** Schedule C and deductible expenses

*Schedule C* reports business income and expenses. You're taxed on the *net* — what's left
after legitimate business expenses. Common ones:

- Home office (must be a space used *regularly and exclusively* for business — the kitchen
  table does not qualify, however much you'd like it to).
- Computer, monitor, software, and other equipment.
- Internet and phone (the business-use percentage).
- Professional development, courses, books relevant to the business.
- Business mileage (a per-mile rate set annually) or actual vehicle costs.
- Professional fees — accountant, lawyer.
- Business insurance.
- Self-employed health insurance premiums (above-the-line, not on Schedule C, but don't
  miss it).

Keep records contemporaneously. A separate business bank account and a separate card make
this nearly automatic and are worth the twenty minutes to set up.

*** QBI deduction

The *Qualified Business Income deduction* lets many self-employed people deduct up to 20% of
business income. Made permanent by 2025 legislation. It has limits and phase-outs at higher
incomes and for certain service businesses. Software handles it; just know the phrase so you
notice if it's missing.

*** Retirement plans for the self-employed

Meaningfully better than an IRA. See §8.8 — a *Solo 401(k)* can absorb a very large share of
self-employment income, which is one of the genuine advantages of 1099 work.

** 4.12 Actually filing your return

*** Deadlines

- *April 15* — filing deadline (shifts a day or two for weekends and holidays).
- *October 15* — deadline if you file an extension.
- An *extension gives you more time to file, not more time to pay.* Estimate and pay by April
  15 or interest accrues. People misunderstand this constantly.

*** How to file

| Method                        | Cost        | When it's right                        |
|-------------------------------+-------------+----------------------------------------|
| IRS Free File / Direct File   | Free        | Below the income threshold; simple return |
| FreeTaxUSA                    | ~$0 federal | Excellent, cheap, handles complexity    |
| TurboTax / H&R Block          | $40–120     | Popular, aggressive upselling           |
| VITA (volunteer prep)         | Free        | Below ~$60K income; IRS-trained volunteers |
| A CPA or enrolled agent       | $250–800+   | Self-employment, multi-state, unusual situations |

For a straightforward W-2 return with children, software is fine and a CPA is not necessary.
*FreeTaxUSA* is the quiet recommendation of people who do this for a living: federal filing
is free regardless of complexity, state is a small fee, and it handles Schedule C, rental
income, and most real-world complications.

*VITA* (Volunteer Income Tax Assistance) is genuinely good and genuinely free for lower
incomes — IRS-certified volunteers, often at libraries and community centers. Search "VITA
site locator."

*When to hire a professional:* self-employment with meaningful income, multi-state, rental
property, a business with employees, anything involving foreign accounts (Part 14 — this one
is not optional), or an IRS notice you don't understand.

*** What to bring / gather

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
- W-2 from each employer
- 1099s: NEC (contract work), INT (interest), DIV (dividends), B (investment sales),
         G (unemployment/state refund), R (retirement distributions), K (payment apps)
- 1098: mortgage interest;  1098-T: tuition;  1098-E: student loan interest
- 1095-A if you had marketplace health insurance (you cannot file correctly without it)
- Childcare provider's name, address, and tax ID
- Charitable contribution receipts
- Last year's return
- Social Security numbers for everyone in the household
- Bank routing and account number for direct deposit of the refund
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** Records: keep for how long?

- *Three years* — the normal IRS audit window.
- *Six years* — if income was substantially understated.
- *Seven years* — a safe default for most people.
- *Forever* — the returns themselves (they're small), and records of home purchases and
  improvements (needed to compute gain when you sell, possibly decades later), plus
  retirement account basis records (Form 8606 for nondeductible IRA contributions).

A scanned PDF per year in a cloud folder is fine and is what most people should do.

** 4.13 Audits, notices, and not panicking

*** Reality check

Overall audit rates are around 0.4%, and most "audits" are *correspondence audits* — a letter
asking about one line item, answered by mailing documentation. Nobody arrives at your door.

Higher-risk profiles: very high income, large Schedule C losses year after year, large
charitable deductions relative to income, claiming EITC with inconsistent information,
round numbers everywhere, and cash businesses.

*** If you get an IRS letter

1. *Open it.* The single worst response is the drawer.
2. *Read what it actually says.* Most are: math error corrected, a form missing, income
   reported to us that isn't on your return, or a balance due.
3. *Check whether they're right.* They're often right. Sometimes they're wrong — a duplicate
   1099, or a basis they don't know about.
4. *Respond by the deadline in the letter.* Deadlines are real; the consequences of silence
   are automatic assessments.
5. If you owe and can't pay: the IRS has *payment plans* (apply online) and, in genuine
   hardship, an *Offer in Compromise*. They are considerably more workable than their
   reputation, provided you engage.
6. *Never* respond to an IRS "call" or text or email demanding immediate payment in gift
   cards or crypto. The IRS initiates contact by *mail*. Anyone calling to threaten arrest is
   a criminal, full stop.

*** Frum-community-specific tax mistakes worth naming

Said plainly, because these cause real damage:

1. *"Off the books" income.* Working for cash and not reporting it is tax evasion — a crime,
   not a gray area. It also builds no Social Security credits, no work history for a
   mortgage, no verifiable income for a lease, and no unemployment eligibility. The
   short-term gain is real and the long-term cost is usually larger than people calculate.
2. *Mosdos issuing 1099s to people who should be W-2 employees.* Common, sometimes
   deliberate, and it shifts the entire employer FICA burden onto the worker. If you're
   treated as a contractor but work fixed hours under supervision using their equipment, you
   may be misclassified. Form SS-8 asks the IRS to determine status; be aware it can be a
   fraught thing to file while still employed there.
3. *Deducting non-deductible tzedakah.* Money to an individual, to a meshulach, or to a
   foreign institution without a US arm is not deductible. Giving it is still a mitzvah.
   Deducting it is still not allowed.
4. *Not filing because income was low.* Discussed above — this leaves refundable credits on
   the table, and those credits are frequently thousands of dollars.
5. *Ignoring a spouse's small business income.* A side business selling wigs, catering,
   graphic design — reportable, and also deductible-expense-generating. Report it and take
   the expenses; that's the better trade in most cases anyway.
6. *Kollel stipends.* Whether a kollel check is taxable income depends on its structure and
   how the institution treats it — some are reported, some are structured as scholarships,
   some are neither. Don't guess. Ask the mosad what they report and ask a CPA familiar with
   this world. The answer affects your return, your Social Security credits, and your
   eligibility for income-based programs, so it's worth the phone call.
** 4.14 If you start a business: entities, in plain words

The frum world is entrepreneurial. Someone in your circle sells on Amazon, does graphic
design, caters, runs a camp, flips real estate, or built a small software product. Here is
the vocabulary so you're not lost when it's your turn.

*** Sole proprietorship (the default)

If you earn money freelancing and do nothing else, you *are* a sole proprietorship. There is
no form to file, no registration, nothing to set up. Income goes on Schedule C of your
personal return (§4.11).

- *Cost:* $0.
- *Downside:* no liability protection. If the business is sued, your personal assets — your
  savings, your house — are exposed.

*** LLC (Limited Liability Company)

A legal entity registered with your state. Its purpose is right there in the name: it limits
your personal liability for business obligations.

- *Cost:* a state filing fee ($50–500 depending on state) plus an annual fee or report. New
  York has a peculiar and expensive *publication requirement* — you must publish notice in two
  newspapers, which can run over $1,000. Know that before you file in NY.
- *Taxes:* by default, a single-member LLC is a "disregarded entity" — taxed exactly like a
  sole proprietorship, on Schedule C. Forming an LLC by itself changes *nothing* about your
  taxes. People get this wrong constantly.
- *What it actually buys you:* separation. Business debts and lawsuits stop at the entity,
  *provided* you maintain the separation — separate bank account, separate records, no paying
  personal expenses from the business account. Mix the money and a court can "pierce the
  corporate veil," at which point you paid for nothing.

*** S-corporation election

Not an entity — an *election* an LLC or corporation makes with the IRS (Form 2553).

The point is a specific tax play: as a sole proprietor you pay 15.3% self-employment tax on
*all* net profit. As an S-corp, you pay yourself a "reasonable salary" (subject to payroll
tax) and take the rest as a *distribution*, which is not subject to self-employment tax.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Net profit: $120,000

  As a sole proprietor:
    SE tax on ~$110,800 (92.35% of profit) at 15.3%  ≈ $16,950

  As an S-corp with a $70,000 reasonable salary:
    Payroll tax on $70,000 at 15.3%                  ≈ $10,710
    $50,000 distribution: no SE tax                  ≈      $0
    ----
    Saved:                                           ≈  $6,240
    Minus payroll service and extra accounting       ≈ −$2,000
    Net benefit                                      ≈  $4,240
#+END_EXAMPLE

*The catch:* "reasonable salary" is a real IRS requirement with real enforcement. Pay yourself
$12,000 on $200,000 of profit and you are inviting an audit you will lose. And an S-corp
requires payroll processing, a separate business tax return (Form 1120-S), and more
accounting — call it $1,500–3,000 a year of overhead.

*Rule of thumb:* the S-corp election starts making sense somewhere around $50,000–80,000 of
net profit, and clearly makes sense above that. Below it, the overhead eats the savings.

*** Partnership

Two or more owners without an S-corp or C-corp election. Files Form 1065 and issues each
partner a *K-1* reporting their share.

*If you go into business with a friend or relative — and in this community you will be asked —
get a written partnership or operating agreement first.* It should cover: who owns what
percentage, who decides what, how profits are split, what happens if someone wants out, what
happens if someone dies, and how disputes are resolved. This document costs a lawyer's fee
and saves friendships. Handshake partnerships between people who trust each other completely
are the single most reliable way to destroy a relationship in the business world.

*** C-corporation

Double taxation (the company pays tax, then you pay tax on dividends). Almost never right for
a small business. Relevant only if you're raising venture capital or issuing stock to
employees.

*** What you actually need to do when you start earning freelance money

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. Open a separate business checking account. Do this first, before anything else.
2. Get an EIN (Employer Identification Number) at irs.gov. Free, instant, five minutes.
   Use it instead of your SSN on W-9 forms so clients don't hold your Social Security number.
3. Track income and expenses from day one. A spreadsheet is fine. Wave is free.
4. Set aside 25–30% of every payment for taxes, in a separate account (§4.11).
5. Pay quarterly estimated taxes.
6. Consider an LLC once you have real liability exposure or real revenue.
7. Consider an S-corp election once profit clears ~$50–80K.
8. Open a Solo 401(k) (§8.8) — the biggest tax advantage of self-employment.
9. Get business liability insurance if you're doing anything that could harm a client.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Note on "I'll just set up an LLC first."* People do this before they have any revenue, pay
the fees, and never file anything. Get the revenue first. The entity is a solution to a
problem you don't have yet.

* PART 5 — GOVERNMENT BENEFITS AND THE BENEFITS CLIFF

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* The government runs programs for working families with children and modest
incomes: health coverage, food help, childcare help. Use what you qualify for. Most American
families use at least one at some point, and there is nothing shameful about WIC.

Two rules, and both matter.

*Report honestly and quickly.* Underreporting income is a crime with real prison sentences
attached, and when frum families get prosecuted for it the damage runs far past the family
involved.

*Know where the "cliffs" are.* Some programs cut off completely at an exact income. Earn one
dollar past the line and you can lose $8,000 of health coverage. So a raise genuinely can
leave you worse off — not because of tax brackets, but because of this.

The answer isn't to stay poor. The answer is to know exactly where your cliff sits, and to
plan a jump clear over it rather than creeping onto the edge.
#+END_QUOTE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
This part is included because it is directly, concretely relevant to a kollel household with
one earner and small children, and because it is almost never explained properly. It is also
the part where I have to be careful to say two things at once: *use what you are legally
entitled to, and be scrupulously honest in what you report.* Both halves matter, and neither
excuses the other.
#+END_QUOTE

** 5.1 The programs, in plain terms

*** Medicaid

Government health insurance for lower-income people. Administered by states, so names and
rules vary (in New York it's just Medicaid; California calls it Medi-Cal). Income limits are
set as a percentage of the *federal poverty level* (FPL), which rises with household size —
so each additional child raises your eligibility ceiling.

Coverage is generally comprehensive, often with $0 or near-$0 premiums and copays. Provider
acceptance varies by area; in heavily frum neighborhoods, acceptance is usually good because
the local practices are used to it.

*** CHIP (Children's Health Insurance Program)

Health coverage for children in families that earn too much for Medicaid but not enough for
comfortable private insurance. Income ceilings are considerably higher than Medicaid's. In
many states children remain covered well into middle-income ranges.

*** WIC (Women, Infants, and Children)

Nutrition support for pregnant and postpartum women, infants, and children under five.
Provides specific foods — milk, eggs, cereal, formula, produce — plus nutrition counseling
and breastfeeding support. Income ceiling is around 185% of FPL, and being on Medicaid
usually confers automatic eligibility.

With a baby and more expected, this is the program most immediately relevant to you.

*** SNAP (Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, formerly food stamps)

Monthly benefit on an EBT card for groceries. Income and asset tests apply, varying by
state. Kosher food's higher cost is not reflected in the benefit formula, which is a real
and frequently-voiced frustration, but the program is what it is.

*** Housing assistance

*Section 8* (Housing Choice Vouchers) subsidizes rent in private housing, with the tenant
generally paying ~30% of income. Waiting lists in the tri-state area are extremely long,
often years, and sometimes closed entirely. Apply anyway if eligible; the list only moves if
you're on it.

*** Childcare assistance

Most states subsidize childcare for working (or sometimes studying) parents below an income
threshold. This one is large — childcare is often the second-biggest line in a young
family's budget after housing. Names vary by state; search "[your state] child care
assistance program."

*** HEAP / LIHEAP

Help with heating and cooling bills, typically a once-a-year benefit applied to your utility
account. Low effort, real money, badly underused.

*** School meal programs

Free and reduced-price breakfast and lunch. Many yeshivos and day schools participate.

*** Unemployment insurance

If you lose a job through no fault of your own, you may collect a percentage of prior wages
for a limited period. Funded by employer taxes on *reported* wages — which is another reason
being on the books matters. Self-employed people generally are not covered.

** 5.2 The benefits cliff: the real reason a raise can cost you money

Earlier I said that moving into a higher tax bracket cannot cost you money. True. But
*benefit phase-outs absolutely can*, and that is a completely different mechanism that
deserves its own explanation.

*** How it works

Most benefits phase out as income rises. Two shapes:

- A *gradual phase-out*: for each extra dollar earned, the benefit drops by some fraction of
  a dollar. Annoying but manageable — you still net more.
- A *cliff*: cross a threshold by one dollar, lose the entire benefit. Genuinely possible to
  end up worse off after a raise.

*** A worked example

Suppose a family's Medicaid eligibility ends at $52,000 of household income, and buying
comparable coverage on the marketplace would cost $700/month.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Income $51,500  →  Medicaid, $0 premiums
  Income $53,000  →  no Medicaid, $8,400/year in premiums

  Gross gain from the raise:      +$1,500
  Payroll and income tax on it:     −$300 (approx)
  Lost Medicaid, replaced:        −$8,400
                                  --------
  NET CHANGE:                     −$7,200
#+END_EXAMPLE

The family earned $1,500 more and is $7,200 poorer. That is a cliff. It's a genuine flaw in
how these programs interact, it is widely documented, and it lands hardest on exactly the
families trying hardest to climb.

*** The stacking problem

Now stack them. A household near the threshold may simultaneously face phase-outs of
Medicaid, SNAP, WIC, childcare subsidy, housing assistance, *and* the EITC. Economists who
measure this find *effective marginal tax rates* — the share of each additional dollar lost
to taxes plus reduced benefits — that can exceed 80% and occasionally exceed 100% over
certain income ranges.

Meaning: for some families, over some income ranges, earning an extra dollar leaves them
with less than a dollar, sometimes with nothing, sometimes worse off than before.

*** What to actually do about it

1. *Know where your cliffs are.* Get the specific income thresholds for every program you're
   on, for your household size, this year. They change annually and they change with each
   new baby (which usually helps — larger household, higher ceiling).
2. *Model any income change before accepting it.* When your wife is offered a raise, or you
   take on freelance work, run the actual numbers. This is a genuine, non-obvious, and
   completely legitimate reason to think carefully.
3. *Use pretax contributions as a lever.* Many programs count *gross* or *adjusted gross*
   income. Contributing to a traditional 401(k), an HSA, a Dependent Care FSA, or a
   traditional IRA reduces AGI and can keep you under a threshold. Note carefully: some
   programs use MAGI with items added back, so verify per program rather than assuming.
   This is one of the most useful and least known tools available to a family in this range.
4. *Plan the jump, don't creep.* The worst place to sit is just past a cliff. If a raise from
   $52K to $56K makes you worse off, the answer isn't to refuse raises forever — it's to
   understand that the next *worthwhile* move is to $70K, and to aim deliberately at that.
   Creeping across a cliff is the trap; jumping clear of it is the escape.
5. *Time transitions.* Many programs have eligibility redetermination dates. Understanding
   when yours falls can matter — not to hide anything, but to plan a transition rather than
   be surprised by one mid-year.

*** The strategic point for your specific situation

You're in kollel and studying computer science. Your wife works. In three to five years,
plausibly, you'll enter a field where entry-level salaries run $70,000–110,000 and senior
salaries much more.

That means you will cross *every* one of these cliffs, probably in a single year. That's
good — it's the whole point — but it needs planning:

- The year you start earning, your household income may double. Tax withholding, benefit
  eligibility, and health insurance all change at once.
- Report changes promptly. Most programs require you to report income changes within
  10–30 days. Failing to report and continuing to receive benefits is *fraud*, with real
  penalties including repayment and prosecution. This is not a technicality and people have
  been badly hurt by treating it as one.
- Build the emergency fund *before* the transition so a coverage gap is survivable.
- Expect the first year of real income to feel less transformative than it looks on paper,
  because taxes appear and benefits vanish simultaneously. Know it in advance so it's a
  prediction rather than a disappointment.

** 5.3 Honesty, and why it isn't merely a mussar point

Let me be direct, because this is a place where the frum world has taken real damage.

Benefits fraud — underreporting income, hiding assets, failing to report household changes —
is a federal and state crime. Convictions carry restitution, fines, and prison. Beyond the
individual: high-profile cases involving frum defendants generate coverage that makes life
harder for every honest family in the community, and creates a chilul Hashem that outlives
the case by decades.

The correct posture is not avoidance out of fear. It is this:

- *Apply for what you qualify for.* These programs exist for working families with children
  and modest incomes. Using them is not a moral failing, and a large majority of American
  families use one at some point. There is no shame in WIC.
- *Report accurately and promptly.* All household income, including cash, including gifts
  that count as income under that program's rules, including a spouse's side business.
- *Keep documentation.* If asked, you want a folder, not a memory.
- *When a rule is unclear, ask the agency in writing* and keep the answer. "The caseworker
  told me on the phone" is worth very little; a written answer is worth a great deal.
- *When you no longer qualify, exit cleanly.* Promptly, in writing, with a record.

** 5.4 The Israel version, briefly

Covered properly in Part 14, but the parallel is worth naming now: Israel has *Bituach
Leumi* (National Insurance), which provides child allowances (*kitzbat yeladim*) paid monthly
per child regardless of income, income support, disability benefits, and unemployment.

The philosophical difference is real: Israel's child allowance is *universal*, not
means-tested, so it does not create the same cliff structure. Large families receive
meaningful monthly amounts. Combined with subsidized healthcare and heavily subsidized
education, the arithmetic of raising many children is genuinely different in Israel — lower
income, but also dramatically lower per-child cost. This is one of the real financial
arguments in the aliyah conversation and it deserves to be made with actual numbers rather
than vibes. Part 14 does that.

* PART 6 — INSURANCE: WHAT TO BUY AND WHAT TO REFUSE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Insurance is for disasters you could not survive paying for yourself.
Someone dying. A hospital stay. Being sued. Buy those.

Do not buy insurance for things you could simply pay for — a cracked phone, a warranty on a
toaster, a service plan on a washing machine. On average the insurance company wins every
one of those bets. That is literally the business model. You buy insurance anyway for the
few risks where the worst case would wipe you out, because "on average" is not how you
experience one catastrophe.

For you right now, the important one is *term life insurance* on both of you. You are 24 and
healthy, so you will never be cheaper to insure than you are this year. A million dollars of
coverage costs roughly what a phone plan costs.

Someone will try to sell you "whole life" instead. It costs about ten times more and is
almost certainly the wrong product for you. Ask one question and watch what happens: *"How
are you paid on this, compared to the alternatives?"*
#+END_QUOTE

** 6.1 The single principle

*Insurance exists to transfer catastrophic risk you cannot absorb.*

That's it. That's the whole theory. Every correct insurance decision follows from applying
it, and every bad insurance purchase in your life will be a violation of it.

The corollaries do real work:

1. *Insure large, unlikely, ruinous events.* Death of an earner. A $400,000 hospitalization.
   A lawsuit. A house fire.
2. *Do not insure small, affordable events.* A cracked phone screen. A $200 appliance. Any
   "extended warranty" ever offered to you at a register.
3. *A higher deductible is usually correct*, if you have an emergency fund. You're paying a
   premium to avoid a cost you could cover; that's expensive by definition.
4. *Insurance is not an investment.* When someone sells you a product that is both, you are
   usually buying mediocre insurance bundled with a mediocre investment at high fees. §6.4
   handles this at length because it's the most common way young frum couples get sold
   something wrong.

*The arithmetic reason:* insurance companies employ actuaries specifically to ensure premiums
exceed expected payouts. On average, across all buyers, you lose money on insurance. You buy
it anyway for the risks whose *worst case* would destroy you — because "on average" is not
how you experience a single catastrophic event. For small risks, where the worst case is
merely irritating, that logic doesn't apply and the average is all there is.

** 6.2 Insurance vocabulary, defined once

| Term            | Definition                                                          |
|-----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Premium         | What you pay, usually monthly, to have coverage                     |
| Deductible      | What you pay out of pocket before insurance pays anything           |
| Copay           | A flat fee per service ($30 per office visit)                       |
| Coinsurance     | Your percentage share after the deductible (you 20%, insurer 80%)   |
| Out-of-pocket max | The most you can pay in a year. After this, insurance pays 100%   |
| Network         | Providers who've contracted with your insurer for set rates          |
| In-network      | Covered at the good rate                                            |
| Out-of-network  | Covered poorly or not at all; you may owe the difference            |
| Claim           | A request for the insurer to pay                                    |
| Rider           | An add-on to a policy                                               |
| Beneficiary     | Who receives the payout when you die                                |
| Underwriting    | The insurer evaluating your risk to decide price and eligibility    |
| Term            | The length of time a policy covers you                              |
| Face amount     | The payout amount of a life insurance policy (the "death benefit")  |

** 6.3 Health insurance

Complicated, expensive, and non-optional. Here's how to think about it.

*** How the four numbers interact

You have a $3,000 deductible, 20% coinsurance, and a $9,000 out-of-pocket maximum. You have
surgery costing $50,000 (negotiated in-network rate).

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  First $3,000            you pay (the deductible)
  Remaining $47,000       insurer pays 80% = $37,600
                          you pay 20%      =  $9,400
  Your total so far:      $3,000 + $9,400  = $12,400
  BUT your out-of-pocket max is $9,000
  → You pay $9,000. Insurer covers the rest.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*The out-of-pocket maximum is the most important number in the policy*, because it defines
your worst case. That's the number that answers "what's the most this can cost us?" — which
is the only question insurance is really for.

*** Where coverage comes from

1. *Employer plan.* Usually the best deal, because the employer pays a large share (often
   70–80% of the employee premium, though frequently much less for family coverage). If your
   wife's employer offers coverage, start there.
2. *ACA Marketplace* (=healthcare.gov= or your state's exchange). Buy directly, with
   income-based subsidies (the Premium Tax Credit, §4.9). Metal tiers — Bronze, Silver, Gold,
   Platinum — describe what share of costs the plan covers, not quality of care. Enrollment
   is limited to an annual *open enrollment* window unless you have a *qualifying life
   event*: marriage, birth, job loss, a move. *A baby is a qualifying event*, and there's a
   limited window (usually 30–60 days) to add the child — miss it and you wait for open
   enrollment.
3. *Medicaid / CHIP* (Part 5), if income-eligible.
4. *COBRA* — after leaving a job you can keep the employer plan for up to 18 months, but you
   pay the *entire* premium including the employer's former share, plus 2%. Typically
   $1,500–2,200/month for a family. Almost always more expensive than a marketplace plan, so
   compare before defaulting to it.

*** Plan types

- *HMO* — must use in-network providers; needs a referral from a primary care doctor for
  specialists. Cheaper, less flexible.
- *PPO* — larger network, no referrals needed, some out-of-network coverage. More expensive,
  more flexible.
- *EPO* — in-network only, but no referrals required. In between.
- *HDHP* — High-Deductible Health Plan. Higher deductible, lower premium, and — critically —
  it's the only kind that lets you use an *HSA* (§8.9).

*** Choosing a plan: how to actually decide

Do not pick by premium alone. Compute the *total annual cost* under two scenarios:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
GOOD YEAR (nothing happens):
    12 × monthly premium + routine care copays

BAD YEAR (something serious):
    12 × monthly premium + out-of-pocket maximum
#+END_EXAMPLE

Run both for each plan offered. A high-deductible plan often wins the good year *and* has a
similar bad-year total, because low premiums compound over twelve months.

Then check three things that override arithmetic:

1. *Are your doctors in network?* Especially your wife's OB and the pediatrician. Look them
   up individually on the insurer's site — don't trust the practice's word for it.
2. *Are your medications covered?* Check the *formulary*, the list of covered drugs and their
   tiers.
3. *If you're planning a pregnancy*, model that as the expected year, not the good year.
   Maternity care runs $10,000–30,000 before insurance; assume you'll hit the deductible and
   possibly the out-of-pocket max.

*** For a family that expects many children

Plan on hitting the deductible most years. That changes the calculus toward plans with lower
out-of-pocket maximums even at higher premiums, and it makes the "cheap high-deductible
plan" less obviously attractive than it looks for a single 25-year-old.

Also: keep a sinking fund for the deductible (§1.7). It resets every January, and a January
or February baby means paying two years' deductibles within a few months if timing is
unkind.

*** FSA and HSA, briefly

- *FSA (Flexible Spending Account)* — pretax money for medical expenses, through an employer.
  *Use it or lose it* by year end (some plans allow a small carryover or a grace period). Fund
  it to your *predictable* spending, not aspirationally.
- *HSA (Health Savings Account)* — pretax money for medical expenses, requires an HDHP.
  Money rolls over forever, is yours if you leave the job, can be invested, and has the best
  tax treatment of any account in the US code. Covered fully in §8.9, because it's secretly a
  retirement account.
- *Dependent Care FSA* — pretax money for childcare. Separate from the medical FSA. Real money
  for a family paying for daycare.

*** Reading a medical bill

Two documents arrive and people confuse them constantly:

- *EOB (Explanation of Benefits)* — from the *insurer*. Says what was billed, what they paid,
  what you may owe. It is *not a bill*. It usually says "THIS IS NOT A BILL" in small print
  that nobody reads.
- *The actual bill* — from the *provider*.

Check that they agree. Billing errors are extremely common — duplicate charges, services not
rendered, in-network providers billed as out-of-network. Call and question anything odd; ask
for an itemized bill, which you're entitled to.

*Hospitals negotiate.* Ask for a discount, ask about financial assistance (nonprofit
hospitals are legally required to have a charity care policy), and ask for a payment plan
before putting a medical bill on a credit card. Medical debt is generally 0% interest and
non-urgent; credit card debt is 25% and urgent. Never convert the former into the latter.

*The No Surprises Act* (federal, effective 2022) protects you from most surprise
out-of-network bills for emergency care and for out-of-network providers at in-network
facilities. If you get a surprise bill from, say, an anesthesiologist you never chose, look
this up before paying.

** 6.4 Life insurance — and the sales pitch you are going to hear

*** What it does

You pay premiums; if you die while covered, the insurer pays a lump sum (the *death benefit*)
to your *beneficiaries*, generally free of income tax.

*** Who needs it

Anyone whose death would cause financial hardship to someone else. That means:

- *Any earner with dependents.* Obviously.
- *A non-earning spouse* who provides childcare. If your wife died, you would need to pay for
  childcare, and that is a large real cost. Insure her too — a smaller amount, but a real one.
- *You, right now,* even in kollel with little income. Two reasons: your wife would face
  raising a child alone with reduced capacity to work, and — importantly — *you will never be
  cheaper to insure than you are at 24 in good health.* A policy bought now locks in a rate
  for its whole term.

Who doesn't need it: a single person with no dependents, and a retiree whose assets already
cover their family.

*** Term vs permanent — the argument you need to understand

*Term life insurance* covers a fixed period — 10, 20, or 30 years. If you die during it, it
pays. If you don't, it expires and pays nothing. It is pure insurance, and it's cheap.

*Permanent life insurance* (whole life, universal life, variable universal life) covers you
for life and includes a *cash value* component that grows over time. It is expensive —
routinely 8–15× the cost of equivalent term coverage.

Ballpark, for a healthy 25-year-old male, $1,000,000 of coverage:

| Product              | Approximate cost   |
|----------------------+--------------------|
| 20-year term         | $30–45 per month   |
| 30-year term         | $50–70 per month   |
| Whole life           | $600–900 per month |

*** Why you will be sold whole life

Because commissions on whole life are enormous — often *the entire first year's premium*,
sometimes more, paid to the agent. Commission on term insurance is a small fraction of that.

The result: an entire industry of agents, many of them lovely people who genuinely believe
in the product, incentivized to explain to a young couple that whole life is "forced
savings," "tax-free growth," "a legacy," "an asset you can borrow against," and "insurance
that doesn't expire." In the frum world these conversations frequently happen through
someone from shul, someone's cousin, someone who came to your chasunah. That social layer is
precisely what makes it hard to say no.

*** The honest analysis

Whole life's problems:

1. *You get insurance OR cash value, not both.* In most traditional whole life policies, the
   cash value is absorbed by the insurer when you die; your family receives the death
   benefit only. You saved into an account that vanishes.
2. *Terrible early returns.* Fees and commissions come out first. Cash value is typically
   near zero for several years, and internal rates of return over decades often land around
   2–4% — well below index fund returns and sometimes below inflation.
3. *"Borrow against it" means borrowing your own money, with interest.* And unpaid loans
   reduce the death benefit.
4. *Enormous opportunity cost.* The difference between $50/month term and $700/month whole
   life is $650/month. Invested at 7% for thirty years, that's roughly *$760,000*. You'd need
   very impressive policy performance to compete with that, and whole life does not deliver
   it.
5. *Complexity is a feature for the seller.* Illustrations project non-guaranteed dividends
   with confident-looking charts. Ask which columns are *guaranteed*. The answer is usually
   sobering, and asking the question changes the conversation immediately.

*** "Buy term and invest the difference"

The standard, well-supported advice: buy cheap term insurance for the years your family is
vulnerable, and invest the money you saved in low-cost index funds. You end up with both
more protection and more money in the overwhelming majority of scenarios.

*** When permanent insurance is genuinely appropriate

I'm not going to pretend it's never right. Legitimate cases:

- *Estate tax planning* for very large estates (well over $10M), where permanent insurance
  funds an estate tax bill and keeps a business or property intact.
- *A special-needs child* who will require support for life, where a guaranteed payout
  whenever you die is genuinely valuable and a 30-year term isn't.
- *A business succession* funding a buy-sell agreement between partners.
- *Someone who is genuinely, provably incapable of saving any other way* — this is a real
  human category and I'd rather they own whole life than nothing.

None of these describes a 24-year-old kollel yungerman with a baby. If someone is selling
you whole life, the honest question is: "What am I in this list?"

*** How much term coverage

Common approaches:

- *10–12× annual income* — rough, standard, easy.
- *DIME method* — Debt + Income (years needed × annual) + Mortgage + Education. More precise.
- *Needs-based* — what would your family actually need? Pay off the mortgage, replace income
  until the youngest is independent, fund tuition, cover a chasunah or several.

For a frum family planning many children, the numbers run higher than mainstream calculators
suggest, because tuition and chasunos are large obligations mainstream calculators don't
model. If you're the future earner in a family of eight, $1.5–2M of term coverage is not
extravagant, and at your age it costs less than a phone plan.

*** How to buy it

1. Decide on amount and term. A *30-year term* bought now covers you to 54, by which point
   your kids are largely launched and you have assets. Alternatively "laddering" — a 30-year
   policy plus a 20-year policy — front-loads coverage in the expensive years and costs less
   overall.
2. Shop through a broker who works with many carriers, or a comparison site. Ask directly:
   "Are you paid the same regardless of which product I choose?"
3. Expect *underwriting*: a health questionnaire, often a paramedical exam (blood, urine,
   height, weight). Some carriers offer no-exam policies at a modest price premium.
4. *Buy while you're young and healthy.* Rates are locked at issue. A diagnosis at 30 can make
   coverage expensive or unobtainable. This is the strongest argument for doing it this year.
5. *Name your beneficiaries*, and name *contingent* beneficiaries. Then re-check them after
   every birth. See §13.3, because beneficiary designations override wills and this catches
   people out constantly.
6. Consider whether the beneficiary should be a *trust* rather than a person if the children
   are minors — a minor cannot legally receive a large payout, and the default outcome
   (court-supervised guardianship of the funds) is worse than a simple trust. Part 13.

*** Group life through an employer

Many employers provide free coverage of 1–2× salary. Take it — it's free. But don't rely on
it: it's usually inadequate in amount and it disappears when you leave the job, often at the
exact moment you can least afford to be uninsured. Own your own policy.

** 6.5 Disability insurance — the coverage nobody buys and many need

*** The case, in one number

You are far more likely to become disabled during your working years than to die during
them. Estimates put the odds of a working-age adult experiencing a disability lasting 90+
days at somewhere around one in four.

Yet most people insure death and not disability, because death is easier to imagine and
someone is actively selling it.

*** What it does

Pays a percentage of your income (typically 60%) if you can't work due to illness or injury.

- *Short-term disability* — covers weeks to a few months. Often employer-provided. Some states
  (NY, NJ, CA, and others) mandate it. *This is what covers maternity leave in many cases*,
  which makes it immediately relevant to your household.
- *Long-term disability* — kicks in after a waiting period (the *elimination period*, often 90
  days) and can run to age 65. This is the one that matters most and the one people lack.

*** Key policy terms that determine whether it's worth anything

- *"Own occupation"* — pays if you can't do *your* job. A programmer who develops a condition
  preventing programming collects, even if they could theoretically work as a greeter.
- *"Any occupation"* — pays only if you can't do *any* work you're reasonably suited for. Much
  weaker, much cheaper, and it's what most cheap group policies provide.

Own-occupation coverage costs more and is worth it for a skilled professional.

- *Elimination period* — how long before payments start. Longer = cheaper. Your emergency fund
  covers the gap, which is one more argument for having one.
- *Benefit period* — how long payments last. To age 65 is the meaningful version.
- *Non-cancelable and guaranteed renewable* — the insurer can't raise your rate or drop you.
- *Cost-of-living adjustment (COLA)* rider — benefits rise with inflation. Valuable on a
  policy that might pay for thirty years.
- *Taxation* — if the employer pays the premium, benefits are *taxable*. If you pay with
  after-tax dollars, benefits are *tax-free*. So a "60% of income" individual policy you paid
  for is worth notably more than the same 60% through work.

*** Why this is sharper for you specifically

Two reasons:

1. *SSDI requires work credits* (§4.5). Years in kollel without reported earnings mean you may
   not qualify for Social Security disability at all. The government backstop most people
   quietly assume exists may simply not be there for you.
2. *Your future earning power is your family's main asset.* At 24 with a CS career ahead, the
   present value of your lifetime earnings is measured in millions. That asset is
   uninsured until you insure it.

*** When to buy

When you start earning — most policies are priced against income, so there's little to
insure now. But buy it in your first year of professional work, when you're young, healthy,
and cheap. Check first whether your employer offers group long-term disability; if so,
consider a supplemental individual policy to top up the coverage and to own something
portable.

** 6.6 Renters and homeowners insurance

*** Renters insurance

If you rent, get it. It's absurdly cheap — $10–25 a month — and covers:

- *Your belongings*, against fire, theft, water damage. Your landlord's policy covers the
  building, not your things. People are consistently shocked by this.
- *Liability*, if someone is injured in your apartment or you damage the building.
- *Additional living expenses*, if the place becomes uninhabitable and you need somewhere to
  stay — meaningful in a fire or flood.

The most commonly overlooked part is liability, which is also the part that could actually
ruin you. A kitchen fire that spreads to neighboring units is not a theoretical scenario in
dense frum neighborhoods with a lot of yom tov cooking.

*** Homeowners insurance

Required by any mortgage lender. Covers the structure, your belongings, liability, and
living expenses.

Things to get right:

- *Replacement cost, not actual cash value.* Replacement cost rebuilds; actual cash value pays
  depreciated value, which after ten years is a fraction of what you need.
- *Dwelling coverage should equal rebuild cost*, not market value. These differ a lot — the
  land isn't going anywhere in a fire.
- *Flood is not covered.* Ever. By any standard homeowners policy. Flood insurance is separate,
  usually through the NFIP. Check your flood zone; note that a meaningful share of flood
  claims come from outside designated high-risk zones.
- *Earthquake* is also separate, where relevant.
- *Sewer/water backup* is usually an inexpensive rider and a common claim. Add it.
- *Scheduled personal property* — standard policies cap jewelry, silver, and similar at low
  amounts (often $1,500 total for jewelry). Frum households frequently own silver, a
  significant sheitel collection, jewelry, and valuable seforim well beyond those caps.
  Schedule them individually, with an appraisal. Photograph everything.

*** Documentation

Walk through your home with your phone, recording video of every room, closet, and drawer.
Narrate. Store it in the cloud. Ten minutes, and it's the difference between a smooth claim
and an argument with an adjuster about whether you owned a dining room set.

** 6.7 Auto insurance

*** Required components
