- *Liability* — damage and injury you cause to others. State minimums are almost always far
  too low. A serious accident produces claims well into six figures; minimum coverage might
  be $25,000. *Carry at least $250,000/$500,000.* The extra cost is small; the extra
  protection is not.
- *Property damage liability* — damage you cause to others' property.
- *Uninsured/underinsured motorist* — covers you when the other driver has nothing. About one
  in eight American drivers is uninsured. Get this.
- *Personal Injury Protection (PIP)* — required in no-fault states like NY and NJ; covers
  medical costs regardless of fault.

*** Optional components

- *Collision* — damage to your car in an accident.
- *Comprehensive* — theft, fire, weather, a tree, a deer.

Rule of thumb: drop collision and comprehensive when the car's value drops below roughly ten
times the annual premium for them. Paying $600/year to insure a $3,000 car is poor value.

*** How to lower the premium

- Raise the deductible (if the emergency fund can absorb it).
- Bundle with renters or homeowners — usually a real discount.
- Shop every 2–3 years. Loyalty is actively punished in this industry; "price optimization"
  means long-tenured customers often pay more.
- Ask about every discount: good driver, low mileage, defensive driving course, good student,
  paid-in-full, paperless.
- Your credit score affects your premium in most states (§3.1).
- Choose the car with the premium in mind. A minivan is cheaper to insure than a sporty
  sedan, and in your future, a minivan is coming anyway.

** 6.8 Umbrella liability insurance

An *umbrella policy* adds liability coverage on top of your auto and home policies — typically
$1,000,000 for $150–300 a year, which is remarkable value.

It covers you when a judgment exceeds your underlying limits: a serious car accident, a
guest injured at your home, your child causing significant damage, certain defamation
claims.

*Who should have one:* anyone with meaningful assets to protect, or meaningful future income
a judgment could attach. Once you're a homeowner with a professional income, get one. It's
the cheapest large protection available.

** 6.9 What NOT to buy

| Product                          | Why to skip it                                    |
|----------------------------------+---------------------------------------------------|
| Extended warranties              | Priced to be profitable; self-insure              |
| Credit card payment protection   | Expensive, riddled with exclusions                |
| Rental car insurance at counter  | Usually covered by your card or auto policy — check first |
| Cancer / dread disease policies  | Health insurance already covers illness           |
| Accidental death (AD&D)          | Only pays for a narrow cause of death. Buy term   |
| Mortgage life insurance          | Declining benefit, fixed premium. Buy term        |
| Child life insurance             | Children have no income to replace. (See note)    |
| Flight insurance                 | Statistically absurd                              |
| Identity theft insurance         | Freeze your credit instead — free and better      |
| Phone insurance                  | Usually a bad deal; self-insure with a case       |
| Indexed universal life           | Complexity as a sales tool. See §6.4              |

*The child life insurance note:* the one real argument is guaranteeing *future insurability*
— a small policy with a rider letting the child buy more later regardless of health. It's a
minor benefit for a real cost, and it's usually sold with emotional pressure rather than
arithmetic. If someone raises it at a bris, the answer is "let me think about it," which is
the answer to every insurance pitch.

** 6.10 How to deal with an insurance agent

*** The three questions

1. *"How are you compensated on this product versus the alternatives?"* Watch the answer.
   Watch whether they answer.
2. *"Which numbers in this illustration are guaranteed and which are projected?"* For
   permanent life insurance, this question is decisive.
3. *"What would this cost as plain term insurance, and can you show me both quotes side by
   side?"*

*** The two rules

- *Never sign at the first meeting.* Take the paperwork home. Any product that requires an
  immediate decision is a product that fears comparison.
- *Get a second opinion from someone with no stake.* A fee-only fiduciary financial planner
  (§7.14) charges a flat fee and doesn't sell products. One hour of that is cheap relative to
  a thirty-year commitment.

*** The frum-community version

The person selling you insurance may daven in your shul, may have made your shidduch, may be
your wife's cousin. This is genuinely hard, and pretending otherwise is useless.

Two things that help:

- "I have a rule that I don't sign financial paperwork the same day. Let me take it home."
  This is a *policy*, not a judgment on them, and it's much easier to say than "no."
- You can buy something small from them and still not buy the wrong big thing. Term life
  through a friend at a fair price is fine. A $700/month whole life policy is not fine, and
  the friendship is not worth $250,000 of opportunity cost over thirty years.

If someone pressures you after "let me think about it," you've learned what you needed to
learn about that relationship's commercial dimension.
** 6.11 Long-term care, Medicare, and aging parents

Distant for you, urgent for your parents, and the reason many families lose an inheritance
they assumed was coming. Read it once now so you recognize the terms when they come up in a
phone call about your father.

*** Long-term care: the risk nobody insures

*Long-term care* means help with daily living — bathing, dressing, eating, moving — over
months or years. Provided at home by an aide, in an assisted living facility, or in a nursing
home.

The critical fact people don't know:

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*Health insurance does not cover long-term care. Medicare does not cover long-term care.*
Medicare covers a limited stretch of skilled nursing after a qualifying hospital stay —
roughly up to 100 days, with cost-sharing after the first 20 — and then it stops. Custodial
care, which is what most people actually need, is not covered at all.
#+END_QUOTE

The costs, roughly, and they vary enormously by region:

| Care type                      | Approximate annual cost |
|--------------------------------+-------------------------|
| Home health aide (part-time)   | $30,000–60,000          |
| Home health aide (full-time)   | $60,000–150,000+        |
| Assisted living facility       | $55,000–90,000          |
| Nursing home, semi-private     | $95,000–130,000         |
| Nursing home, private room     | $110,000–160,000        |

Roughly half of people turning 65 will need some paid long-term care in their lifetime, and a
minority will need years of it. It is the single largest uninsured financial risk most
American families face.

*** The three ways it gets paid for

1. *Out of savings.* Which is how a lifetime of assets disappears in three years.
2. *Long-term care insurance.* Bought in one's 50s or early 60s; expensive, and premiums on
   older policies have been raised sharply. *Hybrid* policies — life insurance with a
   long-term care rider — have largely replaced standalone ones and are worth a look when your
   parents are in that age range. This is one of the few places where permanent life insurance
   has a defensible role (§6.4).
3. *Medicaid.* The payer of last resort, and the largest actual funder of nursing home care in
   America. But Medicaid requires you to spend down assets to a very low level first, and it
   has a *five-year lookback*: transfers of assets in the five years before applying can
   trigger a penalty period of ineligibility.

*** The lookback trap, stated plainly

Parents who give money to children — for a down payment, for a chasunah, for tuition — and
then need nursing home care within five years can find those gifts counted against them,
creating months of ineligibility during which someone must pay privately.

This is a real, common, and avoidable problem. If your parents have meaningful assets and may
need care, the conversation with an *elder law attorney* should happen years before it's
needed, not during a hospitalization. It's a genuine specialty and the planning is legitimate
and legal — it just has to be done in advance.

*** Medicare, briefly

Health insurance for people 65+ (and for some younger people with disabilities). Four parts,
badly named:

| Part | Covers                        | Cost                                    |
|------+-------------------------------+-----------------------------------------|
| A    | Hospital care                 | Free for most (you paid via FICA)       |
| B    | Doctors, outpatient           | Monthly premium, income-adjusted        |
| C    | "Medicare Advantage" — a private plan bundling A, B, usually D | Varies |
| D    | Prescription drugs            | Monthly premium                         |

*Medigap* (also called Medicare Supplement) is separate private insurance covering the gaps
in A and B. You generally choose between Medigap + Original Medicare, or a Medicare Advantage
plan — not both.

Two things worth knowing now:

1. *Enrollment deadlines are real and the penalties are permanent.* Missing your Part B
   enrollment window can add a penalty to your premium *for life*. If your parents are
   approaching 65, make sure someone is paying attention to this.
2. *Medicare does not cover you outside the United States.* Relevant to anyone contemplating
   retirement in Israel (Part 14).

*** The conversation to have with your parents

Awkward, and much less awkward now than during a crisis. What you want to know:

- Do they have a will, a power of attorney, and a healthcare proxy? Where are they?
- Do they have long-term care insurance? Life insurance?
- What are their retirement resources, roughly?
- Who do they want making decisions if they can't?
- Is there an attorney, an accountant, an advisor? Names and numbers?
- Have they thought about long-term care at all?

You are not asking what they're worth. You are asking who to call and where the paperwork is.
Frame it that way and it goes much better.

* PART 7 — INVESTING

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Prices rise about 3% a year, so money sitting in a bank quietly loses value
even though the number on the statement never drops. Money you won't need for ten years or
more has to grow faster than that. That means investing.

Investing sounds complicated. It isn't — once you ignore the industry built around making it
sound complicated. Here is the whole thing:

Buy *one* fund that owns a tiny piece of thousands of companies at once, costs almost nothing
to own, and asks no decisions of you. Put money in every month. Then don't touch it. Not for
thirty years. Especially not when it drops 30%, which it will, several times.

That's the entire strategy, and over long stretches it beats what most professional money
managers achieve. Not because it's clever. Because it's cheap and it doesn't panic.

The reason it works is time. Money roughly doubles every ten years. You're 24 — that's four
doublings before you're 65. Nobody can sell you those. You can only start early.
#+END_QUOTE

** 7.1 What investing actually is

*Investing* means buying something that produces value over time, in the expectation of
ending up with more than you started with.

*Saving* is different: putting money somewhere safe where it will still be there. Savings
preserve. Investments grow — and can shrink.

The distinction matters because they have different jobs:

- Money you need within five years: *save* it. Cash, HYSA, CDs.
- Money you don't need for ten-plus years: *invest* it, or inflation quietly eats it (§1.10).

** 7.2 Compounding: the entire reason any of this works

*Compound growth* means your returns start earning returns.

Year one: $1,000 grows 10% to $1,100. Year two you earn 10% on $1,100, not on $1,000 — $110,
not $100. Trivial at first, absurd later.

*** The rule of 72

Divide 72 by the annual return to get the years to double.

| Return | Doubling time |
|--------+---------------|
| 3%     | 24 years      |
| 6%     | 12 years      |
| 7%     | ~10 years     |
| 10%    | ~7 years      |

At 7%, money doubles roughly every decade. You're 24. By 65 that's about four doublings —
$1 becomes about $16.

*** Why starting now beats starting later, dramatically

Two people invest $300/month at 7%:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  AVI starts at 25, stops at 35. Ten years of contributions: $36,000 total.
       Then never adds another dollar; leaves it alone until 65.
       At 65: roughly $393,000

  BINYAMIN starts at 35, contributes until 65. Thirty years: $108,000 total.
       At 65: roughly $367,000
#+END_EXAMPLE

Avi invested a third as much and finished ahead. The ten years between 25 and 35 were worth
more than the thirty years between 35 and 65.

This is the most important chart in personal finance and it is the reason I keep pointing at
your age. You are currently holding the asset — a decade of compounding — that no amount of
money can buy back later.

*** The corollary you can act on this year

Even small amounts matter enormously right now. $50/month at 24 is meaningful. Waiting until
"we can afford to do it properly" is the single most expensive form of perfectionism
available to a young couple.

** 7.3 What you can actually buy

*** Stocks

A *stock* (or *share*, or *equity*) is a fractional ownership stake in a company. Own one
share of a company with a million shares and you own a millionth of it — a millionth of its
profits, assets, and future.

You make money two ways:
- *Capital appreciation* — the share price rises and you sell for more than you paid.
- *Dividends* — the company distributes some profit to shareholders, usually quarterly.

Stocks are volatile. Individual companies fail entirely. The stock market as a whole has
historically returned about 10% nominal per year over long periods — but with terrifying
interruptions: −37% in 2008, −34% in a few weeks in early 2020, −50%+ in 2000–2002.

*** Bonds

A *bond* is a loan. You lend money to a government or corporation; they pay you interest
(the *coupon*) periodically and return the *principal* at *maturity*.

Safer than stocks, lower returns. US Treasury bonds are considered the safest financial
asset in the world.

The one non-obvious mechanic: *bond prices move opposite to interest rates.* If you hold a
bond paying 3% and new bonds pay 5%, nobody wants yours at full price, so its market value
falls. Hold to maturity and you still get your principal; sell early and you take the hit.
This is why 2022 was miserable for bonds — rates rose fast and bond values fell.

*** Mutual funds

A *mutual fund* pools money from many investors to buy a basket of stocks or bonds. You buy
shares of the fund and own a slice of everything in it.

- *Actively managed* — a manager picks investments, trying to beat the market. Higher fees.
- *Index fund* — mechanically holds everything in a market index, no picking. Very low fees.

*** ETFs

An *Exchange-Traded Fund* is a mutual fund that trades like a stock during the day. For your
purposes, an index ETF and an index mutual fund are nearly interchangeable; ETFs are
slightly more tax-efficient in taxable accounts and have no minimum purchase.

*** Index funds, and why they win

An *index* is a list of companies (the S&P 500 is roughly the 500 largest US public
companies). An *index fund* simply owns all of them, weighted by size, and does nothing else.

The case for them is one of the most robust findings in finance: *over 10–20 year periods,
roughly 85–90% of actively managed funds underperform their index.* Not because managers are
stupid — because fees compound against you and because markets are hard to beat consistently.

Then the practical problem: even if 10% of managers will outperform, you must identify them
*in advance*, and past performance is a famously poor predictor.

So the strategy the evidence supports is: buy the whole market at the lowest possible cost
and stop trying. It's boring. It works. Warren Buffett's instruction for his own estate is a
low-cost S&P 500 index fund, which should tell you something.

*** Expense ratios

The *expense ratio* is the annual percentage a fund charges. 0.03% means $3 per $10,000 per
year. 1% means $100.

That sounds small. Over decades it isn't:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  $100,000 invested for 30 years at 7% before fees:

    0.03% expense ratio  →  roughly $754,000
    1.00% expense ratio  →  roughly $574,000

  Difference: about $180,000, for holding essentially the same thing.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Fees are the one variable in investing you fully control.* Returns are uncertain. Fees are
certain. Minimize what's certain.

*Target:* keep every fund you own under 0.20%, ideally under 0.10%. Broad index funds from
Vanguard, Fidelity, Schwab, and iShares are all in that range.

*** Other things people will offer you

- *Individual stocks* — fine as entertainment with a tiny slice, bad as a strategy. You are
  competing against people with better information and faster computers.
- *Crypto* — speculative. It may go up. It has no cash flows and no intrinsic valuation
  anchor, so nobody, including its proponents, can tell you what it should be worth. If you
  must, cap it at a few percent of assets and be genuinely prepared to lose all of it.
- *Real estate* — a real asset class with real returns, real work, and real leverage risk.
  Part 9 covers the house you live in. Investment property is a business, not a passive
  investment, and should be entered as such.
- *Gold* — a hedge against catastrophe that produces nothing. Small allocation at most.
- *Annuities* — insurance products that pay income. Some (simple immediate annuities in
  retirement) are legitimate. Most sold to young people are expensive and inappropriate.
- *Options, futures, forex, day trading* — ways to lose money faster. The retail success rate
  is dismal and well documented.
- *"An opportunity" from someone in shul* — read §17.4 before you write a check. Please.

** 7.4 Risk, and what it actually means

*** Volatility is not the same as risk

*Volatility* is how much a price moves around. *Risk* is the chance of permanently losing
money or failing to meet your goal.

For a 24-year-old investing for retirement in forty years, volatility is mostly noise. The
market falling 30% next year is genuinely irrelevant to you *provided you don't sell*, and
is arguably good — you're buying for forty more years at lower prices.

For a 64-year-old who needs the money next year, that same 30% is catastrophic. Same event,
different risk, because the time horizon differs.

*The real risk for you is not volatility. It's (a) selling in a panic, and (b) not investing
at all.*

*** What the market actually does

Some perspective, because people who haven't lived through a crash imagine it wrong:

- Roughly one year in four is negative.
- A 10% drop ("correction") happens about once a year.
- A 20% drop ("bear market") happens roughly every five to seven years.
- A 50% drop has happened twice since 2000.
- And yet: every single historical 20-year holding period in the broad US market has been
  positive.

Crashes are not anomalies. They are a scheduled feature you cannot schedule. Plan for them
in advance — decide *now* what you'll do when your account is down 35% — because deciding in
the moment goes badly for nearly everyone.

*** The behavior gap

Studies consistently find that the average investor earns meaningfully less than the average
fund, because people buy after things go up and sell after they go down. The fund returns
10%; the investors in it earn 7%. The gap is entirely self-inflicted.

*This is where your money will actually be won or lost.* Not in fund selection. In not
touching it.

** 7.5 Asset allocation

*Asset allocation* is how you split money among asset classes — stocks, bonds, cash. It is
the decision that drives most of your outcome, far more than which specific funds you pick.

*** Stocks vs bonds by time horizon

| Money needed in | Suggested stock % |
|-----------------+-------------------|
| < 3 years       | 0% — keep it in cash |
| 3–5 years       | 0–30%             |
| 5–10 years      | 40–60%            |
| 10–20 years     | 70–90%            |
| 20+ years       | 90–100%           |

Old rule of thumb: stock percentage = 110 minus your age. At 24 that's 86% stocks. Many
would say a 24-year-old with a forty-year horizon should simply be at 90–100% stocks, and
the historical record supports that — *if* you can genuinely tolerate watching the balance
halve without flinching.

Be honest with yourself about that. A 100% stock portfolio you panic-sell in a crash is far
worse than an 80/20 portfolio you hold through it.

*** Diversification

*Diversification* means not depending on any single outcome. Own thousands of companies
across many industries and countries, so that any one failing is a rounding error.

An index fund does this automatically. A total US stock market fund holds roughly 3,500
companies. That's why the single-fund approaches below are legitimate rather than lazy.

*** The three-fund portfolio

The classic simple portfolio (associated with the "Bogleheads," followers of Vanguard
founder John Bogle):

1. *Total US stock market index fund* — every public US company.
2. *Total international stock index fund* — everywhere else.
3. *Total bond market index fund* — US investment-grade bonds.

A reasonable allocation for you at 24: 60% US stocks, 30% international, 10% bonds. Or, if
you prefer simple: 70/30 US/international with no bonds for now.

Example tickers (Fidelity / Vanguard / Schwab):

| Role          | Fidelity | Vanguard | Schwab |
|---------------+----------+----------+--------|
| Total US      | FSKAX    | VTSAX/VTI| SWTSX  |
| International | FTIHX    | VTIAX/VXUS| SWISX |
| Total bond    | FXNAX    | VBTLX/BND| SWAGX  |

*** The one-fund portfolio (probably what you should actually do)

A *target-date fund* holds a diversified mix and automatically shifts from stocks to bonds as
the target year approaches. You buy one fund and never think about it again.

For you: "Target Retirement 2065" or thereabouts. Look for a *low-cost index-based* version —
Vanguard, Fidelity Freedom *Index* (note the word "Index"; the non-index Fidelity Freedom
funds cost several times more for the same idea), and Schwab all offer them around
0.08–0.15%.

Honest assessment: a target-date fund is very slightly less optimal than a hand-built
portfolio and *dramatically* better than the portfolio most people actually maintain. For a
busy couple with a baby and no interest in becoming hobbyist investors, it's the right
answer, and I'd rather you do this than do nothing while planning something better.

*** Rebalancing

Over time, allocations drift — stocks go up, so the stock share grows. *Rebalancing* means
selling some of what grew and buying what lagged, to return to the target.

Do it annually, or when something drifts more than 5 percentage points. In a target-date
fund it's automatic. In a retirement account, rebalancing has no tax consequence; in a
taxable account it can, so prefer rebalancing with new contributions there.

** 7.6 Dollar-cost averaging vs lump sum

*Dollar-cost averaging (DCA)* means investing a fixed amount on a schedule — $400 on the
first of every month — regardless of price. Buys more shares when prices are low, fewer when
high, and removes the temptation to time anything.

*This is automatically what you're doing* with regular paycheck contributions, and it's a
good default.

If you receive a lump sum (an inheritance, a bonus, a large refund): the research says
investing it all at once beats spreading it over months about two-thirds of the time, simply
because markets rise more often than they fall. But spreading it over three to six months is
a legitimate way to buy your own peace of mind, and the cost of that insurance is small.

*What is never right:* holding cash while "waiting for a better entry point." That's market
timing, it doesn't work reliably for anyone, and the cost of being out of the market during
its best days is severe — a large share of long-run returns come from a handful of days that
nobody identifies in advance.

** 7.7 Account types: the crucial distinction

This trips up everybody, so it gets its own section.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*An account is a container. An investment is what's inside it.*

A 401(k) is not an investment. An IRA is not an investment. They are *tax wrappers* — boxes
with special tax rules. Inside each box you choose actual investments (index funds, bonds).

"I have an IRA" tells me nothing about what you own. People open IRAs, leave the money in a
cash sweep account, and wonder years later why it didn't grow. It didn't grow because they
never bought anything.
#+END_QUOTE

Three tax treatments:

*** Taxable (brokerage) account

- No special tax treatment, no contribution limits, no withdrawal restrictions.
- You owe tax on dividends and interest each year, and on capital gains when you sell.
- Total flexibility. Use for goals before retirement, and for anything after you've filled
  the tax-advantaged accounts.

*** Tax-deferred (Traditional 401(k), Traditional IRA)

- Contribute *pretax* — reduces your taxable income now.
- Grows untaxed.
- Withdrawals in retirement are taxed as ordinary income.
- Penalty for withdrawing before 59½, with exceptions.

*** Tax-free (Roth 401(k), Roth IRA)

- Contribute *after-tax* — no deduction now.
- Grows untaxed.
- *Qualified withdrawals in retirement are completely tax-free.*
- Contributions (not earnings) can be withdrawn any time, tax and penalty free.

Part 8 covers when to use which, and for you specifically the answer is unusually clear.

** 7.8 Capital gains and taxes on investments

*** Capital gains

A *capital gain* is profit from selling an investment for more than you paid. Your purchase
price is the *cost basis*.

- *Short-term* (held one year or less): taxed as ordinary income, at your regular rate.
- *Long-term* (held more than one year): taxed at preferential rates — 0%, 15%, or 20%
  depending on income.

*The 0% bracket is real and remarkable.* A married couple with taxable income below roughly
$97,000 (Appendix B) pays *zero percent* on long-term capital gains. During your low-income
years this is an enormous, underused opportunity — see "tax gain harvesting" below.

*** Dividends

- *Qualified dividends* — taxed at the same favorable long-term rates.
- *Ordinary (non-qualified) dividends* — taxed as regular income.

Most dividends from broad index funds are qualified.

*** Tax-loss harvesting

If an investment is down, you can sell it, realize the loss, and use it to offset gains — or
up to $3,000 per year against ordinary income, carrying forward the rest. Then buy something
similar-but-not-identical to stay invested.

Beware the *wash sale rule*: buy the same or a "substantially identical" security within 30
days before or after and the loss is disallowed. Buying a different total-market fund from a
different provider generally works.

Only relevant in taxable accounts, and only worth the bother once balances are meaningful.

*** Tax gain harvesting — the version that actually applies to you now

The mirror image, and specific to low-income years. If you're in the 0% long-term capital
gains bracket, you can *sell appreciated investments, pay zero tax, and immediately rebuy
them.* This resets your cost basis higher, permanently reducing future taxable gains.

There's no wash-sale problem because you're realizing a *gain*, not a loss.

For a family in kollel with low taxable income and investments in a taxable account, doing
this deliberately each year is free money. It requires knowing your taxable income and
staying under the threshold, which is exactly the kind of thing tax software will show you.

*** Asset location

Once you have both taxable and tax-advantaged accounts, put the *tax-inefficient* things
(bonds, REITs, funds that throw off lots of ordinary income) in tax-advantaged accounts, and
the *tax-efficient* things (broad stock index funds) in taxable accounts. Small optimization,
free to implement, worth knowing the phrase.

** 7.9 Where to open an account

*** The three good brokerages

| Broker   | Strengths                                     |
|----------+-----------------------------------------------|
| Fidelity | Excellent service, good app, zero-fee index funds, strong HSA |
| Vanguard | Originator of index investing, lowest costs, clunky interface |
| Schwab   | Great service, good branches, solid all-round  |

Any of the three is a genuinely fine choice. I'd nudge a beginner toward Fidelity for the
customer service and the fact that everything (brokerage, IRA, HSA, kids' accounts) lives in
one place.

*** What to avoid

- Brokerages that push commissioned products.
- A "financial advisor" at a bank branch who is compensated on what you buy.
- Apps gamified for trading (confetti when you trade is a design choice with a purpose).
- Anything charging over 1% of assets a year for management (§7.14).

*** Opening one, concretely

Fifteen minutes online. You'll need SSN, address, employment info, and bank details for
funding. Then — and this is the step people miss — *actually buy something.* Transferring
money in leaves it sitting in cash. Log in, place a buy order for the fund you chose, and
verify it executed.

** 7.10 A complete beginner's plan

If you want the whole of investing compressed into an actionable sequence:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. Pay off any debt above ~8% interest first.
2. Build a starter emergency fund.
3. Capture any employer 401(k) match — 100% return, do it before anything else.
4. Open a Roth IRA (a spousal Roth IRA for the non-earning spouse — see §8.6).
5. Buy ONE target-date index fund inside it.
6. Automate a monthly contribution, whatever you can manage. $50 counts.
7. Increase it by 1% of income every time either of you gets a raise.
8. Do not look at it more than quarterly.
9. Do not sell when it drops. Especially then.
10. Repeat for thirty years.
#+END_EXAMPLE

That's genuinely it. Everything else in Part 7 is refinement on a plan that is already 90%
of the available benefit.

** 7.11 The psychology, which is the hard part

Investing is simple and not easy. The mechanics take an afternoon. The discipline takes
decades. What you're up against:

- *Loss aversion.* Losses hurt roughly twice as much as equivalent gains feel good. This makes
  people sell at the bottom, which is the only way to convert a temporary decline into a
  permanent loss.
- *Recency bias.* Assuming what just happened will keep happening. It's why people buy after
  a run-up and sell after a crash.
- *Herding.* When everyone is talking about an investment — crypto in 2021, meme stocks, AI
  stocks, whatever is current when you read this — the easy money has already been made and
  you're being offered the exit liquidity.
- *Overconfidence.* Nearly everyone believes they're an above-average investor. Half of them
  are wrong, and the ones who trade most earn least.
- *Action bias.* Doing something feels productive. In investing, doing nothing is usually the
  productive act, which is psychologically intolerable and financially correct.

*The defenses:* automate everything so no decision is required; write down your plan
including what you'll do in a crash; check quarterly rather than daily; and remember that
every crash in history felt, at the time, like the one that wouldn't recover.

** 7.12 A short note on halacha and investing

I'm flagging these so you know they exist and know to ask a rav — I'm not paskening.

- *Ribbis* — investments involving lending to Jewish-owned entities may raise ribbis questions
  (§3.10). Index funds holding thousands of companies are generally not treated as a problem
  by mainstream poskim, but Israeli banks and Jewish-owned financial institutions can raise
  it, and Israeli-listed investments raise it more often.
- *Shabbos* — you don't trade on Shabbos. Whether a fund or a business you own operates on
  Shabbos is a discussed question; for publicly traded companies where you're a passive
  minority holder, the mainstream position is generally lenient, but this is genuinely
  disputed and some are machmir.
- *Kosher/ethical screening* — some avoid particular industries. There are "kosher"
  investment funds; be aware that screened funds typically have higher expense ratios, and
  weigh that against the reason for the screen.
- *Shemitta* — relevant to Israeli agricultural investments and to some Israeli businesses.
- *Chametz on Pesach* — if you own a business holding chametz, it needs to be included in the
  mechiras chametz. A share of a public company is generally not treated this way; a private
  business you actually own is a different question.

*The practical instruction:* a broad low-cost index fund in a US retirement account is not
generally treated as a halachic problem. When you move into private investments, Israeli
institutions, direct lending, or owning a business, ask.

** 7.13 A worked example: your family, twenty-five years out

Numbers to make the abstraction concrete. Assumptions: you finish CS and start earning at
28; combined household income grows from $60K now to $150K by 40; you save 15% of income
from 28 onward; 7% real return.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Age 24–28  Small contributions, ~$150/month into a spousal Roth IRA
             Contributed: $7,200        Balance at 28:    ~$8,000

  Age 28–35  Household income ~$110K, saving 15% = $16,500/year
             Contributed: $115,500      Balance at 35:  ~$156,000

  Age 35–45  Income ~$150K, saving 15% = $22,500/year
             Contributed: $225,000      Balance at 45:  ~$617,000

  Age 45–55  Same rate
             Contributed: $225,000      Balance at 55: ~$1,524,000

  Age 55–65  Same rate
             Contributed: $225,000      Balance at 65: ~$3,309,000
#+END_EXAMPLE

Three observations.

*First*, total contributed across forty-one years is about $798,000. The balance is about
$3.3 million. So roughly 76% of the final number is growth, not saving. You did not earn
that money; time did.

*Second*, look at the last decade. The balance goes from $1.5M to $3.3M — an increase of
$1.8M — while you contributed $225,000. In that decade, growth outpaced your contributions
by roughly eight to one. This is why quitting at 55, or dipping in at 50 for a chasunah, is
so much more expensive than it feels: you are not withdrawing the dollar, you are
withdrawing the dollar *and* everything it was about to become.

*Third*, this is in *real* (inflation-adjusted) dollars, because I used a 7% real return.
$3.3 million here means $3.3 million of today's purchasing power. The nominal number on the
statement would be far larger and would buy the same groceries.

This is not a projection or a promise. Returns vary, income varies, life happens, tuition
for eight children happens, and a decade of poor returns early would change these numbers a
great deal. It's an illustration of the *shape* — and the shape is the point.

** 7.14 Financial advisors: who's actually on your side

*** The words that matter

- *Fiduciary* — legally obligated to act in your best interest. This is the word you want.
- *Suitability standard* — only obligated to recommend something "suitable," which permits
  recommending the suitable option that pays them most. Many people selling financial
  products operate under this weaker standard.

*** How they're paid

| Model              | How it works                        | Verdict                     |
|--------------------+-------------------------------------+-----------------------------|
| Fee-only, hourly   | $200–500/hour, no products sold     | Best for most people        |
| Fee-only, flat     | $1,500–5,000 for a full plan        | Great for a one-time plan   |
| AUM (% of assets)  | ~1%/year of what they manage        | Expensive at scale; that's $10,000/year on $1M |
| Commission         | Paid by the products they sell you  | Conflicted by construction  |
| "Fee-based"        | Deliberately confusing hybrid       | Ask precisely what it means |

*** What you actually need

Right now, at 24 with a simple situation: *nothing*. This document plus a target-date fund
covers you. Genuinely.

Later, a few hours with a *fee-only fiduciary* is worth it at real decision points: buying a
first home, the year your income jumps, a business, an inheritance, an aliyah decision, or
estate planning with several children.

Find one at =napfa.org= (fee-only fiduciaries) or =xyplanningnetwork.com= (which specializes
in younger clients and charges monthly or hourly rather than by assets).

*** The question that sorts everyone

*"Are you a fiduciary at all times, in writing, and are you compensated in any way by the
products you recommend?"*

Ask it exactly like that. A good advisor answers immediately and cleanly. Anyone who
qualifies, deflects, or explains why the question is more complicated than it seems has
already told you the answer.

** 7.15 Windfalls: what to do with money that arrives all at once

At some point money will arrive in a lump: a tax refund, a bonus, a gift from parents, an
inheritance, a legal settlement, the sale of something. Windfalls are where a surprising
amount of financial damage gets done, because a large number arriving suddenly short-circuits
the ordinary habits that keep you sensible.

*** The first rule: do nothing for thirty days

Put it in a high-yield savings account and leave it. Tell nobody outside the household.

Thirty days does three things: it lets the emotional charge dissipate, it gives you time to
find out the tax treatment before you spend money you owe, and it outlasts the window in
which everyone who wants a piece of it will approach you. This is not a personality
observation — it is what happens, reliably, to people who receive money publicly.

*** The second rule: find out what you actually have

Different windfalls have wildly different tax treatment, and the number in the account is not
always the number you keep.

| Windfall                    | Tax treatment                                            |
|-----------------------------+----------------------------------------------------------|
| Tax refund                  | None — it was already your money                         |
| Gift from a person          | *Tax-free to you.* The giver may file a form; you owe nothing |
| Inheritance (cash)          | Generally not taxable income to you                      |
| Inherited investments       | You get a *stepped-up basis* — see below. Big deal        |
| Inherited retirement account| Taxable as you withdraw, with a 10-year rule. Big deal    |
| Work bonus                  | Fully taxable as wages. Withheld at a flat supplemental rate that is often wrong for you |
| Life insurance payout       | Generally income-tax-free                                |
| Legal settlement            | Depends entirely on what it compensates. Ask             |
| Lottery / gambling          | Fully taxable, and you must report it                    |

*** Stepped-up basis, because it's worth real money

If you inherit an investment, your *cost basis* (§7.8) resets to its value on the date of
death, not what the deceased originally paid.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Your grandfather bought stock for $20,000 in 1985.
  It is worth $200,000 when he dies.
  You inherit it and sell it immediately.

  Capital gain = $200,000 − $200,000 (stepped-up basis) = $0
  Tax owed = $0

  Had he sold it the day before he died:
  Capital gain = $180,000, and tax would have been owed.
#+END_EXAMPLE

$180,000 of gain evaporates. This is why elderly people are generally advised *not* to sell
appreciated assets late in life, and why "gift it to me now" is often worse for the family
than inheriting it, since a lifetime gift carries the *original* basis with it.

*** Inherited retirement accounts: the 10-year rule

If you inherit an IRA or 401(k) from someone who isn't your spouse, current law generally
requires you to empty the account within *ten years*. For an inherited *traditional* account,
every dollar withdrawn is ordinary income to you — so a $200,000 inherited IRA is $200,000 of
taxable income spread over a decade, potentially pushing you into higher brackets.

Planning matters here: spreading withdrawals evenly, or front-loading them in a low-income
year (a year in kollel, say) can save five figures. And do *not* cash it out in one year
without running the numbers, which is the reflexive thing people do.

Inherited *Roth* accounts also follow the 10-year rule but come out tax-free. Much better
problem.

*** The allocation

Once you know what you have and what you owe on it, a sane default:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1.  Set aside the tax owed, immediately, in a separate account.
2.  Kill any high-interest debt. All of it.
3.  Fill the emergency fund to its full target.
4.  Max this year's Roth IRAs (both of you) and the HSA.
5.  Fund the sinking funds you've been underfunding.
6.  Then, and only then: allow yourselves a defined amount — say 5%,
    agreed in advance — to simply enjoy. A trip, a piece of furniture,
    something for the house. Name the number before you start spending,
    not after.
7.  Invest the remainder according to the plan you already had.
#+END_EXAMPLE

Step 6 is not a joke and it is not a moral failure. Windfalls that get entirely swallowed by
responsibility produce a specific resentment that shows up later as a much worse purchase.
Take a defined slice deliberately, and the rest stays disciplined.

*** What not to do

- *Don't quit a job* on the strength of it, unless the arithmetic genuinely supports it for
  the rest of your life. It usually doesn't.
- *Don't buy a more expensive house* whose ongoing costs your income can't carry. A down
  payment is not an income.
- *Don't lend it to anyone* until you've decided what you'd do if they never paid it back —
  because you must be willing to treat any family loan as a gift you might not see again.
- *Don't invest it in a private deal* someone brings you within a month of learning you have
  money. See §17.4, and note that affinity fraud finds windfalls with remarkable efficiency.
- *Don't tell people.* Not out of secrecy — out of self-defense. Money you're known to have is
  money you will spend explaining.

* PART 8 — RETIREMENT ACCOUNTS

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* A "401(k)" or an "IRA" is not an investment. It's a *box* you put
investments inside, and the box means you pay less tax. People open one, never buy anything
inside it, and wonder years later why nothing grew.

There are two kinds of box. *Traditional*: skip the tax now, pay it when the money comes out.
*Roth*: pay the tax now, and never pay tax on that money again as long as you live.

Your choice is unusually easy. You're in kollel, so your tax rate right now is about as low
as it will ever be. Pay the small tax now. Use Roth.

Two things to do this year:

1. If your wife's job offers to *match* retirement contributions, put in enough to collect
   every dollar of it. That's an instant 50–100% return. Nothing else in finance comes close,
   and skipping it is the same as declining part of her salary.
2. Even though you don't earn, you can open a full retirement account *in your own name*,
   funded by her income. It's called a spousal IRA. Almost nobody in kollel knows it exists,
   and it is probably the single most valuable item in this document for your household.
#+END_QUOTE

** 8.1 Why "retirement" is the wrong word

For a 24-year-old, "retirement" is an abstraction so distant it barely registers, especially
in a world where many people work as long as they're able and the concept of stopping isn't
culturally central.

Reframe it. These accounts are not really about retiring. They are *the most tax-advantaged
way to own investments that the US government offers*, and their value is the tax treatment,
not the life stage.

Whether you retire at 65, work until 80, or transition to full-time learning at 55 — you want
money that grew for forty years without being taxed along the way. That's what these are.

** 8.2 The alphabet, sorted

| Account         | Who offers it            | Contributor         | Key feature                    |
|-----------------+--------------------------+---------------------+--------------------------------|
| 401(k)          | For-profit employers      | You + employer      | High limits, employer match    |
| 403(b)          | Nonprofits, schools, mosdos | You + employer   | Nearly identical to 401(k)     |
| 457(b)          | Government, some nonprofits | You + employer   | No early withdrawal penalty    |
| Traditional IRA | You, at any broker        | You                 | Pretax, lower limit            |
| Roth IRA        | You, at any broker        | You                 | Tax-free growth. The good one  |
| SEP-IRA         | Self-employed             | You (as employer)   | High limits, simple            |
| SIMPLE IRA      | Small employers           | You + employer      | Lower limits than 401(k)       |
| Solo 401(k)     | Self-employed, no employees | You (both roles)  | Highest limits for freelancers |
| HSA             | You, with an HDHP         | You + employer      | Best tax treatment in the code |

** 8.3 The employer match: the free money

Many employers match retirement contributions. Common formula: "100% of the first 3%, then
50% of the next 2%."

Meaning: on a $60,000 salary, if you contribute 5% ($3,000), the employer adds 4% ($2,400).

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*That is an immediate, guaranteed 80% return on your money. Nothing else in finance comes
close. Not paying off a credit card. Not any investment. Nothing.*

If your wife's employer offers a match and she isn't contributing enough to capture all of
it, she is declining part of her salary. Fix that this week, before you read the rest of this
part.
#+END_QUOTE

*** Vesting

*Vesting* is when the employer's contributions actually become yours.

- *Immediate* — yours right away.
- *Cliff* — 100% yours after, say, three years; nothing before.
- *Graded* — 20% per year over five years.

Your own contributions are always 100% yours immediately. Only the employer's portion vests.
Worth knowing before you leave a job — sometimes staying an extra two months means keeping
several thousand dollars.

** 8.4 Traditional vs Roth: the decision, and why yours is easy

*** The core trade

- *Traditional*: deduct now, pay tax later. Better if your tax rate today is *higher* than it
  will be when you withdraw.
- *Roth*: pay tax now, withdraw tax-free later. Better if your rate today is *lower* than it
  will be later.

That's the entire question: is your tax rate higher now or later?

*** Worked comparison

$6,000 contribution, 30 years, 7% growth, in the 12% bracket now and assume 22% later.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
TRADITIONAL:
  Contribute $6,000 pretax. Tax saved today: $720.
  Grows to $45,700. Withdraw at 22%: keep ~$35,600.

ROTH:
  Contribute $6,000 of after-tax money.
  Grows to $45,700. Withdraw: keep ALL $45,700.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*The fair-comparison footnote, because this example is slightly rigged and you should know
how.* Putting $6,000 into a Roth costs you more gross income than putting $6,000 into a
traditional account — $6,818 of gross versus $6,000, because you paid the 12% tax first. So
the truly apples-to-apples version is: contribute $6,000 to a traditional account *and*
invest the $720 you saved in taxes. Do that, and if your rate never changed, the two come
out exactly equal. That is the actual mathematical identity underneath all of this.

Two things break the tie in the real world, both in Roth's favor for you:

1. *Nobody actually invests the tax savings.* The $720 goes into the grocery budget. In
   practice the Roth quietly forces a larger real contribution.
2. *Your rate is going to change*, and the direction is knowable. That is the whole point of
   the next section.

Roth wins decisively when today's rate is low and tomorrow's is higher.

*** Why this is unusually clear-cut for you

Right now:
- You're in kollel with little or no earned income.
- Your household is in one of the lowest tax brackets, possibly with an effective federal
  rate near zero after credits.
- You may be paying essentially *nothing* in federal income tax.

Later:
- You'll be a software engineer, plausibly earning $120,000–200,000+.
- Your household will be in the 22–24% bracket or higher.
- Tax rates in general may be higher decades from now, given federal deficits — that's a
  guess, but it's a guess in the same direction.

*Contributing to a Roth at a 0–12% tax rate and withdrawing at 24%+ is close to free money.*
You are paying tax at the cheapest rate you will ever face. These low-income years are a
one-time opportunity that closes permanently when you start earning.

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*The single most valuable financial action available to your household in the next three
years is funding Roth accounts while your tax rate is at its lifetime minimum.* Every dollar
you get in there now is a dollar that will never be taxed again, ever.
#+END_QUOTE

*** When Traditional is better

For completeness: once you're earning well (say 24% bracket or above), traditional
contributions usually win, because you're deducting at a high rate and will likely withdraw
at a lower one. Many people do traditional in peak earning years and Roth in low years — and
that's precisely what your life is set up to allow.

There's also a case for hedging — doing some of each — since nobody knows future tax law.
That's reasonable once you're earning. Not now. Now it's Roth.

** 8.5 IRAs in detail

An *IRA* (Individual Retirement Arrangement) is an account you open yourself at a broker,
independent of any employer.

*** Contribution limits

One combined limit across all your IRAs (Appendix B; recently $7,500/person/year, plus a
catch-up at 50+). You and your wife each get your own limit.

*** Traditional IRA deductibility

Anyone with earned income can *contribute*. Whether it's *deductible* depends on income and
whether you're covered by a workplace plan. If you're not covered by one, it's fully
deductible at any income. If you are, deductibility phases out (Appendix B).

*** Roth IRA income limits

You can contribute to a Roth only if income is below a threshold (roughly $240,000 MFJ,
phasing out below that). Above it, use the *backdoor Roth* (§8.7).

*** The earned income requirement, and the exception that matters to you

To contribute to *any* IRA, the household needs *earned income* — wages or self-employment.
Investment income doesn't count. Neither, generally, does a scholarship or stipend that
isn't reported as compensation.

** 8.6 The spousal IRA — read this one carefully

*This is the most directly actionable item in Part 8 for your household.*

Normally you need your own earned income to fund an IRA. The *spousal IRA* is the exception:
if you file jointly and *one* spouse has enough earned income, the *other* spouse can fund a
full IRA in their own name.

Concretely: your wife works. You're in kollel with little or no earned income. As long as her
earned income covers both contributions, *you can fund a full Roth IRA in your own name every
year.*

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Wife's W-2 earned income:        $52,000
  Her Roth IRA contribution:        $7,500
  YOUR spousal Roth IRA:            $7,500
  Total:                           $15,000  (fully allowed — her income covers both)
#+END_EXAMPLE

Requirements: married filing jointly, and combined earned income at least equal to total
contributions. The account is *yours* — your name, your control, your beneficiary
designation. "Spousal" describes only where the qualifying income came from.

*Why this matters so much:* years in kollel do not have to be years of zero retirement
saving. If you can put even $3,000/year into a spousal Roth IRA for four years while
studying, that's $12,000 contributed at age 24–28, which at 7% is roughly *$170,000 at 65* —
tax-free, forever. From money you might otherwise have assumed you couldn't contribute at
all because you weren't earning.

Combine with the Saver's Credit (§4.9) and the government may hand you back 50% of the
contribution. You'd be putting in $2,000, getting $1,000 back, and it grows tax-free for
forty years. That is about as close to a free lunch as the tax code gets.

** 8.7 Backdoor Roth (for later, when you're earning well)

When your income exceeds the Roth limit, there's a legal two-step:

1. Contribute to a *Traditional* IRA (nondeductible — no income limit on contributing).
2. *Convert* it to a Roth IRA, immediately.

Result: money in a Roth despite exceeding the income limit. This is well-established and
routine; the IRS is fully aware of it.

*The trap — the pro-rata rule.* If you hold *any* pretax money in *any* traditional IRA, the
conversion is taxed proportionally across all your IRA balances, not just the new
contribution. So people with an old rolled-over 401(k) sitting in a traditional IRA get an
unexpected tax bill.

*The fix:* roll pretax IRA money into a current employer's 401(k) first (401(k) balances don't
count for the pro-rata calculation), leaving your traditional IRA at zero.

File *Form 8606* every year you do this. Keep those forms forever — they're the proof of your
after-tax basis, and reconstructing them decades later is miserable.

Not relevant to you for years. Filed here so you recognize it when it becomes relevant.

** 8.8 Self-employment retirement plans

Relevant when you do freelance programming, which is a common path while studying.

*** SEP-IRA

- Contribute up to 25% of net self-employment earnings, to a high dollar cap (Appendix B).
- Trivial to open, no annual filing.
- Downside: if you ever have employees, you must contribute the same percentage for them.
- Also: a SEP balance counts against the backdoor Roth pro-rata rule.

*** Solo 401(k)

For self-employed people with no employees (a spouse is allowed). The best option for
meaningful freelance income, because you contribute in *two capacities*:

- As *employee*: up to the standard elective deferral limit (~$24,500), *and it can be Roth*.
- As *employer*: up to 25% of net earnings.
- Combined cap is high (Appendix B).

The employee portion isn't limited to a percentage of earnings, so on a small freelance
income you can shelter a much larger share than a SEP allows. And the Roth option inside a
Solo 401(k) is exactly right for your low-tax-rate years.

Slightly more paperwork (a Form 5500-EZ once assets exceed $250,000). Fidelity, Schwab, and
E*Trade all offer them free.

*** SIMPLE IRA

Used by small employers. Lower limits than a 401(k), mandatory employer contribution. You'd
encounter it as an employee at a small mosad or business, not choose it yourself.

** 8.9 The HSA: the best account in the US tax code

*** Why it's remarkable

Every other account gives you *two* of three possible tax benefits. The HSA gives all three:

1. *Deductible going in* (pretax, and if done via payroll, it also escapes FICA).
2. *Grows tax-free.*
3. *Tax-free coming out* — for qualified medical expenses.

No other account does this. It is, on the arithmetic, the single most tax-efficient dollar
you can save in America.

*** Requirements

You must be covered by a *High-Deductible Health Plan* (HDHP) and have no other disqualifying
coverage. Contribution limits are separate for self-only and family coverage (Appendix B),
with a catch-up at 55+.

*** The stealth retirement strategy

Most people use an HSA as a checking account for medical bills. That works but wastes it.
The optimized version:

1. Max the HSA every year.
2. *Invest* it (most HSAs let you invest above a cash minimum — many people never notice
   this and leave it earning nothing).
3. Pay current medical expenses *out of pocket* from regular savings.
4. *Save every medical receipt, forever.*
5. Let the HSA compound for decades.
6. Decades later, reimburse yourself tax-free for all those old expenses — *there is no
   deadline* for reimbursement, as long as the expense occurred after the HSA was opened.

You've effectively created a Roth IRA with a deduction on the way in.

And after 65, HSA withdrawals for non-medical purposes are taxed like a traditional IRA — no
penalty, just ordinary income. So the worst case is that it behaves like a traditional IRA,
and the best case is it's better than a Roth.

*** The realistic caveat for your family

The HSA requires an HDHP, and a family expecting frequent pregnancies and pediatric care may
be better served by a lower-deductible plan even without HSA access. Run the numbers (§6.3)
rather than chasing the tax benefit into a plan that costs you more in actual medical
spending. The tax tail should not wag the healthcare dog.

Also: you can only contribute while covered by an HDHP, but the money stays yours forever and
can still be spent on medical expenses regardless of future coverage.

** 8.10 Withdrawal rules, penalties, and the exceptions

*** The general rule

Withdraw before 59½ from a traditional retirement account and you owe income tax *plus* a 10%
penalty.

*** Roth IRA is different, and importantly so

Roth IRA *contributions* (not earnings) can be withdrawn any time, for any reason, tax-free
and penalty-free. You already paid tax on them.

Earnings need the account to be 5 years old *and* you to be 59½ (or an exception).

*The practical consequence:* a Roth IRA doubles as a backup emergency fund. If you put in
$20,000 over five years and it grew to $28,000, you can take out the $20,000 at any time with
no tax and no penalty.

I'd rather you never do this — money removed loses its compounding permanently and can't be
put back — but knowing the option exists makes it psychologically much easier to start
contributing when money feels tight. That's a real benefit for a young couple worried about
locking money away.

*** Penalty exceptions worth knowing

Withdrawals avoid the 10% penalty (income tax may still apply) for:

- First-time home purchase — up to $10,000 lifetime, from an IRA.
- Qualified higher education expenses (IRA).
- Unreimbursed medical expenses above a threshold.
- Health insurance premiums while unemployed.
- Birth or adoption — up to $5,000 per child, per parent.
- Disability.
- Substantially equal periodic payments (Rule 72(t)) — a complex early-retirement mechanism.
- Rule of 55 — leaving a job at 55+ allows penalty-free withdrawal from *that employer's*
  401(k) (not an IRA).

*** Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs)

Starting at age 73 (rising to 75 later this decade), you *must* withdraw a minimum amount
annually from traditional accounts, whether you need it or not, and pay tax on it. The
penalty for missing one is severe.

*Roth IRAs have no RMDs during the owner's lifetime.* One more advantage, and a meaningful
estate-planning one: a Roth can be left to grow untouched for your entire life and passed on.

Roth 401(k)s used to have RMDs; that was eliminated starting in 2024.

** 8.11 Rolling over an old 401(k)

When you leave a job you have four options:

1. *Leave it* — fine if the plan has good low-cost funds. Easy to forget about, though.
2. *Roll to the new employer's 401(k)* — consolidates, and keeps the backdoor Roth path clean.
3. *Roll to an IRA* — usually the best investment options and lowest costs. But it creates
   pretax IRA money, which complicates a future backdoor Roth (§8.7).
4. *Cash out* — *don't.* Income tax plus a 10% penalty plus the permanent loss of decades of
   compounding. Roughly a third of people do this when changing jobs and it is one of the
   most destructive routine financial mistakes in America.

*Do a direct (trustee-to-trustee) rollover*, where the money moves between institutions
without passing through your hands. If they send you a check made out to you, 20% is withheld
and you have 60 days to deposit the full amount — including the withheld portion, from your
own pocket — or it counts as a distribution. Just avoid the whole mess: ask for a direct
rollover.

*Track down old accounts.* People genuinely forget 401(k)s from jobs held years ago. The
Department of Labor now runs a Retirement Savings Lost and Found; there's also
=unclaimed.org= for state-held unclaimed property. Worth twenty minutes someday.

** 8.12 Social Security, properly explained

*** How benefits are computed

1. Your earnings for each year are indexed for wage growth.
2. The highest *35 years* are averaged into your *AIME* (Average Indexed Monthly Earnings).
3. A progressive formula converts AIME into your *PIA* (Primary Insurance Amount) — the
   monthly benefit at full retirement age.

The formula heavily favors lower earners: it replaces roughly 90% of the first slice of
average earnings, 32% of the next, and 15% above that. So Social Security is proportionally
much more valuable to modest earners.

*** The 35-year rule and kollel

Fewer than 35 years of earnings means zeros are averaged in. Ten years in kollel with no
reported earnings means ten zeros, which meaningfully depresses the average.

The mitigation: a career from, say, 28 to 68 is forty years — enough to fill all 35 slots
with real earnings. So an early kollel period followed by a full career is largely
self-correcting. It's a much bigger problem for someone who never accumulates 35 earning
years at all.

*** Claiming age

- *62* — earliest, with a permanent reduction of about 25–30%.
- *Full retirement age* — 67 for anyone born 1960 or later.
- *70* — delayed retirement credits add about 8% per year of waiting. Waiting to 70 yields
  roughly 124% of the full benefit.

For a married couple, coordination matters: the higher earner delaying to 70 maximizes the
*survivor* benefit, since the surviving spouse keeps the larger of the two benefits. If your
wife outlives you — statistically likely — your claiming decision sets her income for the rest
of her life. That's the frame to use, rather than "how long will I personally live."

*** Spousal and survivor benefits

- A spouse can receive up to 50% of the other's PIA, if that exceeds their own benefit.
- A surviving spouse can receive up to 100% of the deceased's benefit.
- These apply even to a spouse who never worked. So years at home raising children are not
  a total Social Security void — the spousal benefit is real.
- Divorced spouses can claim on an ex's record if the marriage lasted 10+ years.

*** Will it be there?

The Social Security trust fund is projected to be depleted in the mid-2030s, at which point
incoming payroll taxes would still cover roughly 75–80% of scheduled benefits. So "Social
Security will disappear" is not accurate; "Social Security may be reduced or reformed" is.

Plan for it to exist at a reduced level. Don't plan for it to be your primary income. That's
what Parts 7 and 8 are for.

*** Check your record

=ssa.gov/myaccount= shows every year of reported earnings and projected benefits. Check it
annually. Employers make reporting errors, and correcting one is far easier within about
three years of the fact than twenty years later.
* PART 9 — HOUSING: RENTING, BUYING, AND MORTGAGES

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* "Renting is throwing money away" is nonsense. You're buying somewhere to
live for a year, the same way groceries buy you food.

Owning has enormous costs people forget: property tax, repairs, and mortgage interest.
Together those run roughly 5% of the house's value *every year*, and you never get any of it
back. On a $500,000 house that's about $25,000 a year of pure cost before you've paid off a
dollar of the loan.

Buy when you'll be there seven years or more, and when you have the down payment *and* the
closing costs *and* your emergency fund still untouched afterward.

The bank will approve you for more house than you can carry, because the bank has never heard
of tuition. Do your own math, against your *take-home* pay rather than your salary, and keep
total housing costs under about a quarter of it.

One fact that shocks every first-time buyer: for the first several years of a mortgage,
nearly every dollar you pay is interest. In month one of a $400,000 loan you pay $2,528 and
reduce what you owe by $361.
#+END_QUOTE

** 9.1 Rent vs buy: the honest version

"Renting is throwing money away" is one of the most confidently repeated pieces of nonsense
in circulation. Renting buys you housing for a period of time, the same way buying groceries
buys you food. Nobody says eating is throwing money away.

The real comparison is more subtle and often closer than people assume.

*** What renting actually costs

Rent, renters insurance, utilities. That's it. When the boiler dies, you call the landlord.

*** What owning actually costs

Everyone budgets the mortgage payment. Almost nobody budgets the rest:

| Cost                     | Typical annual amount                        |
|--------------------------+----------------------------------------------|
| Mortgage principal       | Builds equity — genuinely yours              |
| Mortgage interest        | Gone. This is the "throwing away" part       |
| Property tax             | 0.5–2.5% of value/year. In NJ, often 2%+     |
| Homeowners insurance     | $1,200–3,000                                 |
| Maintenance              | Budget 1–2% of value/year. Non-negotiable    |
| Repairs and replacements | Roof, boiler, appliances — lumpy and certain |
| HOA fees, if applicable  | $0–600/month                                 |
| Closing costs, amortized | 2–5% on purchase, 6–8% on sale               |

On a $500,000 house in New Jersey, property tax and maintenance alone can run $17,000–20,000
a year *before* the mortgage. That's $1,500/month of pure cost that builds no equity — the
homeowner's version of "throwing money away," and it's rarely in the mental arithmetic.

*** The 5% rule (a useful rough test)

Annual unrecoverable cost of owning ≈ 5% of the home's value:

- ~1% property tax (varies enormously — use your actual local rate)
- ~1% maintenance
- ~3% cost of capital (mortgage interest, or the return you gave up on the down payment)

So a $500,000 home costs roughly $25,000/year, or about $2,080/month, in money you never get
back. If you can rent a comparable home for less than that, renting is financially better.
If renting costs more, buying is better.

Adjust for your actual property tax rate — in high-tax New Jersey this test tilts toward
renting more than people expect; in low-tax areas it tilts the other way.

*** When renting is genuinely right

- You might move within five years. Closing costs on both ends (roughly 8–10% round trip)
  usually swamp any appreciation over a short period.
- You don't have a down payment plus an emergency fund. Buying with nothing left over is how
  a broken furnace becomes a credit card balance.
- Your income is unstable or about to change (which describes you exactly, right now).
- You're not sure which neighborhood you want to be in for the long term.

*** When buying is genuinely right

- You'll be there 7+ years.
- You have 10–20% down *plus* closing costs *plus* an intact emergency fund.
- Stable income you can document.
- The monthly all-in cost fits comfortably in your budget.
- You want control — renovating, expanding, a sukkah porch, not being asked to leave.

*** The frum-specific factors that don't show up in calculators

1. *You need to live within walking distance of a shul, a mikvah, and schools.* That
   constrains you to a handful of neighborhoods, each with its own price level, and it
   removes "move somewhere cheaper" as a lever in a way most Americans don't experience.
2. *Family size means you need more house* than a typical American family of the same
   income. Four bedrooms is a floor, not an aspiration.
3. *Hosting.* Shabbos guests, sheva brachos, a bar mitzvah. Space has a social function.
4. *Community stability matters more.* Moving away from your kehilla has costs that don't
   appear on a spreadsheet — schools, chavrusas, your wife's support network, your children's
   friends.
5. *Prices in established frum neighborhoods are high and sticky*, because demand is
   constrained by the same walking-distance requirement. This is real and it means the "just
   buy in a cheaper area" advice often doesn't apply.

** 9.2 How much house you can actually afford

*** The lender's rules

- *Front-end ratio*: housing costs ≤ 28% of *gross* monthly income.
- *Back-end ratio (DTI)*: all debt payments ≤ 36–43% of gross monthly income (some programs
  allow up to 50%).

*DTI* — Debt-to-Income ratio. Total monthly debt payments divided by gross monthly income.
The single most important number in mortgage qualification.

*** Why the lender's number is too high for you

Lenders compute against *gross* income and know nothing about tuition. For a frum family
paying $15,000–40,000+ a year in tuition, the lender's maximum is dangerously optimistic —
tuition functions like a second mortgage that no underwriter sees.

*My suggested rule:* total housing cost (mortgage + taxes + insurance + maintenance reserve)
should be *≤ 25% of net income*, and you should separately confirm that housing plus tuition
plus everything else leaves room to save.

*** A worked example

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Household gross income:         $120,000/year  = $10,000/month
  Net (after tax, ~24% all-in):                    ~$7,600/month

  LENDER SAYS:  28% of gross = $2,800/month for housing
  I SAY:        25% of net   = $1,900/month for housing

  What $1,900/month of all-in housing buys in NJ at 6.5%:
    Property tax ($500K house at 2%):    $833/month
    Insurance:                           $150/month
    Maintenance reserve (1%):            $415/month
    → Only ~$500/month left for the mortgage payment itself.

  That is not enough to buy a $500K house.
#+END_EXAMPLE

That example is deliberately uncomfortable, and it explains something real: in high-tax,
high-price frum neighborhoods, a household earning six figures often genuinely cannot afford
the house their neighbors live in. Those neighbors usually have one of: a much higher income,
substantial family help, a home bought fifteen years ago, or financial stress they don't
discuss at kiddush.

*Do not calibrate your housing decision against what people around you appear to afford.* You
have no idea what's behind it.

** 9.3 The down payment and closing costs

*** Down payment

The classic 20% has a specific purpose: below 20%, lenders require *PMI*.

*PMI* — Private Mortgage Insurance. Insurance protecting the *lender* if you default. You pay
it; you get nothing from it. Typically 0.3–1.5% of the loan annually.

On a $400,000 loan, PMI at 0.7% is $2,800/year — about $233/month, for nothing.

*Getting rid of PMI:* it automatically terminates when you reach 78% loan-to-value by the
original schedule, and you can *request* removal at 80%. Requesting is faster than waiting;
lenders do not volunteer this. If values rose, an appraisal (a few hundred dollars) can get
you there early and pay for itself in a few months.

*** Low-down-payment options

| Program            | Down payment | Notes                                         |
|--------------------+--------------+-----------------------------------------------|
| Conventional 97    | 3%           | Requires good credit. PMI until 20% equity    |
| FHA                | 3.5%         | Easier credit standards; mortgage insurance for the life of the loan in most cases |
| VA                 | 0%           | Veterans only. No mortgage insurance          |
| USDA               | 0%           | Rural areas only                              |
| State/local FTHB   | Varies       | Down payment assistance, grants, cheaper rates |

*First-time homebuyer programs* are worth investigating seriously — most states have them,
"first-time" usually means "hasn't owned in three years," and income limits are often
generous. Start at your state's Housing Finance Agency website.

*** Closing costs

2–5% of the purchase price, due at closing, *on top of* the down payment. Includes:

- Loan origination fee
- Appraisal ($500–800)
- Home inspection ($400–900) — never skip this
- Title search and title insurance
- Attorney fees (required in NY and NJ)
- Recording fees, transfer taxes
- *Prepaid* escrow: several months of property tax and insurance up front
- Prepaid interest to the end of the month

On a $500,000 purchase, budget $15,000–25,000 in closing costs. This is the number that
surprises first-time buyers into either scrambling or backing out.

*** Don't forget the after-costs

Moving, appliances, a washer and dryer, window treatments, immediate repairs the inspection
found, furniture for rooms you didn't previously have. Budget several thousand dollars, and
*do not let the purchase consume your emergency fund.* A house with no cash reserve is a
machine for generating credit card debt.

** 9.4 Mortgages: how they actually work

*** The four parts of a payment (PITI)

- *P*rincipal — reduces the loan balance. This is yours.
- *I*nterest — the cost of borrowing. Gone.
- *T*axes — property tax, collected monthly and held in escrow.
- *I*nsurance — homeowners, same.

Plus PMI if under 20% down, plus HOA if applicable.

*Escrow* — an account the lender holds where your tax and insurance money accumulates, so
they can pay those bills for you. Your payment changes annually as taxes and insurance
change, which surprises people every year. An *escrow analysis* arrives annually explaining
it; read it, because escrow shortages generate a lump-sum demand plus a higher monthly
payment.

*** Amortization: where the money actually goes

*Amortization* is the schedule by which a loan is paid off. The payment is level, but its
composition shifts dramatically over time.

$400,000 at 6.5% for 30 years — payment $2,528/month:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  MONTH 1:      Interest $2,167   Principal   $361
  YEAR 5:       Interest $2,022   Principal   $506
  YEAR 10:      Interest $1,806   Principal   $722
  YEAR 15:      Interest $1,508   Principal $1,020
  YEAR 20:      Interest $1,096   Principal $1,432
  YEAR 25:      Interest   $528   Principal $2,000
  YEAR 30:      Interest    $14   Principal $2,514

  Total paid over 30 years:  $910,000
  Of which interest:         $510,000
#+END_EXAMPLE

Look at month one: $2,167 of a $2,528 payment is interest. You paid $2,528 and reduced the
debt by $361. Early mortgage payments are almost entirely interest, which is why five years
in, people are startled by how little they've paid down.

*** The power of extra principal payments

Because early payments are so interest-heavy, extra principal early is enormously effective:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Same loan, but add $200/month to principal from day one:
    Paid off in:        24 years instead of 30
    Interest saved:     roughly $112,000
#+END_EXAMPLE

$200/month saves $112,000 and six years of payments. Write "PRINCIPAL ONLY" on any extra
payment and confirm the servicer applied it correctly — some default to holding it toward
next month's payment instead, which does nothing for you.

*Counterargument worth taking seriously:* at a low mortgage rate, investing that $200 instead
may earn more than the interest saved. At 6.5%, it's roughly a wash against a 7% expected
return, and paying down debt is guaranteed while investment returns aren't. At 3%, investing
clearly wins. Reasonable people land differently, and the peace-of-mind value of a paid-off
house is not irrational.

*** Fixed vs adjustable

- *Fixed-rate*: the rate never changes. Predictable forever. Almost always the right choice.
- *ARM (Adjustable-Rate Mortgage)*: fixed for an initial period (5/1 ARM = 5 years), then
  adjusts annually with market rates. Lower initial rate, real risk after.

An ARM is defensible only if you're certain you'll sell or refinance before the adjustment.
Most people are not certain, and 2008 was substantially a story about people who were wrong
about this.

*** Term length

| Term     | Payment  | Total interest on $400K at 6.5% |
|----------+----------+---------------------------------|
| 30 years | $2,528   | ~$510,000                       |
| 15 years | $3,484   | ~$227,000                       |

The 15-year saves roughly $283,000. But the payment is $956 higher, which is a real
constraint for a family with tuition.

*A middle path:* take the 30-year for the flexibility of a lower required payment, and
voluntarily pay it like a 15-year when you can. You get most of the savings and keep the
option to fall back in a hard year. For a household with lumpy expenses and a growing family,
that optionality is worth a lot.

*** Points

*Discount points* are prepaid interest — pay 1% of the loan up front to reduce the rate by
roughly 0.25%.

Compute the break-even: cost of points divided by monthly savings equals months to break
even. If it's 60 months and you'll be there twenty years, buying points is good. If you might
move or refinance in four years, it isn't.

*** Getting a mortgage: the sequence

1. *Check your credit* six months out. Fix errors. Don't open new accounts.
2. *Get pre-approved* — a lender reviews income, assets, and credit and states what they'll
   lend. Stronger than a "pre-qualification," which is a guess.
3. *Shop at least three lenders* — a bank, a credit union, and a mortgage broker. Rates vary
   by half a point or more between lenders on the same day. Do it within a two-week window so
   the credit inquiries count as one (§3.2).
4. *Compare Loan Estimates.* Every lender must give you this standardized three-page form.
   Compare the *APR* (which includes fees) and the Total Closing Costs box side by side —
   the form exists precisely so you can compare apples to apples.
5. *Lock the rate* once you're under contract.
6. *Change nothing* between approval and closing. No new credit cards, no car loan, no job
   change, no large unexplained deposits. Lenders re-verify right before closing and deals
   die at this stage routinely.

*** During the process

- *Appraisal* — the lender's valuation. If it comes in below the purchase price, you must
  renegotiate or make up the difference in cash.
- *Inspection* — *your* protection, and separate from the appraisal. Hire your own inspector.
  Attend the inspection. Ask questions. The $600 is the best money in the whole transaction.
- *Title insurance* — protects against defects in ownership history. Lender's policy is
  required; an owner's policy is optional and generally worth it.
- *Final walkthrough* — the day before closing. Confirm nothing broke and agreed repairs were
  made.
- *Closing* — signing an alarming stack of paper. In NY and NJ, with attorneys. Bring a
  cashier's check or wire the funds (re-read the wire fraud warning in §2.5, because this is
  the exact moment it happens).

** 9.5 Refinancing

*Refinancing* means replacing your mortgage with a new one, usually at a lower rate.

*Rule of thumb:* worth considering if you can cut the rate by 0.75–1% or more and you'll stay
long enough to recover the closing costs (2–3% of the loan).

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Refinance costs:      $8,000
  Monthly savings:        $250
  Break-even:           32 months

  Staying more than ~3 years? Worth it.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Cash-out refinance* — borrowing more than you owe and taking the difference in cash. Legitimate
for a value-adding renovation. Dangerous for consumption: it converts unsecured debt into
debt secured by your house, meaning a missed payment can now cost you the home. Think very
