hard before doing this to pay off credit cards, however good the arithmetic looks.

*Don't restart the clock carelessly.* Refinancing a 30-year loan into a new 30-year loan after
eight years means paying interest for 38 years total. Refinance into a 15- or 20-year term if
the payment allows.

** 9.6 The house as an investment (a corrective)

People say "a house is the best investment you'll make." The data is more measured.

- Long-run US home price appreciation, adjusted for inflation, is roughly *0–1% per year*.
  Homes track inflation; they don't beat it by much. (Certain metros and certain decades
  differ dramatically, in both directions.)
- Stocks return roughly 7% real.

So why does buying a house build wealth for so many people? Three reasons, and none of them
is appreciation:

1. *Leverage.* You control a $500,000 asset with $50,000 down. Modest appreciation on the
   whole asset is a large return on your cash — and the same leverage works in reverse, which
   is what 2008 taught.
2. *Forced savings.* The principal portion of every payment is savings you cannot skip. For
   most households this is the only large-scale saving they actually do.
3. *Imputed rent.* Once it's paid off you live rent-free, which is an enormous and invisible
   return, especially in retirement.

The honest conclusion: buying a home you'll live in for decades is usually a good financial
decision *for these reasons*, not because houses are magical growth assets. And a house you
buy at the edge of affordability, in a market you may leave in four years, may not be a good
decision at all.

** 9.7 Renting well

Since you're renting now and probably will for several years:

- *Read the lease.* All of it. Note the notice period for non-renewal, the rules on subletting,
  and what counts as normal wear and tear.
- *Document the condition on move-in* with photos and video, timestamped, and email a copy to
  the landlord. This is how you get the security deposit back.
- *Know your security deposit rights.* Most states cap the amount and require return within a
  set period (often 14–30 days) with an itemized list of deductions. NY and NJ both have
  specific rules; NJ requires the deposit be held in an interest-bearing account.
- *Get renters insurance* (§6.6). $15/month.
- *Never pay rent in cash* without a receipt. Ideally pay by check or electronic transfer so
  there's a record.
- *Negotiate.* Renewal rents are more negotiable than people think, especially if you've paid
  on time and the landlord would rather not turn the unit over.
- *Ask about a longer lease* for rate stability if you plan to stay.

** 9.8 Buying with family help

Very common in the frum world, and worth doing properly rather than informally.

*** Gift for a down payment

Parents can gift you money for a down payment. Lenders require a *gift letter* stating it's a
gift and not a loan (because a loan would change your DTI). The funds usually need to be
*seasoned* — sitting in your account for 60+ days — or fully documented.

Gift tax: each person can give each recipient a substantial amount annually with no tax and
no filing (Appendix B). Above that the *giver* files Form 709, and still owes no tax until
lifetime gifts exceed a very large exemption. So four gifts — from each parent to each of you
— multiply the annual exclusion by four. Practically, your parents can help you buy a house
without a tax problem.

*** Parents on the mortgage or the deed

Sometimes parents co-sign or hold partial ownership. This has real consequences:

- A co-signer is *fully liable* for the debt. It affects their credit and their own borrowing
  capacity.
- Joint ownership complicates a future sale, a future refinance, and eventually inheritance.
- It can create gift-tax and capital-gains-basis issues.

Get it in writing, with a lawyer, before it happens. Family real estate arrangements that
were "obvious" at the time are a reliable source of litigation twenty years later.

*** An intra-family loan

Parents lending you money at a low rate is legal, but the IRS requires a minimum interest
rate (the *Applicable Federal Rate*) or it may treat the foregone interest as a gift. And
between Jews, an interest-bearing loan raises ribbis questions (§3.10) — this is a case where
a heter iska is commonly used. Ask a rav *and* a tax professional before structuring it.

* PART 10 — EARNING: CAREER, SALARY, AND THE TRANSITION AHEAD

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* You can only cut spending so far — maybe 20% before it starts costing you
things that actually matter. Income has no ceiling.

For your family, finishing computer science and getting a real software job is worth more
than every budgeting technique in this document put together. That's not a figure of speech.
It's the arithmetic of paying tuition for a large family.

When an offer comes: never say a number first. Negotiate *after* they've said they want you,
which is the only moment you hold any leverage. And compare the whole package — health
insurance, retirement match, time off, parental leave — not just the salary. Those swing the
real value of a job by 20–30%, and the "lower" offer often wins once you do the arithmetic.

When the money finally arrives, don't upgrade your life for six months. Taxes appear and
government benefits disappear at the same moment, so a big raise will feel like half a raise
at first. Know that in advance, so it's a prediction instead of a disappointment.
#+END_QUOTE

** 10.1 The uncomfortable truth about frugality

Everything in Parts 1–9 optimizes what you do with the money you have. There's a ceiling on
that. You can cut expenses by maybe 20% before it starts costing you quality of life, health,
or the ability to raise children the way you want to.

Income has no ceiling.

For a household in your situation, the single highest-leverage financial action over the next
five years is not budgeting harder. It is *raising the household income*, which for you means
completing the CS path and entering a well-paid field.

I'm saying this plainly because the personal finance genre tends to obsess over lattes and
under-discuss earning. For a family that will support many children through decades of
tuition, earning is the main event.

** 10.2 Software as a parnassah: an honest assessment

*** Why it fits your situation well

- *Remote and flexible work is normal*, which accommodates a schedule with sedarim, early
  Friday finishes, and yom tov.
- *Credentials matter less than demonstrable skill* in this field than in almost any other
  well-paid profession. Nobody asks a working engineer where they went to school after the
  first job.
- *Salaries are high* relative to the training time.
- *No licensure, no residency, no bar exam.*
- *Yom tov is manageable* — a two-day yom tov is a scheduling conversation, not a career
  problem, at most companies.

*** Honest about the current market

The 2021-era market where a bootcamp graduate got three offers is gone. The market as of the
mid-2020s is meaningfully harder for entry-level candidates: fewer junior openings, more
competition, more scrutiny, and AI tooling has raised the bar for what "junior" means.

That doesn't make it a bad path. It makes it a path that requires *actually being good*
rather than merely credentialed. Plan for that: build things, finish them, ship them
publicly.

*** Rough compensation ranges (US, mid-2020s, vary hugely by location and company)

| Level                   | Typical total compensation |
|-------------------------+----------------------------|
| Junior / entry          | $70,000–110,000            |
| Mid-level (3–5 yrs)     | $110,000–160,000           |
| Senior (5–10 yrs)       | $150,000–220,000           |
| Staff / principal       | $200,000–350,000+          |
| Big tech, senior        | $300,000–500,000+          |

Non-tech-company jobs (a bank, a hospital system, an insurance company, a frum-owned
business) pay less but are frequently more stable, more predictable in hours, and more
flexible about religious observance. That's a legitimate trade and not a lesser choice.

*** Adjacent paths worth knowing about

If pure software engineering doesn't land, the adjacent roles pay well and use overlapping
skills: data analysis, business intelligence, DevOps/platform, QA automation, technical
product management, IT infrastructure, cybersecurity, and technical sales engineering.
Several of these have shorter ramps.

** 10.3 Getting the first job

The first job is the hard one. After that, experience does the talking.

*** What actually gets you hired

1. *Projects you built that work and are visible.* Not tutorials — things you designed and
   finished. Deploy them. Put them on GitHub. Write a README that explains what and why.
2. *Contributing to open source.* Even small fixes. It demonstrates you can read someone
   else's codebase, which is 80% of the job.
3. *A referral.* By an enormous margin the highest-yield channel. Which means networking,
   which in your world has an unusual advantage: the frum community is densely connected and
   there are many frum people in tech who will genuinely take a call from a yungerman who
   asks well. Ask.
4. *Interview preparation.* Technical interviews are their own skill, largely separate from
   the job. Practice deliberately — data structures, algorithms, system design basics — and
   practice out loud, because the format is verbal.
5. *Internships*, if the schedule permits. The single most reliable conversion path to a
   full-time offer.

*** Frum-specific organizations

There are organizations specifically bridging the frum community into professional careers —
career training programs, placement services, and networking groups. In the New York area
several exist and have real placement records. Ask around; a name from someone who used one
is worth more than a search result.

** 10.4 Negotiating salary

Most people accept the first number. That is expensive — the starting salary compounds
through every future raise, and the gap never closes.

*** The rules

1. *Never state a number first.* "I'd like to understand the range budgeted for this role."
   Many states now require employers to post ranges; know your local law.
2. *If pressed, give a researched range* and anchor high: "Based on what I've seen for this
   role in this market, I'm targeting $95,000–110,000."
3. *Negotiate after the offer, not before.* You have the most leverage in the window between
   "we want you" and "you've accepted."
4. *Ask for more than salary.* Signing bonus, start date, remote flexibility, extra PTO,
   professional development budget, an earlier review date. A company that can't move on base
   often can move on these.
5. *Get it in writing* before resigning anywhere.
6. *"I'm excited about this role. Is there flexibility on the base?"* — then be quiet. The
   silence does more work than anything you could add.

*** Research the number

- levels.fyi — the best source for tech compensation, by company and level
- Glassdoor, Blind, LinkedIn Salary
- Ask people. Frum professional networks are often quite forthcoming about numbers.

*** A note on yom tov and Shabbos in negotiation

Raise it, matter-of-factly, once the offer is in hand rather than in the first screening.
"I observe the Jewish sabbath and holidays, so I don't work Friday afternoons or a set list of
days each year — here's the list, and I'm happy to shift hours to cover." Most employers are
fine with it and legally obligated to reasonably accommodate it under Title VII. Framing it as
a solved logistics question rather than an apology sets the tone for the whole tenure.

** 10.5 The benefits package: what it's actually worth

Comparing job offers by salary alone is a mistake. Benefits routinely swing total value by
20–30%.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  OFFER A: $95,000 salary
    Health insurance: employee pays $600/month for family    −$7,200
    401(k) match: none                                            $0
    PTO: 10 days
    ----
    Effective value: ~$87,800

  OFFER B: $88,000 salary
    Health insurance: employer pays 90% — you pay $120/mo    −$1,440
    401(k) match: 5% fully matched                           +$4,400
    PTO: 20 days + floating holidays for religious observance
    HSA contribution from employer                           +$1,000
    Tuition reimbursement $5,000/year                        +$5,000
    ----
    Effective value: ~$96,960
#+END_EXAMPLE

The "lower" offer is worth roughly $9,000 more. This arithmetic is worth doing every single
time, on paper.

*** What to evaluate

- Health insurance: premium, deductible, out-of-pocket max, network (§6.3)
- 401(k) match and vesting schedule (§8.3)
- HSA/FSA availability and any employer contribution
- PTO, sick leave, and whether unused days roll over
- *Parental leave* — hugely relevant to you. Ranges from zero to six months paid
- Life and disability insurance provided
- Tuition reimbursement — real money if you're still studying
- Flexibility: remote, hybrid, hours
- Religious accommodation in practice, not just policy

** 10.6 Raises, promotions, and job changes

*** The blunt fact

Staying in a job typically yields 3–5% annual raises. Changing jobs typically yields 10–20%.

Over a career, people who change every 3–4 years generally out-earn people who stay put by a
large margin. This isn't disloyalty; it's how the market prices experience. Loyalty is
usually repaid with a cost-of-living adjustment.

That said: job-hopping every twelve months reads badly, and a good manager, a flexible
schedule, and colleagues who understand why you leave early on Friday have real value that
doesn't appear in a salary comparison. Weigh it honestly rather than romantically in either
direction.

*** Asking for a raise

1. Keep a running document of what you accomplished, with numbers. Start it now, not the week
   before the review.
2. Research market rate for your role and level.
3. Ask for a specific number, framed around value delivered and market rate.
4. Time it well — after a visible win, during budget planning, at review time.
5. If refused, ask what specifically would justify it and by when. Get it concrete. Then
   either they deliver or you have your answer.

** 10.7 Side income

Real options while studying:

- *Freelance development* — Upwork and similar to start, then referrals. The first few
  projects are underpaid; that's tuition.
- *Tutoring* — technical or limudei kodesh. Reliable in frum communities.
- *Web development for local businesses* — many frum-owned businesses have genuinely bad
  websites and real budgets.
- *Technical writing* — undersupplied and pays decently.

Remember: side income is generally self-employment income (§4.11). Set aside 25–30% for
taxes from the first dollar, and check its effect on any benefits eligibility (Part 5) before
scaling it up.

** 10.8 The transition year: a warning and a plan

The year you go from kollel-plus-one-income to two real incomes will be financially strange.
Plan for it rather than experiencing it.

*What happens simultaneously:*

- Income rises sharply.
- Federal and state income tax appears where there was little or none.
- FICA appears on your earnings.
- Refundable credits (EITC, part of the CTC) shrink or vanish.
- Medicaid/CHIP eligibility likely ends; you're buying insurance.
- WIC, SNAP, childcare subsidies end.
- Tuition scholarship gets recalculated, probably upward — often with a lag, so it lands the
  *following* year, after you've adjusted your spending.

*The net effect:* a household income increase of, say, $80,000 might improve actual spendable
resources by $35,000–45,000. Still excellent — but roughly half of what the number suggests.

*The plan:*

1. Model it before it happens. Write the numbers down.
2. Do not raise your lifestyle for at least six months after the income arrives. Bank the
   difference and let the true picture emerge.
3. Fix withholding immediately (§4.4) so April isn't a disaster.
4. Report income changes to benefit programs promptly (§5.3).
5. Expect the tuition recalculation and save for it in advance.
6. *Then* decide what to do with the surplus — and do it deliberately: emergency fund to full
   size, retirement contributions up, debt gone, and only then lifestyle.

*** Lifestyle creep, named

*Lifestyle creep* is spending rising to consume every income increase, so that a person
earning $200,000 feels exactly as squeezed as they did at $80,000. It is the single most
common reason high earners have no money.

The defense is mechanical: when income rises, *immediately* increase automatic savings by a
fixed share of the raise — say half — before the money ever touches the spending account. You
never feel the loss of money you never saw, and the other half still makes life better.
** 10.9 Equity compensation: RSUs, options, and ESPP

If you end up at a tech company of any size, part of your pay will not be cash. This section
exists because equity compensation is genuinely confusing, the tax treatment is where people
get hurt, and nobody explains it before your first grant letter arrives.

*** RSUs (Restricted Stock Units)

The most common form at established companies. The company promises you shares that *vest*
(become yours) over time — typically 25% per year over four years, or quarterly after a
one-year *cliff*.

*The tax rule, which is the whole thing:*

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*When RSUs vest, their full market value is ordinary income to you, taxed exactly like
salary, whether or not you sell.* It appears on your W-2. Your employer usually withholds by
selling some shares ("sell to cover").
#+END_QUOTE

Two consequences people learn the hard way:

1. *Default withholding is often too low.* Many employers withhold at the flat 22%
   supplemental rate. If you're in the 24% or 32% bracket, you are under-withheld on a large
   sum and will owe in April. Check this the first year and adjust your W-4 (§4.4).
2. *After vesting, you own stock with a cost basis equal to the vest-day price.* If you hold
   and it rises, that further gain is a capital gain. If it falls, you have a capital loss —
   *but you already paid ordinary income tax on the higher vest-day value.* People have paid
   tax on stock worth $80,000 that was worth $20,000 by the time they sold.

*The default advice, and it's good advice:* **sell RSUs as they vest.** Immediately, or close
to it. Reasoning:

- There is no tax advantage to holding — you already paid ordinary income tax at vest, and
  holding just adds capital gains exposure.
- Ask yourself: "If the company handed me this as a cash bonus, would I use all of it to buy
  this one stock?" Almost certainly not. Holding vested RSUs *is* that decision, made by
  default.
- *Concentration risk is the real issue.* Your salary, your health insurance, your career
  trajectory, and your investment portfolio would all depend on one company. That is one
  event away from a very bad year. Diversify (§7.5).

*** Stock options

The right to *buy* shares at a fixed price (the *strike price* or *exercise price*). Valuable
only if the stock is worth more than the strike. Common at startups.

- *ISOs (Incentive Stock Options)* — potentially favorable tax treatment, but exercising can
  trigger *AMT* (Alternative Minimum Tax), a parallel tax system that catches people who
  exercise a lot of options in one year. This is a genuine trap that has bankrupted people
  who exercised options in a company whose stock then collapsed — they owed AMT on paper
  gains that evaporated.
- *NSOs (Non-qualified Stock Options)* — the spread between strike and market value at
  exercise is ordinary income. Simpler, less favorable.

*Startup equity, said honestly:* most startup equity is worth zero. Not "less than hoped" —
zero. Value it accordingly when comparing offers, and never accept a materially lower salary
for equity unless you can genuinely afford for it to be worthless. If you take options,
understand your *exercise window* after leaving — traditionally 90 days, which can force you
to spend real money on an uncertain bet at the worst moment.

*** ESPP (Employee Stock Purchase Plan)

A payroll deduction plan letting you buy company stock at a discount, commonly 15%, often
with a *lookback* that applies the discount to the lower of the price at the start or end of
the period.

*This one is usually genuinely good.* A 15% discount on a guaranteed purchase is roughly an
18% immediate return if you sell right away, and the lookback can make it much more.

The standard play: contribute the maximum you can afford, sell immediately at purchase, take
the gain, and diversify it. The discount is taxed as ordinary income; further gain is capital
gain. Holding for favorable tax treatment ("qualifying disposition") requires two years from
the offering date and one from purchase, and generally isn't worth the concentration risk.

*Check for a blackout or holding requirement* before planning to sell immediately — some plans
impose one.

*** Reading an offer that includes equity

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  "$140,000 base + $200,000 RSUs vesting over 4 years"

  Annualized:  $140,000 + $50,000 = $190,000/year
  BUT:
    - The $200,000 is valued at TODAY's stock price. It can halve.
    - Nothing vests before the one-year cliff. Leave at 11 months, get nothing.
    - Future grants ("refreshers") are not guaranteed unless stated.
    - After year 4, if refreshers are small, your comp can DROP.
#+END_EXAMPLE

Ask three questions: what is the vesting schedule, is there a cliff, and what has the refresh
grant looked like historically for someone at my level? The third question is the one people
forget, and it determines your pay in years five and after.

*** A halachic footnote

Owning shares in a company that operates on Shabbos is the same question raised in §7.12, and
mainstream poskim generally treat a passive minority shareholder leniently. Employer stock is
a slightly different case in that you're an employee too. If it troubles you, ask. And if
you're ever offered equity in a *privately held* Jewish-owned business, that's a genuinely
different question — partnership, ribbis, and shabbos issues can all arise, and it's a real
"ask a rav before signing" situation.

* PART 11 — MARRIAGE, CHILDREN, AND MONEY

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Money fights in a marriage are almost never really about money. They're two
people who grew up in different houses with different habits, arguing without either of them
naming what's actually happening.

Fix it with structure instead of willpower: talk about money for twenty minutes once a month
at a set time; agree on a dollar amount above which you check with each other before
spending; and treat all income as household income no matter who earned it. The moment money
becomes a scoreboard, everything else in this document stops working.

Children cost far less than the frightening headline numbers, and each additional child costs
much less than the first. The genuinely large line is childcare.

If a baby may be coming: sign up for short-term disability insurance *before* you're pregnant
— you cannot add it afterward — and find out whether your state pays for parental leave. New
York and New Jersey both do, and you, as the father, can probably claim it too. Almost nobody
does.
#+END_QUOTE

** 11.1 Money and shalom bayis

Money is consistently among the top reported sources of marital conflict. Not because of the
amounts — couples with plenty fight about it too — but because money is where values,
anxieties, and family-of-origin habits collide without anyone naming what's actually being
discussed.

You've been married a year. This is exactly the right moment to build the habits, before
patterns calcify.

*** The conversation to have once, properly

Set aside an hour, not on a Motzei Shabbos when you're both exhausted. Questions:

1. What did money look like in your parents' home? Was it discussed? Tight? Anxious? Freely
   spent? Silent? — This is the most revealing question on the list, and almost nobody asks
   it. Most money conflicts in a young marriage are two different childhoods arguing.
2. What are we each afraid of, financially?
3. What do we each want in ten years? Twenty?
4. What's a purchase we'd each make without asking the other? What's the threshold above
   which we always consult?
5. How do we want to handle helping family? Both directions?
6. What does "enough" look like?

*** The threshold rule

Agree on a number — say $200, or $500 — above which neither of you spends without checking
with the other.

This isn't about permission or control. It's about eliminating the specific, corrosive
experience of discovering a purchase after the fact and feeling blindsided. Below the
threshold, complete autonomy, no questions, no explaining. Above it, a conversation.

Pick a number that gives each of you real freedom. A threshold so low it makes an adult ask
permission for a sefer will breed resentment faster than any overspending.

*** Joint, separate, or both?

- *Fully joint* — everything shared. Simplest, most common, and the frum-world default. Works
  well when both partners feel equal ownership regardless of who earns.
- *Fully separate* — each keeps their own, splits bills. Rare and generally creates more
  friction than it solves in a family with one primary earner.
- *Yours, mine, ours* — joint account for shared expenses, plus a modest personal allowance
  each, unquestioned.

That third option is worth considering seriously, especially where one spouse earns and the
other doesn't. A non-earning spouse having money that is unambiguously theirs — to spend on a
gift, a sefer, a coffee, a thing they don't have to justify — removes a quiet indignity that
does real damage over years.

*** Money is not scorekeeping

In a household where one spouse earns wages and the other is in kollel and raising children,
the income is *household* income. The learning and the childcare are contributions of value.
The moment money becomes leverage — "I earn it" — something has broken that is much harder to
repair than a budget.

This isn't sentiment; it's the practical prerequisite for both of you being honest about
money, which is the prerequisite for every technique in this document working at all.

** 11.2 What a child actually costs

*** The scary headline number

USDA estimates put the cost of raising one child to age 18 at roughly $300,000 in a
middle-income family, excluding college. Frum families face higher costs in some categories
(tuition, food, larger housing) and lower in others (per-child costs fall substantially with
family size).

Don't let the number frighten you into inaction or into fewer children. It's an aggregate
across eighteen years. What matters is the monthly, and the monthly is manageable when
planned.

*** The real per-child marginal cost

The *second* child costs much less than the first. The *sixth* costs much less than the
second. Economies of scale in a large family are genuine:

- Clothing is handed down.
- Baby gear is bought once — crib, stroller, car seat (check expiration dates), high chair.
- Food scales sub-linearly; you're already cooking.
- Housing is a step function, not per-child — you need a bigger house at some point, not with
  each child.
- Older children help with younger ones.

The costs that *don't* scale down: tuition (usually per-child, though most schools offer
multi-child discounts), medical, camp, and the big one-time events — bar/bas mitzvah,
chasunah.

*** First-year costs, concretely

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Birth (with insurance, hitting deductible):   $1,500–5,000
  Bris                                            $500–1,500
  Crib, mattress, stroller, car seat            $500–1,500 (much less used or gifted)
  Diapers and wipes, year one                   $600–900
  Formula, if used                              $1,200–2,400
  Clothing, year one                            $300–600
  Baby items, gear, misc                        $300–800
  Increased laundry, utilities                  $200–400
  Childcare, if the mother works       THE BIG ONE: $8,000–20,000+/year
#+END_EXAMPLE

Childcare dominates everything else for a working mother. Which is why the Dependent Care
FSA (§6.3), the Child and Dependent Care Credit (§4.9), and state childcare subsidies (§5.1)
are worth genuine research effort rather than a shrug.

*** How to reduce baby costs without being miserable

- *Gemachs.* Baby equipment gemachs exist in most frum communities. Cribs, pack-n-plays,
  breast pumps, bassinets. Free or nearly.
- *Hand-me-downs.* Ask. People are relieved to have someone take the bin of 0–3 month clothes
  out of their basement.
- *Buy used for anything that isn't a safety item.* Car seats are the exception — buy new, or
  only from someone you trust completely, because they expire and are compromised by
  accidents you can't see.
- *Diapers in bulk*, subscribe-and-save. Store brands are usually fine.
- *WIC* (§5.1) covers formula, which is otherwise brutal.
- *Skip most of the gadget category.* Wipe warmers, bottle sterilizers, five kinds of carrier.
  The industry is built on the anxiety of new parents.

** 11.3 Planning financially for a growing family

*** Housing is the step function

The moment you have four children you need a fundamentally different home than you did with
one. Start planning for that transition years before it's urgent. In frum neighborhoods where
inventory is tight, the family that started saving and watching the market three years early
gets a different outcome than the family that started looking when the fifth baby arrived.

*** Car

At some point a sedan stops working. A minivan or a large SUV becomes structural, not
aspirational. Budget for it before the day you need it. Buy used — a three-year-old minivan
has absorbed the worst depreciation and has a decade of life in it.

*** Health insurance

Family plans generally cost the same for three children as for eight — "employee + family" is
one tier. That's a rare piece of good news; verify it in your specific plan, but it's the
usual structure.

*** Income planning

The years with several small children are the years when a mother's ability to work outside
the home is most constrained and childcare costs are highest. That is often a household's
tightest financial period, and it may coincide with your career being young.

Plan for it. If you know that years 3–8 of your marriage will be tight, the years before and
after should be deliberately building reserves rather than expanding lifestyle.

** 11.4 Teaching children about money

You have a daughter who will one day handle money, and likely several siblings after her.
The chinuch here starts earlier than people think, and it is mostly caught rather than
taught — children learn what money means from watching how their parents behave around it,
long before anyone sits them down.

*** By age

| Age    | What they can learn                                              |
|--------+------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 3–5    | Coins and bills exist; things cost money; a tzedakah pushke      |
| 6–9    | An allowance; saving for something wanted; the wait              |
| 10–12  | Comparing prices; earning through work; maaser on their own money|
| 13–15  | A bank account; budgeting a real amount; the cost of things      |
| 16–18  | A job; a debit card; how a paycheck and taxes work               |
| 18+    | Credit, a credit card used correctly, a first Roth IRA           |

*** Principles that matter more than the schedule

- *Let them make small mistakes with small money.* A child who blows an entire allowance on
  something disappointing at nine has learned something for $12 that some adults pay $30,000
  to learn.
- *Let them see you make decisions.* "We'd like that, but it isn't in the budget this month"
  is one of the most valuable sentences a child can hear, and it costs nothing.
- *Give them the maaser calculation to do themselves.* It embeds the arithmetic and the value
  simultaneously.
- *Don't make money a source of anxiety.* Children absorb financial stress with startling
  accuracy and no context to process it. Discuss constraints calmly; discuss crises out of
  earshot.
- *Pay for work, not for existing.* Some allowance unconditionally is fine; extra earned
  through real jobs teaches the connection that matters.

*** A concrete idea worth doing

When a child earns money — a summer job, tutoring, anything with reportable earned income —
*open a Roth IRA for them.* Minors can have custodial Roth IRAs funded up to their earned
income.

$1,000 contributed at age 16, untouched, at 7%, is roughly $27,000 at 65. From one summer of
babysitting. And it teaches investing at the age where the lesson is cheapest.

** 11.5 Supporting parents, being supported

*** Receiving help

Many young frum couples receive support from parents — a monthly amount, help with rent,
covering a chasunah, a down payment. This is normal and generous and there is no shame in it.

Handle it well:

- *Be explicit about whether it's a gift or a loan* (§3.10). Ambiguity here poisons
  relationships.
- *Understand its effect on benefits eligibility* (Part 5). Some programs count regular gifts
  as income. Ask before assuming.
- *Don't build a permanent lifestyle on temporary support.* If in-law support ends — and it
  usually does, whether through their circumstances or yours — the household must be able to
  contract. Know in advance what you'd cut.
- *Express hakaras hatov concretely and repeatedly.* Not a finance point exactly, but the
  relationships this money runs through are worth more than the money.

*** Eventually giving help

Someday your parents may need support, possibly while you're paying tuition for eight
children and saving for a chasunah. The "sandwich generation" is real and financially brutal.

What helps: knowing early what their situation actually is. It's an awkward conversation and
worth having before a crisis forces it. Do they have retirement savings? Long-term care
coverage? A will? Where are the documents? Who has power of attorney?

Ask now, gently, while it's hypothetical. The alternative is finding out during a hospital
stay.

** 11.6 The financial conversation before a shidduch

Not for you — you're married — but you'll be on the other side of this in about eighteen
years, and it's worth naming.

Frum communities have norms about who pays for what in a chasunah, and about support for a
young couple in kollel. These norms are often unstated and sometimes assumed differently by
the two families, which produces the worst possible time to discover a mismatch.

The financial expectations conversation — who's paying for what, what support is expected and
for how long, what happens after — should be explicit, early, and between the parents. This
is a place where clarity is a chesed and vagueness is a trap.

Start saving for it now. §12.4.

** 11.7 Maternity leave, FMLA, and paid family leave

Immediately relevant to you, poorly understood by almost everyone, and worth real money.
There are three separate things here and people confuse them constantly.

*** 1. FMLA — job protection, not pay

The *Family and Medical Leave Act* is federal law giving eligible employees *12 weeks of
unpaid, job-protected leave* for the birth or adoption of a child, or to care for a seriously
ill family member, or for one's own serious health condition.

- *Unpaid.* FMLA guarantees your job and your health insurance continuation. It does not
  guarantee a paycheck. This is the single most common misunderstanding.
- *Eligibility:* you must have worked for the employer at least 12 months, at least 1,250
  hours in the past year, at a location with 50+ employees within 75 miles.
- That last condition excludes a lot of people — many small businesses, many mosdos, many
  small frum-owned employers are simply not covered.

*** 2. Short-term disability — this is what usually pays for maternity leave

In the US, the paycheck during maternity leave usually comes from *short-term disability
insurance* (§6.5), not from FMLA.

- Typically pays 50–70% of wages for 6–8 weeks after birth (longer for a C-section or
  complications).
- Some employers provide it. Some states *mandate* it: New York, New Jersey, California,
  Rhode Island, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico have state disability programs.
- *Critical timing rule:* you generally cannot buy short-term disability coverage once you are
  already pregnant — pregnancy is then a pre-existing condition. If your employer offers it as
  a voluntary benefit at open enrollment, *elect it before you need it.* This is a real,
  concrete, several-thousand-dollar decision made a year in advance.

*** 3. Paid Family Leave — a separate, newer, state-level program

Distinct from disability. Disability covers the mother's own recovery; *paid family leave*
covers *bonding* with a new child, and is available to fathers as well.

State programs as of the mid-2020s include New York, New Jersey, California, Massachusetts,
Washington, Connecticut, Rhode Island, Colorado, Oregon, Maryland, Delaware, Minnesota, and
Washington DC — the list keeps growing, so check yours.

Rough shape in the two states most relevant to you:

| State      | Program                                                        |
|------------+----------------------------------------------------------------|
| New York   | NY Paid Family Leave: up to 12 weeks, ~67% of average weekly wage up to a cap. Funded by a small employee payroll deduction. *Both* parents can take it. Plus NY short-term disability for the birth itself |
| New Jersey | NJ Family Leave Insurance: up to 12 weeks, ~85% of wages up to a cap. Plus NJ Temporary Disability Insurance for the birth |

*The practical point:* these are funded by deductions already coming out of your wife's
paycheck. She has been paying for this. Many people never claim it, either because nobody
told them or because they assume it's for someone else.

*** What to actually do

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
BEFORE pregnancy:
[ ] Elect short-term disability at open enrollment if it's voluntary. You cannot
    add it later once pregnant.
[ ] Check whether the employer has 50+ employees (FMLA eligibility).
[ ] Read the employee handbook's parental leave policy. Actually read it.

WHEN PREGNANT:
[ ] Notify HR in the timeframe the policy requires (often 30 days' notice for FMLA).
[ ] File the state disability and paid family leave claims — they are separate
    applications with separate forms and separate deadlines.
[ ] Confirm how health insurance premiums get paid during unpaid leave. Sometimes
    you must send the employer a check, and coverage lapses if you don't.
[ ] Find out whether PTO can be stacked on top of, or must run concurrently with,
    the leave. Policies vary and it can be weeks of difference.
[ ] Ask whether YOU (the father) are eligible for paid family leave. In NY and NJ
    you very likely are, and almost nobody claims it.

AFTER BIRTH:
[ ] Add the baby to insurance within the window — usually 30–60 days (§6.3).
[ ] Apply for the Social Security number.
[ ] Everything else in the checklist at §D.7.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** Budgeting for the gap

Even in a good state, leave pays a fraction of wages, capped. Assume the household loses
30–50% of her income for two to three months, and treat that as a known, dated expense — a
sinking fund (§1.7), not a surprise. If you know a baby is coming in seven months, you know
exactly when the shortfall arrives and roughly how big it is. That is the most plannable
"emergency" in existence.

* PART 12 — THE FRUM FINANCIAL LIFE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Two things dominate frum family money, and neither appears in ordinary
finance books.

*Tuition.* Once several children are in school it can run $60,000 a year or more — more than
most families earn. That's why almost nobody pays the published price and almost everybody
applies for a reduction. So apply, every year, be completely honest, and understand that when
your income jumps, the tuition bill follows about a year later. Save for that in advance.
It's the most predictable financial shock in frum life and it still catches people.

*Weddings.* Figure $50,000–150,000 per child, all in. Your daughter's is about twenty years
away. Saving $130 a month starting now gets you there. Starting when she's eighteen costs
$2,360 a month.

That gap — $130 against $2,360 for the identical result — is the whole argument for starting
now. It's the same argument as compound interest, aimed at the bill that ambushes frum
families most reliably.
#+END_QUOTE

** 12.1 Tuition: the defining financial fact of frum life

*** The scale of it

Yeshiva and Bais Yaakov tuition, per child per year, in the American frum world, commonly
runs:

| Level                     | Typical range          |
|---------------------------+------------------------|
| Playgroup / nursery       | $3,000–8,000           |
| Elementary                | $6,000–15,000          |
| Girls' high school        | $8,000–18,000          |
| Boys' mesivta             | $10,000–25,000         |
| Beis medrash / seminary   | $15,000–35,000+        |
| Israel year(s)            | $25,000–40,000         |

For a family of six children, the peak years — when several are in high school
simultaneously — can mean an obligation of $60,000–100,000 a year. Against a household
income that, for most frum families, is nowhere near that.

*This is the central financial fact of the community*, it drives an enormous share of the
financial stress in frum life, and it is not solvable by budgeting. It's worth stating
plainly rather than pretending careful shopping closes that gap.

*** How the system actually works

Almost no one pays full tuition. The published rate is closer to a list price, and most
families are on some form of *scholarship* (also called tuition assistance or a reduction).
Schools operate on the assumption that a minority of families paying full or above-full
tuition subsidizes the majority who cannot.

The mechanism is a *scholarship committee* — usually lay leaders plus administration — that
reviews each family's finances and sets a number.

*** What the committee looks at

Practices vary by school, but generally:

- Tax returns (usually the last two years). This is the primary document.
- W-2s and pay stubs.
- Bank statements, sometimes.
- Assets — savings, investments, home equity.
- Other children's tuition at other schools.
- Extraordinary expenses — medical, special needs.
- Support received from family.
- Sometimes: what car you drive, whether you went away for Pesach, where you vacation. Yes,
  really. Some committees consider visible lifestyle, whether or not that's how you'd design
  the system.

*** How to handle the process

1. *Apply.* Every year. Do not assume you don't qualify. Do not skip it out of pride — that is
   an expensive form of pride and the school would rather have the conversation.
2. *Be completely honest.* Committees compare documents, they talk to each other across
   schools, and being caught misrepresenting is catastrophic for your standing in the
   community and for the children's placement.
3. *Document everything unusual.* Medical bills, a job loss, support that ended, a special
   needs expense. Committees can only account for what they know.
4. *Meet in person if offered.* A conversation conveys context a form can't.
5. *Ask about payment plans.* Ten or twelve monthly payments instead of two large ones is
   almost always available and dramatically easier on cash flow.
6. *Understand the recalculation timing.* If your income jumps, the scholarship usually
   adjusts the *following* year. Save for that increase in advance — it's the single most
   predictable financial shock in frum life and people are still surprised by it.

*** Planning for it

- *Treat tuition as a fixed cost, like a mortgage*, in every housing and lifestyle decision.
  Because it is one, for about thirty consecutive years.
- *A 529 plan is of limited use for K-12*, though federal law now permits up to $10,000/year
  per beneficiary for K-12 tuition. Critically: *state* treatment varies, and some states do
  not conform, meaning a K-12 withdrawal can trigger state tax and recapture of prior
  deductions. Check your state before using a 529 this way.
- *Some employers offer dependent scholarship programs.* Rare, but worth asking.
- *Talk to the school before you're in crisis*, not after you've missed three payments.

*** The strategic point

The main lever on tuition is *income*, not thrift. This is one more reason Part 10 matters
more than Part 1 for your household over the next decade. A family earning $200,000 pays more
tuition than a family earning $90,000 — but keeps far more after paying it.

** 12.2 Yom tov and the annual cycle

Yom tov expenses are the textbook case for sinking funds (§1.7). A realistic annual picture:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Tishrei (Rosh Hashana, Yom Kippur, Sukkos):
    Food for many meals, guests             $600–1,500
    Lulav and esrog                         $80–400
    Schach, sukkah maintenance              $100–500
    Yom tov clothing for the family         $400–2,000
    Travel, if applicable                   $0–2,000

  Chanukah:
    Menorah, oil, gifts                     $150–500

  Purim:
    Mishloach manos                         $150–600
    Matanos l'evyonim                       $100–500 (a chiyuv, not optional)
    Costumes                                $50–200
    Seudah                                  $150–400

  Pesach:
    Food (the big one)                      $800–3,000
    Cleaning help                           $0–800
    New dishes/utensils over time           $100–500
    Clothing                                $300–1,500
    Program or travel, if applicable        $0–15,000+

  Shavuos:
    Food, dairy, learning-night needs       $200–500

  ANNUAL TOTAL, modest version:             ~$3,500
  ANNUAL TOTAL, comfortable version:        ~$8,000+
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** Reducing yom tov costs without reducing yom tov

- Buy non-perishables for Pesach starting months early, on sale, a few items per shopping
  trip.
- Split bulk purchases with another family.
- Host rather than travel — cheaper, and often better anyway.
- Set a mishloach manos budget and a standard package before Adar, not during it.
- Buy clothing off-season. Yom tov clothes in the weeks before yom tov are priced for people
  who had no plan.
- Be honest about which expenses are the mitzvah and which are the social expectation around
  it. That's a personal line, not one I can draw for you, but drawing it deliberately is the
  point.

*** On Pesach programs

A Pesach program for a family can cost $15,000–30,000. For most families this is not a
financial decision that survives contact with arithmetic — it's often more than a year's
retirement savings, or a meaningful fraction of a down payment.

If it's genuinely affordable for you, enjoy it without guilt. If it's going on a credit card
or displacing savings, it's worth naming clearly what it's costing. Fifteen thousand dollars
at 7% for thirty years is roughly $114,000. That's the real price of one Pesach.

** 12.3 Simchos: attending and making

*** Attending

You'll be invited to many. Gift norms vary by community and closeness — anywhere from $50 to
several hundred. Plus travel, plus clothing, plus babysitting.

Budget it annually as a sinking fund line. If you attend fifteen simchos a year at an average
all-in cost of $120, that's $1,800 — real money that never appears in anyone's budget.

*** Making one — a bar mitzvah

Ranges enormously: from a kiddush in shul (a few thousand) to a full hall affair
($15,000–40,000+).

The pressure to match what others do is intense and mostly invisible until you're in it.
Decide your number *before* you start planning, and let the plan fit the number rather than
discovering the number after the plan.

** 12.4 The chasunah fund: start it now

*** The numbers

A frum wedding in America commonly costs $30,000–80,000, split between families by local
custom. Plus, in many communities, an expectation of supporting the young couple during
kollel years, plus setting up an apartment.

Total realistic obligation per child, in today's dollars: *$50,000–150,000.*

Your daughter is one year old. Her chasunah is plausibly eighteen to twenty years away. You
may have several more children, meaning several of these, possibly two or three within a few
years of each other.

*** Why starting now is not premature

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Goal: $60,000 per child in 20 years, invested at 6%.

  Start at her birth (20 years):    $130/month
  Start when she's 5  (15 years):   $206/month
  Start when she's 10 (10 years):   $366/month
  Start when she's 15  (5 years):   $860/month
  Start when she's 18  (2 years): $2,360/month
#+END_EXAMPLE

$130 a month versus $2,360 a month. That is the entire argument, and it's the same argument
as §7.2, applied to the expense frum families are most reliably ambushed by.

*** Where to put it

A time horizon of eighteen years means invest it, don't save it (§1.10, §7.5). Options:

- *A taxable brokerage account* in your name, in a target-date-style fund or a simple index
  mix, shifting toward bonds as the date approaches. Flexible — usable for a chasunah, a down
  payment, or anything else. My default recommendation, because a chasunah is not an
  education expense and flexibility is worth a lot across eighteen unpredictable years.
- *A 529 plan* — only if the money might go to education (seminary, yeshiva, college). Tax-free
  growth for qualified education expenses, with penalties on non-qualified withdrawals. See
  §12.7.
- *A Roth IRA* — contributions can be withdrawn any time tax and penalty free (§8.10). Some
  families use it as a flexible dual-purpose account. Downside: it consumes retirement
  contribution room you can never get back.

*** The uncomfortable priority question

If you can't fund both retirement and a chasunah fund: *fund retirement first.*

This feels backwards and un-frum, and I want to argue it properly rather than assert it.
There are loans, gemachs, and community mechanisms for a chasunah. There is no loan for
retirement. A couple who spends their retirement savings on their children's weddings
becomes financially dependent on those same children twenty years later — which transfers the
burden to a generation who will be paying their own tuitions.

Funding your retirement *is* a way of caring for your children. It's just one that pays off
on a delay long enough that it doesn't feel like it.

** 12.5 Kollel economics, honestly

*** The financial shape

A kollel household typically combines: a kollel stipend (modest, sometimes a few hundred to a
couple thousand dollars a month), a working spouse, parental support, and community
programs. Total household income is usually well below what the same couple could earn
otherwise.

That is a chosen trade, and it's not my place to evaluate the choice. What I can do is name
the financial consequences clearly so they're planned for rather than discovered.

*** The consequences worth planning around

1. *Social Security credits* (§4.5). Years with no reported earnings are zeros. Mitigated by a
   later full career and by spousal benefits, but real.
2. *Retirement savings*. Years without contributions are years without compounding, and those
   are the highest-value years (§7.2). The spousal IRA (§8.6) is the direct answer and it is
   badly underused.
3. *Disability exposure* (§6.5). Without work credits, no SSDI. If the working spouse is the
   sole earner, *her* disability coverage becomes the household's critical protection — check
   whether her employer provides it.
4. *Career runway.* Entering a profession at 30 rather than 22 shortens the earning career by
   eight years and delays peak earnings. It is entirely survivable — many frum professionals
   start later and do very well — but it means the years after entry must be more
   deliberate, not less.
5. *Credit history.* Years without income can mean years without credit-building. See §3.6;
   this is fixable and should be fixed now.

*** The exit ramp

If and when kollel transitions to full-time parnassah, the transition is smoother when
prepared:

- Build credentials or skills *during* kollel where possible — which is exactly what you're
  doing with computer science, and it's the right move.
- Maintain some reportable income if you can, for credits and credit history.
- Network before you need to. The frum professional world runs on referrals and the
  relationships take time to build.
- Have the emergency fund in place before the transition, not after.
- Model the transition year's finances in advance (§10.8).

*** For those who stay in chinuch or klei kodesh

If the long-term path is teaching, rabbanus, or communal work rather than a secular career,
the numbers are different and worth naming:

- Salaries are modest, often with irregular payment timing.
- Many mosdos are 501(c)(3)s, which makes *Public Service Loan Forgiveness* available for
  federal student loans (§3.11) — a genuinely valuable and underused benefit.
- Some offer 403(b) plans, sometimes with a match. Ask; many employees never do.
- *Parsonage* (housing allowance) is a real and significant tax benefit for qualifying clergy:
  a designated portion of compensation can be excluded from federal income tax. The rules are
  specific about who qualifies and how it must be designated *in advance* by the employer.
  This requires a CPA who knows clergy taxation — it's a genuine specialty and worth finding.
- Tuition breaks for employees' children are common and are a large piece of effective
  compensation. Factor them into any comparison.

** 12.6 Maaser: the practical mechanics

I'm going to lay out the questions and the common approaches. The answers are for your rav —
there is real machlokes on several of these and I'm not going to paper over it.

*** The questions people actually face

1. *Gross or net?* Do you calculate maaser on income before or after taxes? The common
   practice is to calculate on income after taxes — since tax money was never really yours —
   but there are different views and it's worth an actual psak rather than an assumption.
2. *What counts as income?* Salary, obviously. What about gifts? Inheritance? A tax refund
   (which is your own money returned)? Investment gains that are unrealized? Reimbursements
   (not income at all)? A scholarship?
3. *Can business expenses be deducted first?* Generally yes — maaser is on profit, not gross
   receipts. But which expenses count is a real question.
4. *What can maaser money be used for?* This is the big one. Tuition? Supporting a relative?
   A shul membership? A meshulach at the door? Different poskim draw these lines differently,
   and the tuition question in particular is widely asked because of the sums involved.
5. *Can you pre-pay or borrow against future maaser?* Sometimes, with conditions.
6. *Chomesh* — giving a fifth rather than a tenth. An upper limit exists in most views.

*** The practical system

Whatever your rav tells you, implement it mechanically:

1. *A separate account* (§1.4) or at minimum a dedicated line in a spreadsheet.
2. *Calculate at the same time each month*, on the same basis, so it never becomes a judgment
   call under pressure.
3. *Move the money immediately* when income arrives. Money that sits in checking gets spent;
   money in the maaser account doesn't feel like yours, which is precisely correct.
4. *Track what you gave and to whom*, both for your own accounting and for taxes.
5. *Keep receipts* for anything that's tax-deductible (§4.8) — and note that
   tax-deductibility and maaser-eligibility are two entirely separate questions that happen
   to overlap often.

*** A note on the tax interaction

Tzedakah to a US 501(c)(3) is deductible *if you itemize* (§4.8). For most families the
standard deduction is larger, so the tzedakah produces no tax benefit — which changes nothing
about the obligation, but is worth knowing so you're not surprised.

Two things can change that: owning a home (mortgage interest plus property tax plus tzedakah
may exceed the standard deduction), and bunching two years of giving into one (§4.8).

** 12.7 Donor-advised funds, and giving efficiently

*** What a DAF is

A *donor-advised fund* is an account at a sponsoring charity (Fidelity Charitable, Schwab
Charitable, and several Jewish community foundations run them) where you:

1. Contribute money or appreciated assets — and take the full tax deduction *immediately*.
2. The money is invested and grows tax-free.
3. You *recommend grants* to any qualifying 501(c)(3) over time, at your pace.

*** Why it's useful

- *Bunching* (§4.8): put two or three years of tzedakah in at once, itemize that year, take
  the standard deduction the other years, and still give at your normal annual pace.
- *Donating appreciated stock*: this is the underused one. Give stock you've held over a year
  that has gone up, and you (a) deduct the *full market value* and (b) *never pay capital gains
  tax* on the appreciation. Giving $10,000 of stock you bought for $4,000 avoids tax on
  $6,000 of gain *and* gives you a $10,000 deduction. Selling it first and giving the cash is
  strictly worse.
- Simplifies recordkeeping — one receipt instead of forty.

*** Cautions

- Contributions are *irrevocable*. Once in, the money must go to charity.
- Fees are typically 0.6% plus fund expenses.
- Grants go only to qualified 501(c)(3)s — so no direct grants to an individual, and no direct
  grants to an Israeli institution without a US arm. Many Israeli mosdos have "American
  Friends of" entities precisely for this.
- Minimums vary; several major sponsors now have no minimum.

Not urgent for you now. Very relevant once you're earning well and giving meaningful maaser.

*** 529 plans, since they came up

A *529 plan* is a state-sponsored education savings account. Contributions are after-tax
federally (many states give a state deduction), growth is tax-free, and withdrawals for
*qualified education expenses* are tax-free.

- Qualified: college tuition, fees, books, room and board, and up to $10,000/year per
  beneficiary for K-12 tuition (federal rule — *check your state's conformity*).
- Non-qualified withdrawals: tax plus a 10% penalty on the *earnings* only.
- The *beneficiary can be changed* to another family member. So money saved for one child who
  doesn't use it can go to a sibling.
- Since 2024, up to $35,000 of leftover 529 funds can be rolled into the beneficiary's Roth
  IRA, subject to conditions (the account must be 15+ years old, among others). This
  substantially reduces the "what if they don't go to college" objection.
- 529 assets have a relatively modest effect on college financial aid when owned by a parent.

*For a frum family:* useful if children may attend college, seminary, or an accredited
institution. Less useful for yeshiva tuition given state-conformity issues. Verify your
state's rules before assuming.
* PART 13 — WILLS, GUARDIANSHIP, AND ESTATE PLANNING

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* You have a one-year-old daughter. If both of you died tomorrow with no
paperwork, a judge who never met your family would choose who raises her and how her money is
managed.

That's the only sentence in this part that truly matters. Everything else is how to prevent
it, and it costs a few hundred dollars and one afternoon.

You need four documents: a *will* naming who raises your children; a *power of attorney* so
your wife can handle money if you're incapacitated; a *healthcare proxy* naming who makes
medical decisions; and, for a frum family, a halachic will alongside the civil one.

And one thing people miss constantly: every account has a "beneficiary" line saying who gets
it when you die, and *that line beats your will*. If a retirement account from an old job
still says "mother," your mother gets the money — whatever your will says. Courts have
enforced this many times, unhappily.

Log in and check every account today. It takes twenty minutes.
#+END_QUOTE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*This is the part young couples skip, and it is the part where skipping does the most
damage.* You have a one-year-old daughter. If both of you died tomorrow without documents, a
court — not you — would decide who raises her, and a court would decide how money is managed
for her. That is not a hypothetical inconvenience; it is a real and avoidable outcome.

An hour of attention and a few hundred dollars fixes it. Please don't put this part off.
#+END_QUOTE

** 13.1 What happens if you do nothing

Dying without a will is dying *intestate*. State law then dictates everything:

- *Who inherits.* Usually the spouse and children in state-specified proportions. Note that
  this may not match what you'd want, and it definitely doesn't match halachic inheritance
  (§13.6).
- *Who raises minor children.* A judge decides, considering relatives who petition. Family
  members can and do fight about this, publicly, at the worst imaginable moment.
- *Who manages the money.* A court-supervised guardianship or conservatorship, with ongoing
  court oversight, fees, and reporting — and the child receives everything outright at 18,
  which is rarely what anyone wanted.
- *How long it takes.* Probate without a will is slower and more expensive.

The estate mostly still goes to your family. But *who decides*, *how fast*, *at what cost*, and
*with what conditions* are all handed to a system that has never met you.

** 13.2 The five documents every adult needs

*** 1. A will (Last Will and Testament)

Names who inherits, names an *executor* (the person who administers the estate), and — most
importantly for you — *names a guardian for minor children*.

*** 2. Guardianship designation for minor children

Usually inside the will. Name a *primary* and at least one *backup*. Consider naming a
different person as *guardian of the person* (who raises the child) than *guardian of the
estate* (who manages the money) — those are different skills and it's often wise to separate
them.

*Talk to the people first.* Being named a guardian without warning is a shock, and the answer
"no" is much better received in advance than discovered in a crisis. Discuss with them:
values, chinuch, where the children would live, and what financial resources would come with
them (life insurance, §6.4 — which is what makes saying yes practical).

*** 3. Durable power of attorney

Authorizes someone to handle your finances if you're incapacitated. Without it, your spouse
cannot necessarily access accounts in your sole name, sign on your behalf, or manage your
affairs — she'd need a court-appointed conservatorship, which is slow, expensive, and public.

*"Durable"* means it survives your incapacity. That's the entire point, and a non-durable
power of attorney is useless for this purpose.

*** 4. Healthcare proxy / medical power of attorney

Names who makes medical decisions if you can't. *This is essential for a frum family*, because
end-of-life medical decisions carry halachic weight and the default legal framework does not
account for that. Your proxy should be someone who will consult a rav and follow the psak.

*** 5. Living will / advance directive

States your wishes about life-sustaining treatment. Halachic considerations here are
significant and differ from standard American advance directive forms, which are often
drafted with assumptions that don't match halacha.

*Use a halachic healthcare proxy form.* Agudath Israel, the RCA, and Chayim Aruchim all
publish versions designed for this, typically naming a rav to be consulted. Standard hospital
forms are not adequate for someone who wants decisions made al pi halacha.

** 13.3 Beneficiary designations override your will

*This section is short and it is the one that catches the most people.*

Certain assets pass by *beneficiary designation*, not by will:

- Life insurance
- 401(k), 403(b), IRA, and other retirement accounts
- Bank accounts with "payable on death" (POD) designations
- Brokerage accounts with "transfer on death" (TOD) designations
- Some pensions and annuities

*The beneficiary form wins.* If your 401(k) names your mother — because you filled it out at
your first job before you were married — and your will leaves everything to your wife, *your
mother gets the 401(k)*. The will does not override it. Courts have upheld this repeatedly
and unhappily.

*** What to do

1. Log into every account today. Yes, today.
2. Check the primary and contingent beneficiaries.
3. Update after every major life event: marriage, each birth, a death in the family, a
   divorce in the family.
4. Print or screenshot the confirmations and put them with your other documents.

*** Naming minor children as beneficiaries — don't do it directly

A minor cannot legally receive a large sum. If you name your one-year-old as the beneficiary
of a $1,000,000 policy, the money goes into a court-supervised guardianship until she turns
18 — and then a court hands an eighteen-year-old a million dollars, outright, with no
conditions.

*The fix:* name a *trust* as beneficiary, with a trustee you choose and terms you set (§13.4).
This is the single most common reason a young family needs a trust, and it is a
straightforward one to set up.

** 13.4 Trusts

*** What a trust is

A legal arrangement where a *trustee* holds and manages assets for *beneficiaries*, according
to terms you write. You (the *grantor*) set the rules.

*** Do you need one?

Two situations where a young family genuinely does:

1. *Minor children as beneficiaries* of life insurance or retirement accounts (§13.3). A
   simple *testamentary trust* — created by your will, springing into existence only if you
   die — handles this and costs little to add.
2. *A child with special needs.* A *special needs trust* preserves eligibility for
   means-tested benefits (SSI, Medicaid) that a direct inheritance would destroy. This is
   specialized work and must be done by an attorney who does it regularly. An inheritance
   left directly to a disabled child can disqualify them from benefits worth far more than
   the inheritance.

*** Revocable living trusts

Often marketed to young families as a way to avoid *probate* (the court process of
administering an estate). Genuinely useful in states where probate is slow and expensive
(California, Florida), less so in states where it's straightforward. Costs more than a simple
will.

For most young couples with modest assets, a will plus correct beneficiary designations plus
a testamentary trust for minors is sufficient. Be skeptical of anyone selling an expensive
living trust package to a 24-year-old.

*** Estate tax

The federal estate tax exemption is very large — well over $13 million per person, and raised
further by 2025 legislation. A married couple can shelter roughly double that. Virtually no
one reading this will owe federal estate tax.

*But some states have their own estate or inheritance tax with much lower thresholds.* New
York's exemption is several million with a notorious "cliff." New Jersey repealed its estate
tax but retains an inheritance tax that exempts spouses, children, and parents (so it rarely
affects a nuclear family). Check your state.

** 13.5 Getting the documents made

| Route                   | Cost         | When it's right                      |
|-------------------------+--------------+--------------------------------------|
| Online (LegalZoom, etc.)| $100–400     | Simple situation, no complications   |
| Flat-fee estate attorney| $800–2,500   | Most families. Recommended           |
| Full estate planning    | $3,000–10,000| Business, large assets, special needs|

For your situation — a young couple with a child, modest assets, and halachic considerations
— I'd use *an estate attorney who is familiar with halachic will provisions*. In frum
communities these are not hard to find, and the incremental cost over a DIY form is small
relative to what it covers.

*After signing:*

- Store originals somewhere safe *and accessible*. A home safe or fireproof box beats a bank
  safe deposit box, which may be sealed on death or inaccessible when needed.
- Tell your executor and guardians where the documents are.
- Give copies to your executor and your rav if he's involved.
- Review every 3–5 years and after every major life change.

** 13.6 The halachic dimension

*** The problem

Halachic inheritance (*yerusha*) and American law differ substantially. Under halacha,
inheritance passes to sons, with the *bechor* receiving a double portion; daughters and wives
are provided for through other mechanisms (kesuba, maintenance from the estate) rather than
inheriting directly.

Most people today want to divide their estate equally among sons and daughters and to leave
their wife well provided for. Simply writing that in a will raises a real halachic question:
distributing an estate contrary to the halachic order may be problematic, and heirs who
accept property they're not entitled to under halacha may have their own issue.

*** The common solution

A *halachic will* uses a mechanism — typically a *shtar chatzi zachar*, or a large debt
obligation (*shtar chov*) payable from the estate that is waived if the halachic heirs
cooperate with the civil distribution — to reconcile the two systems. The structures are
well-established.

*** What to do

1. Have your civil will drafted by an estate attorney.
2. Have a halachic will drafted or reviewed by a rav or a beis din experienced in this.
   Several organizations publish standard forms; some batei din will prepare one.
3. Make sure the two documents work together rather than contradicting each other. This
   requires the attorney and the rav to actually see each other's work — a step people skip,
   producing two documents that each solve half the problem.

I'm flagging the issue, not paskening it. Ask your rav.

** 13.7 The "if something happens" document

Separate from legal documents, and arguably more immediately useful: a single document
telling your spouse (and eventually your executor) where everything is.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  ACCOUNTS
    Bank: which banks, which accounts, roughly what's in them
    Brokerage / retirement accounts: where, account numbers
    Credit cards: which ones
    Loans and debts: to whom, how much

  INSURANCE
    Life insurance: company, policy number, amount, agent's phone
    Health, auto, home/renters, disability: same

  DOCUMENTS
    Where the will, POA, healthcare proxy, and halachic will are stored
    Where the marriage license, kesuba, birth certificates, SSN cards are
    Where the safe deposit box is and where the key is

  DIGITAL
    Password manager master password (sealed, or with a trusted person)
    Email accounts, phone passcode
    Any accounts with money in them (PayPal, Venmo, crypto)

  PEOPLE
    Attorney, accountant, insurance agent, financial advisor: names and numbers
    Rav
    Executor and guardians

  RECURRING OBLIGATIONS
    Subscriptions and autopays that would need cancelling
    Tuition payment arrangements
    Standing tzedakah commitments
#+END_EXAMPLE

Update it annually — Elul is a natural time. Tell your wife where it is. This document has
prevented more misery than most legal instruments, because the common crisis isn't a contested
estate, it's a widow who doesn't know which bank held the account.

** 13.8 Planning for a child with a disability

A short chapter that most guides omit. Included because families with many children are more
likely to encounter it, because the planning is genuinely different, and because the default
outcome — leaving money to a disabled child the ordinary way — actively harms them.

*** The core problem

Many programs a disabled adult depends on — *SSI* (Supplemental Security Income) and
*Medicaid* in particular — are means-tested with very low asset limits. SSI's individual
resource limit has been $2,000 for decades.

So: a well-meaning grandparent leaves $50,000 to a disabled grandchild. The child is now over
the asset limit and loses SSI and Medicaid. Medicaid may have been paying for services worth
far more than $50,000 a year. The inheritance did net harm.

*** The two tools

**** ABLE accounts

Created by federal law in 2014. A tax-advantaged savings account for a person whose disability
began before age 26 — *rising to age 46* for disabilities beginning in 2026 and later, which
substantially expands eligibility.

- Contributions are after-tax; growth and withdrawals for *qualified disability expenses* are
  tax-free. Qualified expenses are broad: housing, education, transportation, employment
  support, health, assistive technology, basic living expenses.
- *Up to $100,000 in an ABLE account is disregarded for SSI purposes*, and the full balance is
  generally disregarded for Medicaid.
- Annual contribution limit is tied to the gift tax exclusion (roughly $19,000 — Appendix B),
  with more allowed if the beneficiary works.
- Open one through any state's program; you're generally not restricted to your own state, so
  compare fees and investment options.
- One caution: many states can seek Medicaid reimbursement from what remains in the account at
  the beneficiary's death. Some have eliminated this. Check yours.

Simple, cheap, and dramatically underused. If it applies to your family, open one.

**** Special needs trusts

For larger amounts, a *special needs trust* (also called a supplemental needs trust) holds
assets for the person's benefit without those assets counting as theirs.

- A *third-party* SNT is funded by someone else — you, a grandparent — and has no Medicaid
  payback requirement. This is the one you want for estate planning.
- A *first-party* SNT holds the disabled person's own money (a legal settlement, an
  inheritance received directly) and generally *does* require Medicaid payback at death.
- The trustee can pay for things that *supplement* rather than replace benefits: therapies,
  travel, education, a computer, a companion. Careless distributions — cash directly to the
  beneficiary, or paying for food and shelter — can reduce benefits, so the trustee needs to
  understand the rules.

*** What families should actually do

1. *Tell relatives not to leave money directly to the child.* Grandparents mean well and this
   is exactly how the damage happens. They should leave it to the third-party special needs
   trust instead. One conversation prevents it.
2. *Name the trust, not the child, as beneficiary* on life insurance and retirement accounts
   (§13.3).
3. *Use an attorney who does this specifically.* Not a general practitioner. The rules are
   technical, they interact with both federal and state benefit programs, and a defective
   trust fails precisely when it's needed.
4. *Consider permanent life insurance here.* One of the few genuinely defensible uses (§6.4) —
   a guaranteed payout whenever you die, funding a trust for someone who will need support for
   life, is a real match between the product and the problem.
5. *Write a letter of intent.* Not a legal document — a plain-language description of the
   person: their routines, preferences, medical history, who knows them, what makes them
   happy, what frightens them. When you are gone, whoever steps in will need this far more
   than they need the trust document.
6. *Plan for adult guardianship* or a less restrictive alternative (supported decision-making,
   a power of attorney) as the child approaches 18, when parental authority legally ends.

*** In the frum community

There are organizations dedicated to this — Yachad, Ohel, Hamaspik, Chai Lifeline, and local
equivalents — that provide services and, importantly, know the benefits landscape. They are
also the fastest route to families who have already navigated exactly what you're facing,
which is worth more than any document.

* PART 14 — ISRAEL: MONEY, ALIYAH, AND THE AMERICAN CITIZEN ABROAD

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* If you're an American citizen you file American tax returns *forever* —
living in Israel, earning nothing in America, never coming back. The United States is one of
only two countries on earth that does this. Most people don't know it, and that ignorance is
the most expensive thing in this part.

You usually won't *owe* anything, because rules exist to stop you being taxed twice. But you
must file. And if your Israeli accounts *added together* ever exceed $10,000 at any moment in
the year, you must file a separate report listing them. The penalties for missing that one
are brutal, and it catches thousands of ordinary people who did nothing wrong except not
know.

Second warning: the everyday Israeli savings accounts that Israelis love — keren hishtalmut,
Israeli mutual funds — can be a tax nightmare for an American. Don't buy Israeli investment
funds without asking someone who knows this specific corner.

The money picture itself often favors Israel for a large family: healthcare is nearly free,
and yeshiva tuition largely doesn't exist there the way it does here. The hard part is
earning enough. Talk to a US–Israel accountant *before* you move — several of the good
decisions can only be made while you're still here.
#+END_QUOTE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
You said you're in the US now and may be in Israel later. That combination is one of the most
financially complicated situations an American can be in, and the complications are the kind
that are cheap to prevent and expensive to fix afterwards. This part is longer than a
"maybe someday" would normally warrant, precisely because several of the decisions have to be
made *before* you move.
#+END_QUOTE

** 14.1 The single most important warning

*If you are a US citizen, you file US tax returns for the rest of your life, no matter where
you live.*

The United States is one of only two countries in the world (the other is Eritrea) that taxes
its citizens on worldwide income regardless of residence. Moving to Israel does not end your
US filing obligation. Neither does never returning. Neither does having no US income.

Your daughter, born in the US, has the same obligation for life. Any children born in Israel
to US citizen parents are generally US citizens too, and acquire the same obligation.

This has real consequences, covered below. Ignoring it is the mistake — the penalties are
severe and the compliance is manageable if you know about it in advance.

** 14.2 Israeli income and the paycheck

*** Gross and net (brutto and netto)

Same concept as §1.3, different deductions:

| Deduction              | Roughly                                          |
|------------------------+--------------------------------------------------|
| Income tax (mas hachnasa) | Progressive, roughly 10% to 50%               |
| Bituach Leumi (National Insurance) | A few percent, tiered              |
| Mas briut (health tax) | ~3–5%, collected with Bituach Leumi              |
| Pension contribution   | ~6% employee (mandatory)                          |
| Keren hishtalmut       | ~2.5% employee, if offered                        |

*** Nekudot zikui — tax credit points

Israel's system uses *credit points* rather than a standard deduction. Each point reduces tax
owed by a fixed monthly amount (a few hundred shekels — verify the current value).

- Every resident gets a base number of points.
- *Women receive additional points.*
- *Parents receive points per child*, with more for young children.
- *New olim receive extra points* on a declining schedule over roughly the first 3.5 years.

The practical effect: low-to-moderate earners in Israel often pay very little income tax,
because the credit points wipe out the liability. A family with several young children and a
