modest income may owe essentially nothing.

*** Salary levels — the honest comparison

Israeli salaries are substantially lower than American ones in most fields. Israeli tech is
the major exception — Israeli high-tech pays well by Israeli standards, though generally
below US levels for equivalent roles.

Rough shape (verify current numbers; these move):

| Role                        | Israel (monthly gross, NIS) | Rough USD/year |
|-----------------------------+-----------------------------+----------------|
| Average national wage       | ~12,000–13,000              | ~$40,000       |
| Teacher                     | ~8,000–14,000               | ~$27,000–46,000|
| Junior software developer   | ~15,000–22,000              | ~$50,000–73,000|
| Senior software developer   | ~30,000–50,000              | ~$100,000–165,000 |

*The relevant comparison isn't salary — it's salary against cost of living*, which is a mixed
picture: housing in desirable areas is brutally expensive relative to income (arguably worse
than the US), cars and electronics and many consumer goods are more expensive, while
healthcare, education, and childcare are dramatically cheaper. §14.9 works through it.

** 14.3 Bituach Leumi

*Bituach Leumi* (National Insurance Institute) is Israel's social security system. Every
resident must register and pay, employed or not.

What it provides:

- *Kitzbat yeladim* — child allowance, paid monthly per child, *not means-tested*. The amount
  is modest per child (roughly 150–250 NIS/month depending on birth order) but universal and
  it adds up for a large family.
- *Old-age pension* (kitzvat ziknah).
- *Disability benefits.*
- *Maternity benefits* — including paid maternity leave funded by Bituach Leumi, and a birth
  grant. Considerably more generous than the US federal baseline, which is zero.
- *Unemployment.*
- *Income support* for those below a threshold.
- *Reserve duty compensation.*

*Crucial for olim:* register with Bituach Leumi immediately on arrival. There is typically a
waiting period before certain benefits become available, and healthcare coverage depends on
registration.

*Crucial for anyone leaving Israel:* if you leave, you must handle your Bituach Leumi status
properly or you can accrue debts for years without knowing. People discover five-figure
Bituach Leumi debts on a return visit. Handle it formally, in writing, before departing.

** 14.4 Israeli healthcare

*** How it works

Israel has universal healthcare through the *Health Insurance Law*. Every resident chooses one
of four *kupot cholim* (health funds): Clalit, Maccabi, Meuhedet, or Leumit. Funding comes
from the health tax collected with Bituach Leumi.

- Basic coverage (*sal habriut*) is comprehensive and identical across the four funds by law.
- The funds compete on service, clinic locations, and supplementary offerings.
- *Supplementary insurance* (*bituach mashlim*, sometimes called *shaban*) costs a modest
  monthly amount and adds choice of surgeon, faster access, overseas treatment, dental, and
  more. Nearly everyone buys it, and it's worth it.
- *Private insurance* on top of that exists for those who want it.

*** The comparison to America

This is one of the genuinely large financial differences between the two countries.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  US family health insurance, employer plan:  $500–1,200/month employee share
                                              plus deductible $3,000–8,000
                                              plus copays and coinsurance

  Israel: health tax (a few % of income, already withheld)
          plus supplementary ~100–200 NIS/month for a family
          plus small copays
#+END_EXAMPLE

For a large family with regular medical needs, the difference is thousands of dollars a year.
It is one of the strongest financial arguments for Israel and it is frequently left out of
the comparison because it's invisible — it shows up as an absence of bills.

*** For US citizens

Note that US-based health insurance generally does not cover you living abroad. And Medicare
does not cover care outside the US at all — relevant much later, but it means an American
retiring in Israel needs to plan for it.

** 14.5 Aliyah benefits

Israel provides substantial support for new immigrants (*olim chadashim*). Administered by
the Ministry of Aliyah and Integration, with *Nefesh B'Nefesh* handling much of the process
for North American olim.

Typical benefits (verify current details — these change):

- *Sal klita* — an absorption basket, cash paid over roughly the first six months, scaled by
  family size. Meaningful for a family.
- *Free ulpan* — Hebrew instruction, typically five months.
- *Customs exemptions* — on shipping household goods and on certain purchases, within time
  windows after arrival.
- *Reduced purchase tax (mas rechisha)* on a first home — a genuinely significant benefit,
  usable within a defined period after aliyah.
- *Income tax benefits* — extra credit points on a declining schedule.
- *Rental assistance* for an initial period.
- *Educational benefits* — subsidized or free academic study for eligible olim.
- *Healthcare* from arrival.
- *The ten-year exemption* (§14.7) — the big one.

*** The ten-year foreign income exemption

Under Israeli law (commonly referenced as the "Section 14" benefit), new olim and returning
residents receive a *ten-year exemption from Israeli tax on foreign-source income and capital
gains*, and historically an exemption from *reporting* it as well.

This means: a new oleh with US investments, US rental income, or a US business can have that
income exempt from Israeli tax for a decade.

*Two critical cautions:*

1. *The reporting exemption has been under active legislative revision*, with changes tightening
   reporting requirements while largely preserving the tax exemption. Verify the current state
   of the law when you actually move — this is exactly the kind of provision that changes.
2. *The exemption is from ISRAELI tax. Your US tax obligation is unaffected.* Income exempt in
   Israel is still fully taxable by the US. People misunderstand this and it goes badly.

*** Planning point

The ten-year window creates real planning opportunities — for example, realizing capital gains
on US investments during the window may face no Israeli tax. This is exactly the situation
where paying an accountant who handles US-Israel cases, *before* moving, pays for itself many
times over.

** 14.6 Israeli savings, pension, and investment vehicles

*** Keren pensia — the mandatory pension

Since 2008, pension contributions are mandatory for employees. Roughly:

- Employee: ~6% of salary
- Employer: ~6.5% toward pension
- Employer: ~8.33% toward *pitzuim* (severance)

The money goes into a pension fund providing retirement income, plus disability and survivor
coverage. Contributions receive tax benefits.

*** Keren hishtalmut — the "study fund," and the best deal in Israel

Historically intended for professional development, now effectively a *tax-advantaged medium-
term savings account*, and one of the most attractive savings vehicles available anywhere.

- Employer contributes ~7.5% of salary, employee ~2.5%.
- Employer contributions up to a ceiling are not taxed as income.
- *After six years, the entire amount — including all investment gains — can be withdrawn
  completely tax-free.* (Or after three years for approved professional training.)
- Can be left to keep growing tax-free beyond six years.

Capital gains in Israel are otherwise taxed around 25%. A vehicle with tax-free growth and a
six-year lockup is extraordinary. *If an Israeli employer offers a keren hishtalmut, take it.*
If you're self-employed, you can open one yourself with deductible contributions.

*** Kupat gemel and kupat gemel l'hashkaa

- *Kupat gemel* — a provident fund, historically a long-term retirement savings vehicle.
- *Kupat gemel l'hashkaa* — an "investment provident fund," after-tax, flexible, withdrawable
  at any time, with an annual contribution cap. Tax advantages if held until retirement age.

*** The PFIC problem — and why this matters enormously to you

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*This is the single most important technical warning in Part 14, and it catches thousands of
American olim.*

Under US tax law, most non-US pooled investment vehicles are *PFICs* — Passive Foreign
Investment Companies. The US taxes PFICs punitively: high tax rates, an interest charge on
deferred gains, and an annual *Form 8621* filing per fund that is genuinely difficult and
expensive to prepare.

Israeli mutual funds, Israeli ETFs, and — depending on structure and on how they're
characterized — potentially *kupot gemel* and *keren hishtalmut* holdings may be treated as
PFICs by the IRS.

The result: an American in Israel can invest in a completely ordinary, tax-favored Israeli
savings vehicle, and find that the US treats it as a tax nightmare — sometimes taxing gains
the US-Israel treaty doesn't clearly protect, and always generating expensive compliance.
#+END_QUOTE

*The plain-English version:* America does not like its citizens investing in foreign funds,
so it punishes them for it. Any pooled investment run outside the US — an Israeli mutual
fund, an Israeli ETF, possibly the savings plans your Israeli employer signs you up for — can
land in a category the IRS taxes harshly and requires a difficult, expensive extra form for,
*every year, for each fund*. An accountant may charge several hundred dollars per fund just
to prepare it.

So the ordinary, sensible, tax-favored Israeli savings account that every Israeli colleague
recommends can be a genuine problem for you specifically, because of your American passport
and for no other reason. This is not a rare edge case; it is the single most common expensive
surprise for American olim.

*What this means practically:*

- Do not buy Israeli mutual funds or Israeli-domiciled ETFs as a US citizen without
  understanding the US treatment first.
- Many American olim keep their investment portfolio in *US-domiciled* funds at a US
  brokerage precisely to avoid this, holding Israeli accounts only for cash and required
  employer plans.
- Employer-mandated pension contributions are not optional, so the question becomes how to
  report them, not whether to have them. The 1975 US-Israel tax treaty does not have a modern
  pension article of the kind found in newer treaties, and the treatment of Israeli pension
  vehicles by the US is genuinely unsettled among practitioners.
- *This is one of the four places in this document where I say: hire a professional.* You want
  a CPA or EA who specifically handles US-Israel cross-border taxation. There are firms that
  do nothing else. It costs real money annually and it is not optional.

** 14.7 US tax obligations while living in Israel

*** You must file. Every year.

Form 1040, as always, reporting worldwide income. The filing threshold is the same as if you
lived in Ohio. Americans abroad get an automatic extension to June 15, with a further
extension available to October 15.

*** Two mechanisms that prevent double taxation

*1. Foreign Earned Income Exclusion (FEIE), Form 2555.*

Excludes a large amount of *earned* income (roughly $130,000+ per person, indexed — Appendix B)
from US tax, if you meet either:

- *Physical presence test*: 330 full days outside the US in a 12-month period, or
- *Bona fide residence test*: a full calendar year as a genuine resident of a foreign country.

Note the limits: it applies to *earned* income only — not investment income, dividends,
capital gains, or most pension income. And there's a matching housing exclusion.

*2. Foreign Tax Credit (FTC), Form 1116.*

A dollar-for-dollar credit for income taxes paid to Israel. Since Israeli income tax rates
are often higher than US rates, the FTC frequently eliminates US tax entirely on the same
income — and generates excess credits that carry forward.

*The strategic choice between them matters more than people realize:*

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*If you use the FEIE, you generally cannot claim the refundable portion of the Child Tax
Credit on excluded income.* If you use the Foreign Tax Credit instead, you may be able to.

For a family with several children, the refundable Child Tax Credit can be worth thousands of
dollars a year — money the US Treasury actually sends you while you live in Israel. Choosing
the FEIE because it's simpler can cost a large family serious money.

And switching methods later has consequences: revoking an FEIE election generally locks you
out of re-electing it for five years without IRS consent.
#+END_QUOTE

Run both calculations. This is a concrete, recurring, several-thousand-dollar decision, and
it is the main reason to have a cross-border accountant rather than doing it yourself.

*** FBAR — FinCEN Form 114

If the *aggregate maximum* balance of all your foreign financial accounts exceeds *$10,000* at
any point during the year, you must file an FBAR.

- Filed separately from your tax return, electronically, to FinCEN (not the IRS).
- Due April 15 with an automatic extension to October 15.
- Counts *all* foreign accounts: checking, savings, pension, keren hishtalmut, brokerage, and
  accounts you merely have signature authority over.
- It's the *aggregate* that matters. Six accounts with $2,000 each triggers it.

*Penalties are severe*: up to $10,000 per non-willful violation per year, and far more for
willful ones. This is not a form to ignore. It's also not difficult — it's an online form
listing accounts and maximum balances.

*** FATCA — Form 8938

A separate report, filed *with* your tax return, of foreign financial assets above thresholds
that are much higher for people living abroad (roughly $200,000 for a single filer abroad,
$400,000 MFJ abroad — verify).

Yes, you may file both FBAR and 8938 reporting the same accounts. Yes, that's redundant. No,
that doesn't excuse skipping either.

*** Self-employment: the missing totalization agreement

*The US and Israel have no Social Security totalization agreement.*

Consequence: a self-employed American living in Israel may owe *US self-employment tax
(15.3%)* on their earnings *and* Israeli Bituach Leumi on the same earnings. The FEIE excludes
income from *income* tax but does *not* exclude it from self-employment tax.

This is a real and expensive trap for the American freelancer in Israel — a category you could
easily fall into as a remote software developer. It's a significant argument for being an
*employee* of an Israeli company rather than a contractor, or for structuring through an
entity, and it is emphatically a "talk to a cross-border accountant before you set this up"
situation.

*** Streamlined compliance, if you're behind

If you discover you've missed years of filings — extremely common among Americans abroad who
genuinely didn't know — the IRS *Streamlined Filing Compliance Procedures* let non-willful
taxpayers catch up with reduced or no penalties. File three years of returns and six years of
FBARs with a certification of non-willfulness.

*Use it before the IRS contacts you.* It's unavailable once they've opened an inquiry. The
program has been modified over time, so verify it still exists in its current form when
needed.

** 14.8 Buying property in Israel

*** Mashkanta — the Israeli mortgage

Structurally different from an American mortgage in ways that surprise Americans:

- *Loan-to-value limits set by the Bank of Israel*: typically up to 75% for a first home, 70%
  for a replacement home, and 50% for an investment property or for a non-resident. So a
  larger down payment is required than in the US.
- *Mixed tracks.* Israeli mortgages are typically split across several components with
  different characteristics: prime-linked (floating with the Bank of Israel rate),
  CPI-linked fixed (*tzamud madad* — the principal itself rises with inflation), unlinked
  fixed (*kalatz*), and variable tracks resetting every few years. Regulation requires a
  minimum share in an unlinked fixed track.
- *CPI-linked loans are the concept with no American equivalent.* The outstanding principal
  increases with the consumer price index. Your balance can *rise* even as you make payments,
  during inflationary periods. The initial rate looks attractive; understand what you're
  accepting.
- *Mortgage terms* are generally up to 30 years, with age limits on the borrower at maturity.
- *Shopping matters enormously.* Israeli mortgage rates and structures vary between banks far
  more than in the US, and negotiation is expected. Many people use a *mortgage broker (yoetz
  mashkantaot)*, and it's usually money well spent.

*** Purchase taxes and costs

- *Mas rechisha* (purchase tax) — progressive, with a significant exemption band for a first
  home for Israeli residents. *Olim receive a reduced rate* within a defined window after
  aliyah. Non-residents and second-home buyers pay substantially more.
- *Lawyer* — required in practice, typically ~0.5–1.5% of the purchase price.
- *Broker fee* — typically ~2% plus VAT, often paid by the buyer, which surprises Americans.
- *Appraisal, registration fees*, and more.

*** Two structural warnings

1. *Buying off-plan (from a contractor)* is common in Israel and follows a payment schedule
   linked to construction progress and often to a construction index. Delays are routine.
   Understand the contract and the guarantees (*Chok Mechr Dira* protections) before signing.
2. *Currency risk is real and often ignored.* If you earn dollars and borrow shekels, or hold
   dollars and buy a shekel-priced asset, exchange rate moves can swing the effective cost by
   20%+ over a few years. This cuts both ways and it is not a small factor.

** 14.9 The actual financial comparison: US vs Israel

An honest accounting, because this decision is usually made on non-financial grounds — as it
should be — but deserves accurate financial inputs.

*** Where Israel is cheaper

| Category      | Why                                                            |
|---------------+----------------------------------------------------------------|
| Healthcare    | Dramatically. Universal coverage, small copays, cheap supplement |
| Education     | Chinuch atzmai and state-religious schools are heavily subsidized or nearly free; even private frum options cost a fraction of American yeshiva tuition |
| Childcare     | Subsidized maonot, and preschool from age 3 is largely free     |
| Higher education | A fraction of US costs                                       |
| Child allowance | Universal, not means-tested, per child                        |
| Public transport | Extensive and cheap                                          |
| Some food     | Produce, local goods                                            |

*The education line is the big one.* Yeshiva tuition is the defining financial burden of
American frum life (§12.1) and it largely does not exist in Israel in the same form. For a
family planning many children, this single difference can exceed everything else combined.

*** Where Israel is more expensive

| Category      | Why                                                            |
|---------------+----------------------------------------------------------------|
| Housing       | Very expensive relative to local income, especially in central areas and desirable frum neighborhoods |
| Cars          | Heavily taxed. A car costs far more than in the US, as does fuel |
| Consumer goods| Electronics, appliances, imported items                         |
| Some groceries| Imported and packaged foods                                     |
| Salaries      | Lower, in most fields, often substantially                      |

*** The honest bottom line

For a large frum family, the arithmetic often favors Israel — *if* you can secure adequate
income there. Lower earnings are offset by the near-elimination of the two largest American
frum-family expenses: tuition and healthcare.

The binding constraint is almost always *parnassah*. Which is why, if aliyah is genuinely on
your horizon, the software path you're on is a well-chosen one: Israeli high-tech pays
competitively by local standards, and remote work for US companies — while it carries the
self-employment tax complication above (§14.7) — is a genuine option that many olim use.

*** What to do before you move

If aliyah is a real possibility, do these *before* leaving, because several become difficult
or impossible afterward:

1. *Talk to a US-Israel cross-border accountant.* Before, not after. The pre-move year has
   planning opportunities that vanish once you're a resident.
2. *Keep a US brokerage account open.* Many US brokers restrict or close accounts with foreign
   addresses. Some (Interactive Brokers, and certain Schwab and Fidelity international
   arrangements) accommodate expatriates. Establishing the relationship while you have a US
   address is far easier than afterward.
3. *Keep a US bank account and a US credit card.* Useful indefinitely, and hard to open from
   abroad.
4. *Understand what happens to your retirement accounts.* IRAs and 401(k)s can generally be
   maintained from abroad. Distributions have both US and Israeli implications — and during
   the ten-year exemption window, potentially favorable ones. Get advice on sequencing.
5. *Consider Roth conversions* while your income is low and before you're an Israeli tax
   resident. This is a genuinely valuable, time-limited opportunity.
6. *Do not sell everything and wire it over impulsively.* Currency timing, capital gains
   timing, and the ten-year window all interact.
7. *Get your US documents in order* — wills, POA, and the location document (§13.7) — with
   awareness that they may need Israeli counterparts.
8. *Understand Bituach Leumi registration* and the healthcare waiting period.
9. *Talk to Nefesh B'Nefesh early.* Their guidance on benefits, employment, and community is
   free and genuinely good.

** 14.10 Renouncing US citizenship — a note, not a recommendation

Some Americans in Israel eventually consider renouncing US citizenship to escape the lifelong
filing burden. Things to know:

- There is a substantial renunciation fee.
- Renunciation is *irrevocable*.
- Above certain wealth or tax-liability thresholds you are a *covered expatriate* and face an
  *exit tax* — a deemed sale of all assets at fair market value on the day before expatriation.
- You must certify five years of US tax compliance, so you cannot renounce your way out of
  past non-filing.
- You lose the right to live and work in the US, and visa-free entry becomes a visitor
  question.

This is a serious, permanent decision requiring specialized legal and tax advice. Mentioned
only so you know it exists as a category and know that it's not a shortcut.
** 14.11 Moving money between the US and Israel

Practical, immediately useful, and a place where people quietly lose 3–5% of every transfer
without noticing.

*** How you lose money on a transfer

Two costs, and the second is the one that hides:

1. *The stated fee.* Visible. Usually the smaller cost.
2. *The exchange rate margin.* Banks quote you a rate worse than the real one — the *mid-market
   rate*, the number you see on Google — and pocket the difference. A bank offering "no fee"
   transfers is usually taking 2–4% in the rate.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Sending $10,000 to Israel:

  Traditional bank wire:  $45 fee + ~3% rate margin  ≈ $345 total cost
  Wise / specialist:      ~$50 fee + ~0.4% margin    ≈  $90 total cost

  Difference on one transfer: ~$255.
  Do it monthly for a year: ~$3,000.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Always compare against the mid-market rate*, not against the other bank's rate. Look it up on
Google or xe.com before you transfer, and compute what you're actually being charged.

*** The options

| Method                  | Notes                                                    |
|-------------------------+----------------------------------------------------------|
| Wise (formerly TransferWise) | Transparent mid-market rate plus a stated fee. Usually cheapest for moderate amounts |
| Specialist FX brokers   | For large transfers ($50,000+), can beat everyone. Get quotes |
| Bank wire               | Most expensive, most universally accepted, sometimes required for a property purchase |
| Israeli bank's own service | Convenient, rarely competitive                        |
| Revolut and similar     | Competitive; check limits and weekend surcharges          |

For a property purchase, the seller's lawyer will usually require a bank wire from a
recognized institution — and Israeli banks apply anti-money-laundering scrutiny to large
incoming transfers. Expect to document the source of funds thoroughly. Start that
conversation weeks before you need the money to land, not days.

*** Currency risk, said plainly

If you earn dollars and spend shekels, or hold dollars and are saving for a shekel-priced
apartment, the exchange rate is a live financial position whether you think about it or not.
The USD/ILS rate has moved 20%+ within a few years, repeatedly, in both directions.

You cannot predict it. What you can do:

- *Match the currency of your savings to the currency of the expense.* If you're buying an
  apartment in Israel in three years, holding those savings in shekels removes the risk. If
  you'll be living on dollars, hold dollars.
- *Don't try to time it.* People wait years for a "better rate" and lose more to waiting than
  they would have to the rate.
- *Move large amounts in tranches* if the timing is flexible. It's dollar-cost averaging
  applied to currency, and it converts a bet into an average.

*** Credit and debit cards abroad

- Get a card with *no foreign transaction fee*. Many do charge 3% on every purchase abroad,
  which is a slow, invisible bleed.
- When a foreign terminal asks whether to charge you in dollars or shekels, *always choose
  the local currency* (shekels). Choosing dollars invokes "dynamic currency conversion," where
  the merchant's bank sets the rate and it is always worse. This one costs people a few
  percent on every purchase and it is presented as a convenience.
- Tell your bank you're traveling, or expect a fraud freeze at the worst moment.

** 14.12 Israeli banking and daily-life money mechanics

Enough to not be lost in your first month.

*** Opening an account

- You'll need your teudat zehut (Israeli ID) or, before that, your oleh documentation.
- Israeli banks charge *account maintenance fees* per transaction and per month in a way
  Americans find startling. Fees are negotiable — genuinely, and expected. Ask for a discount
  (*hanacha*), ask again annually, and compare banks. Bank Yahav, Bank Massad, and the digital
  banks (One Zero, Ash Israel) are often cheaper than the big three.
- Some banks have English-speaking service or dedicated olim desks. Worth seeking out for the
  first year.

*** Vocabulary you'll hear

| Hebrew term          | What it is                                                  |
|----------------------+-------------------------------------------------------------|
| Hora'at keva         | A standing order / direct debit. How you pay recurring bills |
| Osek patur / murshe  | Small-business tax statuses for the self-employed            |
| Tlush maskoret       | Pay slip                                                     |
| Overdraft (minus)    | Israeli accounts commonly run negative by arrangement. Normal locally, expensive, and a habit worth not acquiring |
| Cheque dachui        | A postdated check — extremely common in Israel, unlike the US |
| Kartis ashrai        | Credit card. Works differently: most Israeli cards are *charge* cards that debit your account in full monthly |
| Tashlumim            | Paying in installments — offered on ordinary purchases, often interest-free. Convenient and a fine way to lose track of commitments |

*** Things that will surprise an American

1. *The overdraft culture.* Running a *minus* is normal and socially unremarkable in Israel. It
   is still debt at meaningful interest. Don't let "everyone does it" become your policy.
2. *Postdated checks* are a standard payment instrument, including for rent — landlords
   commonly ask for twelve postdated checks up front.
3. *Installment payments* (*tashlumim*) are offered on nearly everything at checkout, often at
   zero interest. Genuinely useful; also genuinely easy to accumulate into a large monthly
   obligation you never decided to take on. Track them.
4. *Bank fees are itemized and constant.* Review the statement.
5. *Rent is usually quoted monthly and paid via postdated checks or hora'at keva*, with a
   guarantee (*arvut*) or a bank guarantee often required.

*** For a US citizen, one more time

Every Israeli account — checking, pension, keren hishtalmut, kupat gemel — counts toward your
FBAR aggregate (§14.7). Six small accounts totaling over $10,000 at any point in the year
triggers the filing. Keep a list of accounts and their peak balances as you go; reconstructing
it in April is miserable.

* PART 15 — CARS AND OTHER BIG PURCHASES

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* A car's biggest cost isn't gas or repairs. It's that the car quietly loses
value while sitting in your driveway. A new car loses about 40% of its value in three years.

So buy one that's already two to four years old and let the last owner pay for that. Same
car, much less money.

Three rules: 20% down, a four-year loan at most, and all car costs — payment, insurance, gas,
repairs — under 10% of your income. If you need a seven-year loan to afford it, you can't
afford it. Buy a cheaper car.

At the dealership, understand that they're negotiating *four* things at once and blending
them deliberately so you can't tell where you're losing: the price of the car, your trade-in,
the loan, and the extras. Handle them one at a time. Always ask for the "out-the-door" price,
meaning the total including every fee. And never answer "what monthly payment are you looking
for?" — any payment can be made to look small by stretching the loan.

When the family outgrows a sedan: a used minivan. Everybody resists this and everybody ends
up there anyway.
#+END_QUOTE

** 15.1 Why cars get their own chapter

After housing, transportation is the largest expense in most American households — and unlike
housing, it is almost entirely discretionary in size. A family can spend $250/month on
transportation or $1,400/month, on the same roads, arriving at the same places.

It is also the purchase where people are most systematically separated from their money,
because car dealerships are one of the last places in American commerce where the price is
negotiable, the seller knows vastly more than the buyer, and the transaction is designed to
exhaust you.

And for a growing frum family, the vehicle question is not optional. At some point you need
seats for eight.

** 15.2 Depreciation: the cost nobody sees

*Depreciation* is the loss of a car's value over time. It is the single largest cost of owning
a car and it never appears on any bill.

A typical new car:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  Purchase price, new:              $40,000
  Value after driving off the lot:  $36,000   (−10% immediately)
  After 1 year:                     $32,000   (−20%)
  After 3 years:                    $24,000   (−40%)
  After 5 years:                    $18,000   (−55%)
  After 10 years:                    $9,000   (−78%)

  Cost of the first 3 years:        $16,000 = ~$444/month, before
                                    gas, insurance, or a single repair.
#+END_EXAMPLE

The same car bought at three years old for $24,000 and driven to year ten costs $15,000 of
depreciation over seven years — about $178/month.

*This is the entire argument for buying used*, and it's a strong one: you let someone else pay
for the steepest part of the curve, and modern cars routinely run 200,000+ miles.

*** The sweet spot

*2–4 years old, with 25,000–50,000 miles, from a reliable model line.* The worst depreciation
is behind it, most of the useful life is ahead, and it's often still under some warranty.

*The exception worth naming:* in periods when used car prices spike (as they did in 2021–2023),
the used discount can compress to almost nothing, and a new car with a manufacturer financing
promotion occasionally wins. Check the actual numbers rather than applying the rule blindly.

** 15.3 How much car you can afford

*** The 20/4/10 rule

- *20%* down payment, minimum.
- *4* years maximum loan term.
- *10%* of gross income maximum for all transportation costs — payment, insurance, gas,
  maintenance combined.

If a car fails this test, it's too much car. The rule's real function is the 4-year term: if
you cannot pay it off in four years, you are buying more car than your income supports,
regardless of how comfortable the monthly payment feels.

*** The seven-year loan problem

Average auto loan terms have stretched toward seven and eight years. This is marketed as
affordability and is actually a trap:

- You will be *underwater* (owing more than the car is worth) for most of the loan, because
  the car depreciates faster than the loan amortizes.
- If the car is totaled or you need to sell, you owe the difference in cash.
- You are still paying for a car that is falling apart.
- Total interest paid is far higher.

*Rule:* if you need seven years to afford it, you cannot afford it. Buy a cheaper car.

*** Total cost of ownership

The purchase price is a fraction of what a car costs. Budget all of it:

| Cost                 | Typical annual                                    |
|----------------------+---------------------------------------------------|
| Depreciation         | $1,500–6,000, depending on age and price          |
| Insurance            | $1,200–3,500 (higher with young drivers, in NY/NJ)|
| Fuel                 | $1,200–3,000                                      |
| Maintenance          | $500–1,200                                        |
| Repairs              | $0–3,000, lumpy and unavoidable                   |
| Registration, inspection, tolls | $200–800                               |
| Parking (city)       | $0–5,000+                                         |

A "cheap" $8,000 used car can easily cost $5,000 a year to run. Know the whole number before
you decide it's affordable.

** 15.4 Buying a car without being taken apart

*** The four separate negotiations

The single most important thing to understand about a dealership: they are running *four*
negotiations simultaneously, and they will blend them together deliberately so you can't tell
where you're winning and where you're losing.

1. The price of the car you're buying.
2. The value of your trade-in.
3. The financing terms.
4. The back-end products (warranty, gap insurance, paint protection, VIN etching).

*Negotiate them one at a time, in that order, and refuse to discuss the next until the
previous one is settled in writing.*

The classic maneuver is "what monthly payment are you looking for?" Never answer this. A
monthly payment can be made to look like anything by stretching the term. Answer: *"I'm
negotiating the out-the-door price. We can discuss financing after."*

*** The out-the-door price

Always negotiate the *out-the-door* number — the total including all fees, taxes, and
documentation charges — not the sticker price. Dealer fees materialize after the "agreed"
price otherwise.

Ask for it in writing, itemized, by email, before you go in.

*** Arrange financing before you walk in

Get pre-approved by a credit union or bank first. Then:

- You know your real rate, so the dealer's financing has to beat it or it loses.
- You are a cash buyer as far as the negotiation is concerned.
- Dealers often mark up the rate they get from lenders ("dealer reserve"). Sometimes they can
  genuinely beat your pre-approval, especially with a manufacturer promotion — let them try,
  but from a position where you don't need them to.

*** The finance office

After you agree on price, you're taken to a separate room to sign papers, where a different
person sells you: extended warranty, gap insurance, tire protection, paint sealant, fabric
protection, VIN etching, key replacement. This room is a significant profit center.

*Default answer to all of it: no.* Two carve-outs:

- *Gap insurance* is legitimate if you put little down and the car will be underwater —
  it covers the gap between what insurance pays and what you owe if the car is totaled. But
  buy it from your own auto insurer, where it costs a fraction of the dealer's price.
- *An extended warranty* on a used car of questionable reliability can occasionally make
  sense. Usually it doesn't, and you can always buy one later from the manufacturer.

*** The practical checklist

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
BEFORE:
[ ] Decide the maximum out-the-door price. Write it down. Tell your wife.
[ ] Get pre-approved financing from a credit union.
[ ] Research the model's reliability and common failures (Consumer Reports, owner forums)
[ ] Check pricing data (KBB, Edmunds) for what people actually pay in your area
[ ] Email 4–6 dealers asking for an out-the-door price on a specific stock number

USED CARS SPECIFICALLY:
[ ] Get the vehicle history report (Carfax / AutoCheck)
[ ] PAY FOR AN INDEPENDENT PRE-PURCHASE INSPECTION. ~$150 at a mechanic you chose.
    If the seller refuses, walk away. This single step prevents most disasters.
[ ] Check for open recalls at nhtsa.gov
[ ] Drive it properly — highway speed, hard braking, full turns, cold start

AT THE DEALER:
[ ] Negotiate out-the-door price only. Refuse monthly-payment framing.
[ ] Settle the trade-in separately, after the price is agreed
[ ] Decline everything in the finance office
[ ] READ the contract. Check the price, the rate, the term, and that nothing was added
[ ] Be genuinely willing to walk out. This is the only real leverage that exists.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Go at the end of the month*, when quotas are live. Go with time to spare and having eaten;
exhaustion is a negotiating tool used against you.

** 15.5 Leasing

*Leasing* is renting a car long-term. You pay for the depreciation during your term plus
interest, and return it.

*When leasing can make sense:* you want a new car every three years and accept that as an
ongoing cost; you drive predictable, low mileage; the car is for a business where the lease is
deductible.

*Why it usually doesn't for a family like yours:*

- You never own anything. At the end you have no asset and start again.
- Mileage limits (typically 10,000–15,000/year) with charges of $0.15–0.30 per mile over.
- Wear-and-tear charges at return, assessed by the party who benefits from finding wear.
- *Children are hard on cars.* A lease returned with car-seat marks, juice, and crayon is an
  expensive lease.
- Terminating early is punishing.

For a frum family that will drive a vehicle into the ground with eight people in it, buying
used and keeping it a decade is almost always the better arithmetic.

** 15.6 The big-family vehicle question

At some point a sedan stops working. The honest options:

| Option                  | Seats | Notes                                            |
|-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------|
| Minivan (Sienna, Odyssey, Pacifica) | 7–8 | The correct answer, and everyone resists it. Best space per dollar, easiest child access, cheapest to insure of the large options |
| Large SUV (Suburban, Expedition) | 7–8 | More expensive to buy, run, and insure. Third row often tighter than a minivan's |
| 12-passenger van        | 12    | Once you're past eight. Cheap used, expensive to run, and check your state's licensing rules |
| Two cars                | —     | Sometimes cheaper than one large one, and a second driver is genuinely useful |

Practical notes:

- *Buy used and let someone else eat the depreciation* (§15.2). A four-year-old Sienna is a
  vastly better financial decision than a new one and is the same vehicle.
- *Check car seat fitment before buying.* Three car seats across a row is a real engineering
  question and varies enormously by model. Bring your actual seats to the test drive. People
  buy a car and then discover it doesn't work.
- *Sliding doors are worth more than you think* in a tight parking spot with children.
- *Insurance:* a minivan is typically cheaper to insure than an SUV or a sedan with any
  performance pretensions.
- *Plan the purchase as a sinking fund* (§1.7). You know it's coming. Start putting money aside
  three years before the day you need it, and buy without financing.

** 15.7 Other big purchases: the general method

The same discipline applies to a renovation, furniture, appliances, a computer, a sheitel, or
anything else where the number has a comma in it.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. WAIT. Impose a rule: anything over $X waits 72 hours (or a week) before purchase.
   A remarkable fraction of intended purchases simply evaporate. Free money.

2. Define the requirement before you shop, in writing. "A washing machine that
   handles 8 loads a week and fits a 27-inch space." Not "a washing machine."
   Shopping without a spec is how you end up paying for features you'll never use.

3. Research the CATEGORY, then the item. Twenty minutes on what actually matters
   in this product class, then compare.

4. Buy quality where it's used daily and lasts; buy cheap where it isn't.
   A mattress, a chair you sit in for hours, shoes, a laptop you work on: buy well.
   A guest room lamp: buy cheap.

5. Compare total cost, not price. Energy use, consumables, repairability, expected life.

6. Negotiate, even in America. Appliance stores, furniture stores, jewelers, and
   any independent business will usually move on price or throw in delivery.
   The phrase is "is that the best you can do?" and then silence.

7. Never finance a depreciating consumer good at interest. If it needs 0% for 24
   months to be affordable, it isn't affordable — with the narrow exception of a
   genuine 0% promotion you could pay off in cash today and choose not to.

8. Decline the extended warranty (§17.5).
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** The frum-specific version

Seforim, silver, a sheitel, yom tov clothing, a sukkah, a shtender — these are purchases with
a social and religious dimension as well as a financial one, and pretending otherwise is
useless. Two honest observations:

1. *Things that last decades and get used constantly are worth buying well.* A good set of
   seforim, a decent sukkah, quality shabbos clothing that survives a hundred wearings. The
   per-use cost is trivial and the quality is felt weekly.
2. *Things bought for how they look to others are worth examining.* Not condemning — examining.
   There's a real difference between hiddur mitzvah and keeping up with a neighbor, and only
   you can locate that line in your own house. The useful question isn't "can we afford it"
   but "if nobody would ever see this, would I still want it at this price?"

* PART 16 — WHEN THINGS GO WRONG

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* When the income stops, the thing that costs the most is delay.

File for unemployment the same day, because benefits often start from the day you file rather
than the day you lost the job. Sort out health insurance immediately — losing a job opens a
special window to buy coverage, and the window closes. Call every creditor *before* you miss
a payment, because hardship programs are available then and much harder to get afterward.

If there isn't enough to go around, pay in this order: food, housing, utilities, the car you
need to get to work, taxes. Credit cards come *last* — even though they call the most.

Never take a payday loan. Not once, not for a small amount. At 300% interest a temporary
shortfall becomes a permanent one, and that's the design rather than an accident.

And if the debt genuinely cannot be repaid within five years, bankruptcy exists for exactly
that. It protects retirement savings, it stops the phone calls the day you file, and eight
years of hopeless minimum payments is worse for your wife and children than a fresh start.

The most expensive thing in a money crisis is usually embarrassment. Open the mail. Make the
call. Tell your wife.
#+END_QUOTE

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
Most personal finance writing assumes everything proceeds smoothly. It often doesn't. Jobs
end, health fails, businesses close, and sometimes a household simply cannot pay what it
owes.

This part exists because the worst outcomes in a financial crisis usually come not from the
crisis itself but from the response: paying the wrong creditor first, borrowing at 300% to
cover a 6% obligation, ignoring mail, or waiting until every option has expired. There are
right moves. Here they are.
#+END_QUOTE

** 16.1 Job loss: the first two weeks

*** Day one

1. *File for unemployment immediately.* Not next week. Benefits often start from the filing
   date, not the job-loss date, and some states have a waiting week. File online the day you
   learn.
2. *Understand your severance, if any*, before signing. Severance agreements typically waive
   your right to sue; that may be fine, but read it, and know that you can usually take a few
   days. If the terms are significant, an employment lawyer's one-hour consultation is cheap.
3. *Handle health insurance.* You have options and a deadline (§6.3): COBRA (expensive, keeps
   your plan and doctors), an ACA marketplace plan (job loss is a *qualifying life event*
   opening a special enrollment period, and your now-lower income may qualify you for large
   subsidies), a spouse's employer plan (also a qualifying event), or Medicaid. Compare before
   defaulting to COBRA, which is usually the most expensive.
4. *Roll over or leave the 401(k). Do not cash it out* (§8.11).
5. *Check for unused PTO payout* and any vested equity deadlines (§10.9) — option exercise
   windows after termination are often 90 days and unforgiving.

*** Week one: triage the budget

Cut into three lists, and be ruthless about which list things go on:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  MUST PAY (in this order):
    Food
    Housing (rent/mortgage)
    Utilities
    Health insurance
    Transportation needed to find work
    Minimum payments on anything secured by something you need (the car)

  PAUSE:
    Retirement contributions (temporarily — restart the moment you're working)
    Extra debt payments beyond minimums
    Sinking funds you can defer (not the medical deductible)
    Every discretionary category

  CALL AND NEGOTIATE:
    Every creditor — before you miss a payment, not after
#+END_EXAMPLE

*** The counterintuitive part: call creditors early

People avoid this call out of shame, and the shame is expensive. Lenders have hardship
programs — forbearance, deferred payments, reduced interest, restructured plans — and they are
far more available *before* you default than after.

The script: "I've lost my job. I want to keep this account current. What hardship options do
you have?" Then be quiet. Write down who you spoke to, when, and what was agreed. Get it in
writing if you can.

Mortgage servicers in particular have formal loss-mitigation obligations, and federal rules
require them to evaluate you for options before foreclosing. Contact them immediately.

*** Where to get money, best to worst

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. Emergency fund                        ← this is what it was for
2. Taxable brokerage account
3. Roth IRA contributions (not earnings) — tax and penalty free (§8.10)
4. A gemach or family loan, documented (§3.10)
5. HELOC or home equity, if you have it and a plan
6. 401(k) loan — repayable, but becomes due if you leave the job. Careful.
7. Credit card at 25%
8. 401(k) hardship withdrawal — tax + penalty + permanent loss of compounding
   ────────────────── everything below this line is a mistake ──────────────────
9. Payday loan, auto title loan, pawn shop
#+END_EXAMPLE

Never, under any circumstances, cross into 9. A payday loan at 300%+ turns a temporary
shortfall into a permanent one, and that is not an exaggeration of the mechanism — it's the
business model.

** 16.2 When you genuinely cannot pay everything

*** The order of priorities

Not all debts are equally urgent. When there isn't enough, this is the order that minimizes
damage:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. Anything that keeps you housed, fed, warm, and able to get to work
2. Child support / alimony if applicable (severe penalties, non-dischargeable)
3. Taxes (the IRS has powers ordinary creditors don't — liens, levies, wage garnishment
   without a court order)
4. Secured debts on things you need (car loan, mortgage)
5. Insurance premiums (letting coverage lapse can be catastrophic)
6. Unsecured debts (credit cards, medical bills, personal loans)
#+END_EXAMPLE

Note where credit cards sit: *last*. People pay the aggressive caller first, which is exactly
backwards. An unsecured creditor can hurt your credit and eventually sue. A mortgage servicer
can take your house. Rank by consequence, not by volume of phone calls.

*** Medical debt specifically

Medical debt behaves differently from other debt and you have more leverage than you think:

- *Always request an itemized bill.* Error rates are high — duplicate charges, services never
  rendered, wrong billing codes.
- *Ask about financial assistance / charity care.* Nonprofit hospitals are legally required to
  have a policy, and many people who qualify never ask. Ask in writing.
- *Negotiate.* Hospitals routinely accept substantially less, especially for a prompt lump-sum
  payment. Offering 30–50% of the balance is a normal opening.
- *Ask for a 0% payment plan.* Standard practice.
- *Recent credit reporting changes are in your favor:* paid medical collections are removed
  from credit reports, unpaid medical collections under $500 are excluded, and there's a
  one-year delay before medical debt appears at all. Rules have been in flux, so verify — but
  the direction has been consistently toward less credit damage from medical bills.
- *Never put medical debt on a credit card.* You would convert a 0%, negotiable, weakly-
  reported debt into a 25%, non-negotiable, fully-reported one. This is the single most common
  medical-debt mistake.

*** Collections and your rights

When a debt is sold to a *collection agency*, federal law (the Fair Debt Collection Practices
Act) gives you specific rights:

- *Debt validation.* Within 30 days of first contact, send a written request for validation.
  They must stop collection until they provide it. A meaningful fraction of collection
  accounts are wrong — wrong person, wrong amount, already paid, or beyond the statute of
  limitations.
- *They may not* call before 8am or after 9pm, call you at work after you tell them not to,
  use threats or obscenity, or claim they'll take actions they cannot legally take.
- *You can demand in writing that they stop contacting you.* They must then only contact you to
  say what specific action they're taking.
- *Statute of limitations.* Debts become time-barred after a state-defined period (typically
  3–6 years). A time-barred debt can't be successfully sued on — *but making a payment or even
  acknowledging it in writing can restart the clock.* Never pay a very old collection without
  understanding this.
- *Get any settlement in writing before paying.* "Paid in full" versus "settled for less" is a
  meaningful difference on your report, and a verbal agreement with a collector is worth
  nothing.

Send everything by certified mail and keep copies. This is one of those areas where a paper
trail is the whole game.

*** Debt settlement companies

Be careful. The standard model has you stop paying creditors and pay into an escrow account
while they negotiate. In the meantime your credit is destroyed, creditors may sue, and fees
are substantial. Sometimes it works out; often it leaves people worse off than either paying
or filing bankruptcy.

*The legitimate alternative* is a *nonprofit credit counseling agency* (look for NFCC
membership, =nfcc.org=). They offer a *Debt Management Plan* — one consolidated monthly
payment, often with reduced interest rates negotiated with creditors. Initial counseling is
usually free.

** 16.3 Bankruptcy: what it actually is

Bankruptcy carries enormous stigma, particularly in a close community, and that stigma keeps
people in a state of prolonged ruin for years longer than necessary. It is a legal process
that exists precisely for situations that cannot be solved by paying.

*** The two kinds for individuals

**** Chapter 7 — liquidation

- Most unsecured debts (credit cards, medical, personal loans) are *discharged* — legally
  erased.
- Non-exempt assets can be sold to pay creditors. But *exemptions* — which vary substantially
  by state — often protect a home up to a certain equity amount, a vehicle, retirement
  accounts, tools of a trade, and household goods. Many filers lose nothing at all.
- *Retirement accounts are generally fully protected in bankruptcy.* This is a major reason
  never to drain a 401(k) trying to avoid a bankruptcy you end up filing anyway — you spend
  protected money to delay the inevitable.
- Requires passing a *means test* based on income.
- Takes about 3–6 months. Stays on your credit report for 10 years.

**** Chapter 13 — reorganization

- You keep your assets and repay a portion of debts over 3–5 years under a court-approved
  plan.
- Used when you have income and want to keep a house you're behind on — Chapter 13 can stop a
  foreclosure and let you catch up arrears over time.
- Stays on your credit report for 7 years.

*** What bankruptcy does NOT erase

- Most student loans (a high bar, though slightly less impossible than it used to be)
- Child support and alimony
- Most tax debt (some older income tax can qualify)
- Debts from fraud
- Court fines and criminal restitution

*** The immediate effect: the automatic stay

The moment you file, an *automatic stay* takes effect. Collection calls stop. Lawsuits stop.
Wage garnishment stops. Foreclosure stops. This is often the single most valuable thing about
filing and it happens on day one.

*** When it's the right answer

Rough test: if you could not repay your unsecured debts in five years even with a reasonable
budget, bankruptcy deserves serious consideration. Spending eight years making minimum
payments that never reduce the balance is worse for you and your family than a discharge and
a fresh start — and materially worse for your children, who live through those eight years.

*** How to approach it

- *Most bankruptcy attorneys offer a free consultation.* Have one. Getting information costs
  nothing and commits you to nothing.
- *Credit counseling is a legal prerequisite* before filing.
- *Don't take actions before filing that could complicate it*: transferring assets to
  relatives, paying one creditor preferentially, running up new debt. All of these can be
  unwound by the trustee and some can be treated as fraud. Talk to the attorney *first*.
- *Credit recovers faster than people expect.* Many people are approved for a mortgage 2–4
  years after a Chapter 7 discharge, because the debt is gone and the ratios work.

** 16.4 Foreclosure and eviction

*** If you can't pay the mortgage

1. *Contact the servicer immediately.* Federal rules require them to evaluate you for
   loss-mitigation options before foreclosing, and options include forbearance, repayment
   plans, loan modification, and — if you must exit — a short sale or deed in lieu, both of
   which are far less damaging than foreclosure.
2. *Get a HUD-approved housing counselor.* Free. Find one at =hud.gov= or by calling 800-569-4287.
   They know the programs and deal with servicers routinely.
3. *Beware "foreclosure rescue" operations.* Anyone charging an upfront fee to save your home,
   or asking you to sign over the deed, is running a scam on a distressed person.

*** If you can't pay rent

- *Talk to the landlord before you're late.* A payment plan is usually cheaper for them than
  turnover and an eviction proceeding.
- *Know your state's process.* Eviction requires legal steps and notice periods. A landlord
  cannot legally change the locks, remove your belongings, or shut off utilities without a
  court order in any state.
- *Emergency rental assistance* programs exist in most areas — search "[your county] emergency
  rental assistance." Also HEAP for utilities (§5.1).
- *An eviction on your record follows you* and makes future renting difficult. This is worth
  real effort to avoid, including borrowing from family, which is the thing people are most
  reluctant to do and should do first.

** 16.5 Identity theft and account takeover

If your identity is stolen — not a single fraudulent charge, but someone opening accounts as
you:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1. Go to identitytheft.gov. It generates a personalized recovery plan and an official
   FTC Identity Theft Report, which creditors are required to accept.
2. Freeze your credit at all three bureaus (§3.7). And place a fraud alert.
3. Contact each institution where a fraudulent account was opened. In writing.
4. File a police report; some creditors require one.
5. Change every password, starting with your email — email is the master key that
   resets everything else. Turn on app-based two-factor authentication.
6. Request your free credit reports and dispute every fraudulent item in writing.
7. If your Social Security number was used for employment or tax fraud, contact the
   IRS Identity Protection unit and get an Identity Protection PIN.
8. Keep a log: every call, every date, every name, every reference number.
#+END_EXAMPLE

*Prevention beats all of it, and it's free:* a credit freeze on every member of the household,
including the children (§3.7), plus app-based two-factor authentication on email and banking.
Fifteen minutes, once.

** 16.6 Divorce and financial separation

Included briefly, without commentary, because a guide that pretends this never happens is not
a useful guide.

- *Get your own financial information first.* Know what accounts exist, what's owed, and where
  the documents are. The person who doesn't know the finances is at a permanent disadvantage
  in the process, and it is usually the spouse who wasn't handling them.
- *Establish credit in your own name*, if you haven't (§3.6). This is why individual credit
  matters even in a fully joint household.
- *Joint debts remain joint* regardless of what a divorce agreement says. A creditor is not
  bound by your settlement; if your name is on the account, the creditor can pursue you. Debts
  need to be refinanced or closed, not merely assigned.
- *Retirement accounts can be divided* without tax consequence via a *QDRO* (Qualified Domestic
  Relations Order) for employer plans, or a transfer incident to divorce for IRAs. Doing it
  any other way triggers taxes and penalties.
- *Update every beneficiary designation* (§13.3). This is forgotten with remarkable regularity,
  and the ex-spouse named on a life insurance policy receives the money.
- *In the frum world*, the civil process runs alongside a get, and financial and halachic
  matters can become entangled in ways that harm people. A rav and an attorney who both
  understand this landscape are not optional.

** 16.7 A note on shame

The most expensive part of a financial crisis is usually the delay, and the delay is almost
always caused by embarrassment.

People don't open the mail. They don't tell their spouse. They don't call the mortgage
company. They don't apply for benefits they qualify for. They don't ask their rav, who has
almost certainly helped families through exactly this and would help theirs. Every one of
those silences costs money, and the cost compounds.

In a community where everyone knows everyone, this pressure is stronger, not weaker. Worth
naming plainly: financial trouble is extremely common, it is usually caused by something
ordinary — a job loss, a medical event, a business that didn't work — and hiding it has never
once made it smaller.

Open the mail. Make the call. Tell your wife. Everything in this part works better early.

* PART 17 — SCAMS, FRAUD, AND BAD DEALS

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Almost every scam has the same fingerprints. It's urgent. The returns are
guaranteed. You can't quite explain how the money gets made. And they want paying in a way
that can't be reversed — a wire, Zelle, gift cards, crypto.

Three sentences that will save you real money: no legitimate business has ever needed payment
in gift cards. No bank will ever ask you to move your money somewhere to "keep it safe" —
that request *is* the theft. The IRS sends letters; it does not telephone you and threaten
arrest.

Then there's the version aimed specifically at people like you. It arrives through your own
community, brought by someone you daven with, who often genuinely believes in it because he
was paid early with somebody else's money. Knowing the man is not the same as checking the
investment. It never has been, and every large community fraud in history has run on exactly
that confusion.

Have a stated rule — *"I don't put money into private deals"* — so that saying no is a policy
rather than an insult to anyone.
#+END_QUOTE

** 17.1 The universal tells

Almost every financial fraud in history shares a handful of features. Learn these six and you
will catch the overwhelming majority of them without knowing anything about the specific
scheme.

1. *Urgency.* "This closes Friday." "Only three spots left." "You must act now." Urgency exists
   to prevent you from thinking, checking, or asking someone. Legitimate opportunities
   survive a week of consideration. *This is the strongest single tell.*
2. *Guaranteed returns.* Nothing is guaranteed except FDIC-insured deposits and US Treasury
   securities. "Guaranteed 12%" is a sentence that only appears in frauds.
3. *Returns too good, too smooth.* Not just high returns — *consistent* high returns. Real
   investments fluctuate. Madoff's tell, visible for years to anyone who looked, was that his
   returns were implausibly steady.
4. *Complexity you can't follow.* If you can't explain how the money is made in two sentences
   to your wife, you don't understand it, and you should not be in it. Complexity is often
   deliberate cover.
5. *Pressure to recruit others.* The defining feature of a pyramid scheme. If your return
   depends on bringing in more people, the money comes from the new people, not from any
   business.
6. *Unusual payment methods.* Wire transfer, gift cards, crypto, Zelle, cash. All chosen
   specifically because they are irreversible. No legitimate business requires payment in
   Apple gift cards. Ever. For anything.

** 17.2 Common consumer scams

*** Impersonation of authority

Someone calls claiming to be the IRS, Social Security, Medicare, a police department, or
immigration, demanding immediate payment and threatening arrest.

*The truth:* the IRS initiates contact by mail. Social Security does not call to threaten. No
government agency accepts gift cards. Anyone threatening immediate arrest over the phone is a
criminal.

Hang up. If you're unsure, look up the agency's real number yourself and call it.

*** The "your account has been compromised" call

Someone claiming to be from your bank's fraud department says there's suspicious activity and
you need to move your money to a "safe account," or send it to yourself via Zelle, or read
them a code.

*The truth:* your bank will never ask you to move money to protect it. The "safe account" is
theirs. The code they want you to read is the two-factor code that lets them into your
account.

*Never* give a verification code to anyone who called you. That code exists specifically to
stop the person asking for it.

*** Grandparent / family emergency scam

A call: a family member is in jail, in an accident, stranded abroad, and needs money wired
immediately. Increasingly these use AI-generated voice cloning from social media audio, which
makes them terrifyingly convincing.

*The defense:* agree on a family code word. Or hang up and call the person directly on their
known number. The urgency is manufactured precisely to prevent that call.

*** Check overpayment

Someone buys something from you, sends a check for more than the price, asks you to refund the
difference. The check clears — apparently — then bounces weeks later. Your refund is gone and
the bank reverses the deposit.

*The rule:* "the check cleared" does not mean the check is good. Funds availability and final
settlement are different things, and the gap between them is where this scam lives.

*** Romance, job, and rental scams

- *Job scams*: an employer sends you a check to buy equipment; the check is fake. Or a "job"
  that involves receiving and forwarding money — that's money laundering and you're the mule,
  criminally liable.
- *Rental scams*: a listing for an apartment the "landlord" doesn't own, requiring a deposit
  before viewing. Never pay before seeing a unit and verifying ownership.
- *Marketplace scams*: overpayment, fake payment confirmations, shipping tricks.

*** Charity fraud

Painfully relevant in a community that gives generously. Fake collectors, fake causes, fake
emergency appeals — sometimes using real names and real photos of real people in need.

*Before giving meaningfully:*
- Verify 501(c)(3) status at =irs.gov/charities-non-profits/tax-exempt-organization-search=
- Check =charitynavigator.org= or =guidestar.org= for established organizations
- For a local collector, ask for a letter from a recognized rav or organization — legitimate
  meshulachim carry them and expect to be asked
- Be especially careful with urgent appeals circulating on WhatsApp. Emotional urgency plus
  viral distribution is the exact shape of a fraud, and it's also the exact shape of a genuine
  emergency, which is what makes it effective.

** 17.3 Investment scams

*** Ponzi schemes

Returns to early investors are paid from later investors' money. No actual investment
generates the returns. Collapses when new money slows.

*Tells:* consistent above-market returns regardless of market conditions; difficulty
withdrawing (or pressure to reinvest instead); no independent custodian holding the assets;
account statements produced by the manager rather than a third party; an auditor nobody has
heard of.

*The Madoff lesson:* he ran the largest Ponzi in history for decades, primarily through
*affinity networks* — Jewish community organizations, country clubs, charities. Sophisticated
people, extensive due diligence, and it still worked. It worked *because* of the trust
network, not despite it. Read §17.4.

*** Pyramid schemes and MLMs

*Pyramid scheme*: returns come from recruiting. Illegal.

*Multi-level marketing (MLM)*: technically legal, sells real products, but income depends
heavily on recruiting a "downline." The FTC and multiple independent analyses consistently
find that the large majority of participants lose money.

MLMs target exactly the demographic of a young mother in a tight-knit community: flexible
hours, work from home, social selling to friends. The pitch is warm and the arithmetic is
cold. If someone shows you an income disclosure statement, read the *median*, not the top
line — it is usually a few hundred dollars a year before expenses.

*** Crypto scams

- *Pig butchering*: a long-con relationship (often starting as a wrong-number text or a dating
  app match) building trust over weeks, then introducing a "trading platform" that shows fake
  gains. Withdrawals require ever-larger "fees." Everything is fake. Losses are typically
  total and typically large.
- *Fake exchanges and wallets.*
- *"Send 1 ETH, get 2 back"* giveaway scams, usually impersonating a celebrity.
- *Rug pulls*: a new token pumped by its creators who then sell everything.

*The rule:* crypto transactions are irreversible and largely unregulated. There is no fraud
department. There is no chargeback. Whatever you send is gone.

*** Precious metals and "safe haven" pitches

Aggressive advertising, often on talk radio and often invoking economic collapse, pushing
gold and silver coins at enormous markups over spot price — sometimes 30–50%. Frequently
paired with a pitch to roll your IRA into a "gold IRA" with high fees.

If you want gold exposure, a low-cost ETF costs a fraction of a percent. Physical coins from a
telemarketer are a bad deal at the moment of purchase.

** 17.4 Affinity fraud: the one aimed at you

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*Affinity fraud* is fraud that spreads through a trusted community — religious, ethnic,
professional. The fraudster is a member, or convincingly appears to be. The trust that makes
a community function is precisely the mechanism the fraud uses.

Orthodox Jewish communities have been targeted repeatedly and successfully. So have Mormon,
evangelical, Chinese-American, Haitian-American, and many other tight communities. This is
not a statement about your community; it is a statement about how trust works.
#+END_QUOTE

*** Why it works so well

- The recommendation comes from someone you genuinely trust — a friend, a relative, someone
  from shul, sometimes a communal figure.
- Skepticism feels like an insult, or like a lack of achdus.
- Early investors are paid (with later investors' money) and become enthusiastic, sincere
  promoters. They are not lying; they genuinely believe it, which is what makes them
  effective.
- Losses are underreported because victims are embarrassed and don't want to damage
  relationships — so the scheme runs longer than it otherwise could.
- Real-estate syndications, private lending funds, and business investments are common
  vehicles, because they're plausible, illiquid, and hard to value independently.

*** The rules that protect you

1. *A relationship is not due diligence.* The fact that he davens in your shul tells you
   nothing about the investment. Nothing. It is not evidence. It is the delivery mechanism.
2. *Verify registration.* Check the individual and the firm at =brokercheck.finra.org= and
   =adviserinfo.sec.gov=. It takes two minutes and it's free. Unregistered people selling
   securities is itself a violation.
3. *Demand independent custody.* Your money should be held at a real, third-party institution
   that sends *you* statements directly. If the manager holds the assets and produces the
   statements, you have no way to know anything is real. This one control would have stopped
   Madoff.
4. *Never invest more than you can lose entirely* in anything private, illiquid, or
   unregistered.
5. *"I have a rule that I don't invest in private deals."* Have it as a *policy*, stated in
   advance, so declining isn't a judgment about the person. This sentence is the single most
   useful social tool in this document.
6. *If there's pressure after you decline*, you've learned something important about what the
   relationship actually is.
7. *Talk to your rav.* Many rabbanim have watched this happen repeatedly and have views.

*** The sentence to remember

If someone says "this is only being offered to a few people in the community, and you can't
tell anyone" — that is not exclusivity. That is a fraudster preventing the people who would
recognize the scheme from hearing about it.

** 17.5 Legal products that are bad deals

Not fraud. Just poor value, sold hard.

| Product                    | The problem                                          |
|----------------------------+------------------------------------------------------|
| Whole/universal life to the young | See §6.4. Huge commissions, poor returns      |
| Variable annuities to the young | High fees, unnecessary complexity, surrender charges |
| Timeshares                 | Nearly impossible to sell, perpetual fees            |
| Rent-to-own furniture      | Effective interest rates in the triple digits        |
| Payday loans               | 200–400% APR. A debt trap by design                  |
| Auto title loans           | Lose the car; the terms are predatory                |
| Credit repair services     | Charge for what you can do free (§3.6)               |
| Student loan "forgiveness" services | Charge for free federal programs             |
| Extended warranties        | Priced for the seller's profit                       |
| Tax refund anticipation loans | High-cost loans against your own refund           |
| Dealer add-ons (paint sealant, fabric protection, VIN etching) | Pure margin |
| Actively managed funds at 1%+ | You're paying to underperform (§7.3)              |
| "Gold IRA" telemarketers   | Enormous markups plus high custodial fees            |

** 17.6 If you've been hit

1. *Stop all further payments immediately.* Scams escalate — the "fee to release your funds" is
   always the next step, and it is always another theft.
2. *Contact your bank or card issuer immediately.* Credit card charges can often be disputed.
   Wires, Zelle, and crypto usually cannot be recovered, but report anyway and fast — very
   occasionally a wire can be recalled within hours.
3. *Report it:*
   - FTC: =reportfraud.ftc.gov=
   - FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center: =ic3.gov=
   - Your state attorney general
   - SEC (investment fraud): =sec.gov/tcr=
   - Local police, for a report number that creditors may require
4. *Freeze your credit* at all three bureaus if any personal information was exposed (§3.7).
5. *Change passwords*, starting with email — email is the master key to everything else.
6. *Do not pay a "recovery service"* that contacts you afterward promising to get your money
   back. That is a second scam, run against known victims from lists that circulate among
   fraudsters. It is depressingly common.
7. *Tell people.* The shame is the fraudster's asset. Victims who stay quiet let the scheme
   keep running through their community. Speaking up is uncomfortable and it protects other
   families.

* PART 18 — THE PLAN: WHAT TO ACTUALLY DO

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*IN ONE MINUTE.* Stop reading. Do these, in this order:

1. Put aside $1,000–2,000 in cash.
2. Collect any employer retirement match. Free money; it beats everything else.
3. Pay off anything charging more than 8% interest.
4. Build the cash cushion up to three to six months of expenses.
5. Fund Roth retirement accounts — including one in your own name, off your wife's
   income — while your tax rate is at its lifetime low.
6. Everything else.

The rest of this part expands that into what to do this week, this month, this year, and over
five years. Start with the "this week" list. It's about three hours of work and most of it is
filling in web forms.
#+END_QUOTE

Everything above is knowledge. This part is the sequence. Work down it in order. Don't skip
ahead to the interesting parts.

** 18.1 This week (about three hours, total)

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Download 3 months of bank transactions. Categorize them. (§1.5)
[ ] Cancel every subscription you find that you'd forgotten about.
[ ] Open a high-yield savings account at an online bank. (§1.8)
[ ] Set up the 3-account structure: Bills / Spending / Savings. (§1.4)
[ ] Freeze your credit at all three bureaus. Yours, your wife's, your daughter's. (§3.7)
[ ] Pull your free credit reports at annualcreditreport.com. Check for errors. (§3.2)
[ ] Turn off overdraft coverage; set a low-balance alert. (§2.6)
[ ] Create a Social Security account at ssa.gov/myaccount for both of you. (§4.5)
[ ] Check the beneficiary on every account either of you has. (§13.3)
[ ] Get renters insurance if you don't have it. It's $15/month. (§6.6)
[ ] Turn on app-based two-factor authentication on email and banking. (§2.8)
#+END_EXAMPLE

** 18.2 This month

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Sit down together and have the money conversation. (§11.1)
[ ] Set a monthly money meeting. Put it in the calendar, recurring. (§1.6)
[ ] Compute your sinking fund total. Set up the named accounts. (§1.7)
[ ] Start the emergency fund. Automate a transfer, however small. (§1.8)
[ ] If your wife's employer offers a 401(k) match, contribute enough to get all of it. (§8.3)
[ ] Check her W-2 Box 1 vs Box 3 to see whether anything is going in pretax. (§4.3)
[ ] Run the IRS Withholding Estimator. Fix the W-4 if it's wrong. (§4.4)
[ ] Compute your net worth. Write it down with today's date. (§1.9)
[ ] If you have no credit history, start building it. (§3.6)
[ ] Price term life insurance on both of you. You are as cheap now as you will ever be. (§6.4)
[ ] Check whether her employer offers voluntary short-term disability. If so, ELECT IT
    at open enrollment — you cannot add it once pregnant. (§11.7)
[ ] Find out whether your state has paid family leave, and whether YOU can claim it
    as the father. In NY and NJ you probably can. (§11.7)
#+END_EXAMPLE

** 18.3 This year

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Get the emergency fund to one month of expenses. Then keep going.
[ ] Pay off anything above 8% interest. (§3.9)
[ ] Open a Roth IRA for each of you — including a SPOUSAL Roth IRA for you. (§8.6)
[ ] Buy ONE target-date index fund inside it. Then verify it actually bought. (§7.5)
[ ] Automate a monthly contribution, even if it's $50.
[ ] File your taxes. File even if you think you don't need to — the refundable
    credits are the point. Check EITC, CTC, Saver's Credit, AOTC. (§4.9)
[ ] Get a will, guardianship designation, POA, and healthcare proxy. (§13.2)
[ ] Ask your rav about the halachic will. (§13.6)
[ ] Ask your rav about your maaser calculation basis, and set up the system. (§12.6)
[ ] Write the "if something happens" document. (§13.7)
[ ] Verify what benefits you qualify for, and apply. Honestly. (§5.1)
[ ] Map your benefit cliffs so future income decisions are informed. (§5.2)
[ ] Start a car sinking fund. The minivan is coming whether you fund it or not. (§15.6)
[ ] Ask your parents where their will, POA, and healthcare proxy are. (§6.11)
#+END_EXAMPLE

** 18.4 The next five years

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Finish the CS path. This is the highest-leverage financial project you have. (§10.2)
[ ] Build a portfolio of finished, deployed projects. Network deliberately.
[ ] Keep funding the Roth every year you can — your tax rate will never be lower. (§8.4)
[ ] Grow the emergency fund toward 6 months. (§1.8)
[ ] Start a chasunah/simcha fund per child. $130/month, started now, is the whole trick. (§12.4)
[ ] Model the transition year BEFORE it arrives. (§10.8)
[ ] When income arrives: fix withholding, report to benefit programs, don't raise
    your lifestyle for six months.
[ ] Buy disability insurance in your first year of professional work. (§6.5)
[ ] Increase the savings rate by half of every raise, automatically. (§10.8)
[ ] If aliyah is real: talk to a US-Israel cross-border accountant BEFORE moving. (§14.9)
[ ] Revisit housing. Rent vs buy with real numbers, not neighborhood pressure. (§9.1)
[ ] When an offer includes equity: understand the cliff, the vesting, and the refresh
    grant before you accept — and plan to sell RSUs as they vest. (§10.9)
[ ] Buy the family vehicle used, from a sinking fund, not on a seven-year loan. (§15.3)
#+END_EXAMPLE

** 18.5 The order of operations, on one line

When you have a spare dollar and don't know where it goes, this is the sequence:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
1.  $1,000–2,000 starter emergency fund
2.  Capture the full employer 401(k) match          ← free money, do this first
3.  Pay off all debt above ~8% interest
4.  Emergency fund to 3–6 months of expenses
5.  Max the HSA, if you have one                    ← best tax treatment available
6.  Max Roth IRAs (both of you — spousal IRA)       ← while your tax rate is low
7.  Increase 401(k) toward the annual maximum
8.  Pay off debt between 5–8%
9.  Taxable brokerage account: chasunah fund, house down payment, general investing
10. 529 plans, if education is a real destination
11. Then, and only then, think about lifestyle
#+END_EXAMPLE
