** 18.6 The annual rhythm

| When            | What                                                        |
|-----------------+-------------------------------------------------------------|
| Every month     | Twenty-minute money meeting. Review spending, adjust         |
| Every quarter   | Update net worth. Rebalance if needed. Check credit report   |
| January         | New year's contribution limits. Reset FSA. Deductible resets |
| Jan–April       | File taxes. Fund IRAs for the prior year (deadline: April 15)|
| Open enrollment | Re-choose health insurance. Don't auto-renew blindly         |
| Every birthday  | Re-check life insurance amount and beneficiaries             |
| Every raise     | Increase automatic savings by half the raise                 |
| Every baby      | Get an SSN. Add to insurance within the window. Update will. Freeze the child's credit. Recheck benefit eligibility |
| Elul            | Review the "if something happens" document. Review insurance |
| Every 2–3 years | Shop auto and home insurance. Loyalty is punished            |
| Every 3–5 years | Review the will and estate documents                         |

** 18.7 The five sentences worth remembering when everything else fades

1. *Save first, automatically, before you can see the money.*
2. *Never carry a credit card balance.*
3. *Buy cheap index funds and don't touch them.*
4. *Insure what would ruin you; self-insure what would annoy you.*
5. *Your income is the main lever, and it's the one nobody talks about.*
* APPENDIX A — GLOSSARY

Every term used in this document, defined in one line.

** Banking and cash

- *ACH* :: Automated Clearing House. The US system for electronic bank-to-bank transfers.
- *APY* :: Annual Percentage Yield. Interest earned in a year, including compounding. The
  number to compare across savings accounts.
- *CD* :: Certificate of Deposit. Money locked for a fixed term at a fixed rate.
- *Compound interest* :: Interest earned on interest already earned.
- *FDIC* :: Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. Insures bank deposits up to $250,000 per
  depositor, per bank, per ownership category.
- *HYSA* :: High-Yield Savings Account. An ordinary savings account paying a competitive rate.
- *NCUA* :: The credit-union equivalent of the FDIC. Same limits.
- *NSF fee* :: Non-Sufficient Funds fee. Charged when a payment is declined for lack of money.
- *Overdraft* :: Spending more than the account holds.
- *Routing number* :: A nine-digit code identifying a bank. Public.
- *Wire transfer* :: Fast, irreversible bank-to-bank transfer. Costs money. Cannot be undone.
- *I bond* :: A US savings bond whose rate tracks inflation. Cannot lose value. $10,000/person/year.
- *Money market fund* :: A brokerage fund holding very short-term safe debt. Not FDIC insured, but very safe.
- *T-bill* :: A short-term loan to the US government. Interest is exempt from state and local tax.

** Credit and debt

- *Amortization* :: The schedule by which a loan is paid down over time.
- *APR* :: Annual Percentage Rate. The yearly cost of borrowing, including some fees.
- *Authorized user* :: Someone added to another person's credit card account.
- *Avalanche method* :: Paying off debts highest-interest-rate first. Mathematically optimal.
- *Credit bureau* :: Equifax, Experian, or TransUnion. Companies that maintain credit reports.
- *Credit freeze* :: A free block preventing new credit from being opened in your name.
- *Credit limit* :: The maximum you can owe on a credit card.
- *Credit report* :: Your borrowing history, held by a credit bureau.
- *Credit score* :: A number (300–850) summarizing your credit report. FICO is the main model.
- *Credit utilization* :: The percentage of your credit limits currently in use. Keep under 30%.
- *DTI* :: Debt-to-Income ratio. Monthly debt payments divided by gross monthly income.
- *Grace period* :: The window between a credit card statement and its due date, during which
  paying in full means zero interest.
- *Hard inquiry* :: A credit check from applying for credit. Small temporary score effect.
- *Minimum payment* :: The smallest payment that keeps a card current. Designed to keep you in
  debt for decades.
- *Revolving credit* :: Credit you can repeatedly borrow and repay, like a credit card.
- *Secured card* :: A credit card backed by a cash deposit. For building credit from zero.
- *Snowball method* :: Paying off debts smallest-balance-first. Psychologically effective.
- *Soft inquiry* :: Checking your own credit. Harmless.
- *Automatic stay* :: The moment you file bankruptcy, all collection, lawsuits, and garnishment stop.
- *Chapter 7* :: Bankruptcy that erases most unsecured debt. Retirement accounts are generally protected.
- *Chapter 13* :: Bankruptcy that reorganizes debt into a 3–5 year repayment plan. Can stop a foreclosure.
- *Collection agency* :: A company that buys or is assigned defaulted debt and pursues it.
- *Debt validation* :: Your right to demand written proof of a debt within 30 days of a collector's first contact.
- *Debt Management Plan (DMP)* :: A consolidated payment plan arranged by a nonprofit credit counselor.
- *Statute of limitations* :: The period after which a debt cannot be successfully sued on. Making a payment can restart it.

** Taxes

- *ACTC* :: Additional Child Tax Credit. The refundable portion of the Child Tax Credit.
- *AGI* :: Adjusted Gross Income. Income minus above-the-line deductions. Gatekeeper number.
- *Above-the-line deduction* :: A deduction you get even if you take the standard deduction.
- *AOTC* :: American Opportunity Tax Credit. Up to $2,500/year for the first four years of
  higher education. 40% refundable.
- *Capital gain* :: Profit from selling an investment for more than you paid.
- *Cost basis* :: What you originally paid for an investment.
- *Credit (tax)* :: Reduces tax owed dollar for dollar. Worth more than a deduction.
- *CTC* :: Child Tax Credit. Per-child credit, partially refundable.
- *Deduction* :: Reduces the income you're taxed on.
- *EITC* :: Earned Income Tax Credit. Large refundable credit for working families.
- *Effective tax rate* :: Total tax divided by total income. What you actually paid.
- *FICA* :: Payroll tax funding Social Security (6.2%) and Medicare (1.45%).
- *Itemized deductions* :: Listing actual deductible expenses instead of taking the standard
  deduction.
- *Long-term capital gain* :: Gain on something held over a year. Taxed at preferential rates,
  possibly 0%.
- *MAGI* :: Modified AGI. AGI with certain items added back. Defined differently per program.
- *Marginal tax rate* :: The rate on your next dollar of income.
- *MFJ / MFS* :: Married Filing Jointly / Married Filing Separately.
- *Progressive tax* :: Higher rates apply only to income above each threshold.
- *QBI deduction* :: Qualified Business Income deduction. Up to 20% of self-employment income.
- *Refundable credit* :: A credit that can pay you money beyond your tax liability.
- *Safe harbor* :: Paying enough during the year to avoid an underpayment penalty.
- *Schedule C* :: The form reporting self-employment income and expenses.
- *SE tax* :: Self-employment tax. 15.3% — both halves of FICA, paid by the self-employed.
- *Standard deduction* :: A flat deduction requiring no documentation.
- *Withholding* :: Tax taken from your paycheck and sent to the government in advance.
- *W-2* :: The form reporting an employee's wages and withholding.
- *W-4* :: The form telling your employer how much to withhold.
- *1099* :: A family of forms reporting non-wage income.
- *EIN* :: Employer Identification Number. A business's tax ID. Free from the IRS. Use it instead of your SSN on W-9s.
- *K-1* :: The form reporting a partner's or S-corp shareholder's share of business income.
- *LLC* :: Limited Liability Company. A state-registered entity that limits personal liability. By itself it changes nothing about your taxes.
- *S-corp election* :: A tax election letting an owner split income between salary and distributions, saving self-employment tax.
- *Stepped-up basis* :: An inherited asset's cost basis resets to its date-of-death value, erasing prior gains.

** Investing

- *Asset allocation* :: How you divide money among stocks, bonds, and cash.
- *Bond* :: A loan to a government or company that pays interest.
- *Brokerage account* :: An account for buying investments. No special tax treatment.
- *Diversification* :: Spreading money across many investments so no single failure matters.
- *Dividend* :: A distribution of company profits to shareholders.
- *Dollar-cost averaging* :: Investing a fixed amount on a regular schedule.
- *ETF* :: Exchange-Traded Fund. A fund that trades like a stock.
- *Expense ratio* :: A fund's annual fee, as a percentage. Lower is better. Under 0.20%.
- *Index fund* :: A fund that mechanically holds everything in a market index. Cheap. Effective.
- *Inflation* :: The general rise in prices; the erosion of a dollar's purchasing power.
- *Mutual fund* :: A pooled investment holding many securities.
- *Nominal return* :: The stated return, before adjusting for inflation.
- *Real return* :: Return after inflation. The one that matters.
- *Rebalancing* :: Restoring your target allocation after drift.
- *Rule of 72* :: Divide 72 by the return rate to get the years to double.
- *SIPC* :: Insurance protecting brokerage assets if the brokerage fails. Not against losses.
- *Stock* :: A share of ownership in a company.
- *Target-date fund* :: A single fund that automatically shifts allocation as a date approaches.
- *Tax-loss harvesting* :: Selling losers to offset gains for tax purposes.
- *Volatility* :: How much a price moves around. Not the same as risk.
- *Wash sale* :: Rebuying a security within 30 days of selling it at a loss, disallowing the
  loss.
- *Cliff (vesting)* :: A date before which nothing vests. Leave a day early and you get nothing.
- *Concentration risk* :: Having too much of your net worth in one asset — classically, your employer's stock.
- *ESPP* :: Employee Stock Purchase Plan. Buy company stock at a discount, usually 15%. Usually a good deal.
- *ISO / NSO* :: The two kinds of stock option. ISOs can trigger AMT; NSOs are taxed as ordinary income at exercise.
- *RSU* :: Restricted Stock Unit. A promise of shares that vest over time. Taxed as ordinary income at vest.
- *Strike price* :: The fixed price at which a stock option lets you buy shares.

** Retirement

- *401(k)* :: Employer retirement plan at a for-profit company.
- *403(b)* :: The same thing at a nonprofit, school, or mosad.
- *Backdoor Roth* :: Contributing to a traditional IRA and converting to Roth, to bypass Roth
  income limits.
- *Employer match* :: Money your employer adds to your retirement account. Free money.
- *HSA* :: Health Savings Account. Triple tax advantage. Requires a high-deductible health plan.
- *IRA* :: Individual Retirement Arrangement. An account you open yourself.
- *RMD* :: Required Minimum Distribution. Forced withdrawals from traditional accounts starting
  in your seventies.
- *Rollover* :: Moving retirement money between accounts without triggering tax.
- *Roth* :: After-tax contributions, tax-free growth, tax-free qualified withdrawals.
- *SEP-IRA* :: A simple high-limit retirement plan for the self-employed.
- *Solo 401(k)* :: A 401(k) for a self-employed person with no employees. Highest limits.
- *Spousal IRA* :: An IRA funded for a non-earning spouse using the earning spouse's income.
- *Traditional* :: Pretax contributions, tax-deferred growth, taxed withdrawals.
- *Vesting* :: When employer contributions actually become yours.

** Insurance

- *Beneficiary* :: Who receives a payout when you die. Overrides your will.
- *Coinsurance* :: Your percentage share of costs after the deductible.
- *Copay* :: A flat fee per service.
- *Deductible* :: What you pay before insurance starts paying.
- *Elimination period* :: The waiting period before disability benefits begin.
- *EOB* :: Explanation of Benefits. A statement from the insurer. Not a bill.
- *HDHP* :: High-Deductible Health Plan. Required for an HSA.
- *Out-of-pocket maximum* :: The most you can pay in a year. The number that defines your worst
  case.
- *Own occupation* :: Disability coverage that pays if you can't do *your* job specifically.
- *Premium* :: What you pay for coverage.
- *Rider* :: An add-on to a policy.
- *Term life insurance* :: Pure death benefit for a fixed period. Cheap. Usually correct.
- *Umbrella policy* :: Extra liability coverage on top of home and auto. Cheap, large.
- *FMLA* :: Family and Medical Leave Act. 12 weeks of *unpaid*, job-protected leave. Not a paycheck.
- *Gap insurance* :: Covers the difference between what you owe on a car and what insurance pays if it's totaled.
- *Long-term care* :: Help with daily living over months or years. Not covered by health insurance or Medicare.
- *Medicare* :: Health coverage at 65+. Parts A (hospital), B (doctors), C (Advantage), D (drugs).
- *Medigap* :: Private insurance covering the gaps in Original Medicare.
- *Paid family leave* :: A state program paying wage replacement for bonding with a new child. Separate from disability and from FMLA.
- *Short-term disability* :: What actually pays during maternity leave. Cannot usually be added once pregnant.
- *Underwriting* :: The insurer assessing your risk to set price and eligibility.
- *Whole life insurance* :: Permanent coverage with a cash value component. Expensive.

** Housing

- *Amortization schedule* :: The table showing principal and interest for every payment.
- *ARM* :: Adjustable-Rate Mortgage. The rate changes after an initial period.
- *Appraisal* :: The lender's valuation of the property.
- *Closing costs* :: 2–5% of the purchase price, due at closing, on top of the down payment.
- *Escrow* :: An account holding your tax and insurance money, paid out by the lender.
- *Inspection* :: Your own assessment of the property's condition. Never skip it.
- *LTV* :: Loan-to-Value. Loan amount divided by property value.
- *PITI* :: Principal, Interest, Taxes, Insurance. The full mortgage payment.
- *PMI* :: Private Mortgage Insurance. Required under 20% down. Protects the lender, not you.
- *Points* :: Prepaid interest to lower the rate.
- *Pre-approval* :: A lender's conditional commitment based on verified information.
- *Refinance* :: Replacing a mortgage with a new one.
- *Title insurance* :: Protection against defects in the property's ownership history.
- *Depreciation* :: The loss of an asset's value over time. The largest and most invisible cost of owning a car.
- *Out-the-door price* :: The total price of a car including every fee and tax. The only number worth negotiating.
- *Underwater* :: Owing more on a loan than the asset is worth.

** Estate

- *Durable power of attorney* :: Authorizes someone to handle your finances if you're
  incapacitated.
- *Executor* :: The person who administers your estate.
- *Guardian* :: The person who raises your minor children if you die.
- *Healthcare proxy* :: The person who makes medical decisions if you can't.
- *Intestate* :: Dying without a will. The state decides everything.
- *Probate* :: The court process of administering an estate.
- *Trust* :: An arrangement where a trustee holds assets for beneficiaries under your terms.
- *Will* :: A document directing who inherits and who raises your children.
- *ABLE account* :: A tax-advantaged account for a person with a disability that doesn't count against SSI and Medicaid limits.
- *Letter of intent* :: A plain-language description of a dependent's needs and life, for whoever takes over. Not a legal document.
- *QDRO* :: The court order that divides an employer retirement plan in a divorce without triggering tax.
- *Special needs trust* :: A trust holding assets for a disabled person without those assets counting as theirs.

** Israel

- *Bituach Leumi* :: National Insurance. Israel's social security system.
- *FBAR* :: The report of foreign bank accounts, required if the aggregate exceeds $10,000.
- *FEIE* :: Foreign Earned Income Exclusion. Excludes a large amount of earned income from US
  tax.
- *FTC* :: Foreign Tax Credit. A credit for income taxes paid to another country.
- *Keren hishtalmut* :: Israeli "study fund." Tax-free after six years. Excellent.
- *Keren pensia* :: Israeli mandatory pension fund.
- *Kitzbat yeladim* :: Israel's universal child allowance.
- *Kupat cholim* :: One of Israel's four health funds.
- *Kupat gemel* :: An Israeli provident/savings fund.
- *Mas hachnasa* :: Israeli income tax.
- *Mas rechisha* :: Israeli property purchase tax. Reduced for olim.
- *Mashkanta* :: An Israeli mortgage.
- *Nekudot zikui* :: Israeli tax credit points. Reduce tax owed directly.
- *PFIC* :: Passive Foreign Investment Company. Punitive US tax treatment of most foreign funds.
- *Sal klita* :: The absorption basket paid to new olim.
- *Tzamud madad* :: CPI-linked. A loan whose principal rises with inflation.
- *Dynamic currency conversion* :: When a foreign terminal offers to charge you in dollars. Always decline; choose the local currency.
- *Hora'at keva* :: An Israeli standing order or direct debit. How recurring bills get paid.
- *Mid-market rate* :: The real exchange rate. Compare every transfer against it to see what you're actually being charged.
- *Minus* :: An Israeli bank overdraft. Culturally normal, financially still debt.
- *Tashlumim* :: Paying in installments, offered on ordinary Israeli purchases, often interest-free.

** Frum-specific

- *Chomesh* :: Giving a fifth rather than a tenth to tzedakah.
- *Gemach* :: A community fund making interest-free loans (or lending objects).
- *Heter iska* :: A document restructuring a loan as a joint venture to permit interest.
- *Maaser* :: Tithing a portion of income to tzedakah.
- *Parsonage* :: A clergy housing allowance excludable from federal income tax.
- *Ribbis* :: The prohibition on interest between Jews.
- *Yerusha* :: Halachic inheritance.

* APPENDIX B — THE NUMBERS

#+BEGIN_QUOTE
*READ THIS BEFORE USING ANY NUMBER BELOW.*

These figures change every year. The values here are my best understanding for *2026* at the
time of writing, and several are approximations. Some may already be wrong.

*Every one of them can be verified in under sixty seconds.* Do that before you act on any of
them. The verification sources are listed with each block. I would rather you check and find
I was slightly off than trust a stale number in a decision that matters.
#+END_QUOTE

** B.1 Retirement contribution limits (2026)

| Item                                    | Amount     |
|-----------------------------------------+------------|
| 401(k)/403(b) employee deferral         | $24,500    |
| 401(k) catch-up, age 50+                | $8,000     |
| 401(k) enhanced catch-up, ages 60–63    | $11,250    |
| IRA contribution (per person)           | $7,500     |
| IRA catch-up, age 50+                   | $1,100     |
| Total 401(k) additions (employee+employer) | ~$72,000 |
| SIMPLE IRA employee deferral            | ~$17,000   |
| HSA, self-only                          | $4,400     |
| HSA, family                             | $8,750     |
| HSA catch-up, age 55+                   | $1,000     |
| Health FSA                              | ~$3,400    |
| Dependent Care FSA                      | $5,000     |

*Verify:* search "IRS retirement plan contribution limits [year]" — the IRS publishes a
newsroom release each November for the following year. For HSAs, search "IRS Revenue
Procedure HSA inflation adjusted amounts [year]."

** B.2 Federal income tax, married filing jointly (2026, approximate)

| Rate | Taxable income (approximate)     |
|------+----------------------------------|
| 10%  | $0 – ~$24,800                    |
| 12%  | ~$24,800 – ~$100,800             |
| 22%  | ~$100,800 – ~$211,100            |
| 24%  | ~$211,100 – ~$402,600            |
| 32%  | ~$402,600 – ~$511,300            |
| 35%  | ~$511,300 – ~$767,000            |
| 37%  | above ~$767,000                  |

Standard deduction, MFJ: approximately *$32,200*. Single: approximately *$16,100*.

*These bracket figures are approximations and should be verified.* Search "IRS Revenue
Procedure inflation adjustments [year]" or use any tax software, which will always have the
current values.

*Remember:* these apply to *taxable* income (after the standard deduction), and only the
dollars inside each band are taxed at that band's rate (§4.2).

** B.3 Long-term capital gains, married filing jointly (2026, approximate)

| Rate | Taxable income        |
|------+-----------------------|
| 0%   | up to ~$98,900        |
| 15%  | ~$98,900 – ~$613,700  |
| 20%  | above that            |

*The 0% band is real and worth planning around during low-income years* (§7.8).

** B.4 Payroll tax (2026)

| Item                              | Amount              |
|-----------------------------------+---------------------|
| Social Security tax rate          | 6.2% each side      |
| Social Security wage base         | ~$184,500           |
| Medicare tax rate                 | 1.45% each side     |
| Additional Medicare (above $250K MFJ) | 0.9% employee only |
| Self-employment tax total         | 15.3%               |
| Earnings needed per SS credit     | ~$1,900             |
| Credits needed to qualify         | 40 (i.e. ~10 years) |

*Verify:* =ssa.gov= publishes a "Fact Sheet: Social Security Changes" each October.

** B.5 Credits (2026, approximate)

| Credit                              | Amount                          |
|-------------------------------------+---------------------------------|
| Child Tax Credit                    | ~$2,200 per child under 17      |
| Refundable portion (ACTC)           | ~$1,700 per child               |
| EITC max, 3+ children               | ~$8,200                         |
| EITC max, 2 children                | ~$7,300                         |
| EITC max, 1 child                   | ~$4,400                         |
| American Opportunity Credit         | up to $2,500, 40% refundable    |
| Lifetime Learning Credit            | up to $2,000, nonrefundable     |
| Saver's Credit                      | 10–50% of up to $2,000 contributed |

EITC income limits depend on filing status and number of children and change annually. *Do
not estimate eligibility from memory* — use the IRS EITC Assistant at =irs.gov/eitc= or let
tax software determine it.

** B.6 Gift and estate (2026)

| Item                                      | Amount             |
|-------------------------------------------+--------------------|
| Annual gift tax exclusion, per giver per recipient | ~$19,000  |
| Lifetime gift/estate exemption, per person | ~$15,000,000      |

So two parents can give a married couple roughly four times the annual exclusion — around
$76,000 a year — with no tax and no filing. Above the annual exclusion the *giver* files Form
709 but owes nothing until lifetime gifts exceed the exemption.

Note: several states have their own estate or inheritance taxes with far lower thresholds.

** B.7 Foreign / expatriate (2026, approximate)

| Item                                    | Amount                     |
|-----------------------------------------+----------------------------|
| Foreign Earned Income Exclusion         | ~$133,000 per person       |
| FBAR filing threshold (aggregate)       | $10,000 — *never indexed*  |
| Form 8938 threshold, MFJ living abroad  | ~$400,000 year-end / $600,000 any time |
| Form 8938 threshold, MFJ living in US   | ~$100,000 year-end / $150,000 any time |

*The $10,000 FBAR threshold has never been adjusted for inflation and is very easy to cross.*

** B.8 Rules of thumb (these don't change)

| Rule                       | Value                                              |
|----------------------------+----------------------------------------------------|
| Long-run stock return      | ~10% nominal, ~7% real                             |
| Long-run inflation         | ~3%                                                |
| Rule of 72                 | 72 ÷ return = years to double                      |
| Emergency fund             | 3–6 months of expenses                             |
| Housing cost               | ≤ 25% of net income, all-in                        |
| Home maintenance           | 1–2% of home value per year                        |
| Unrecoverable cost of owning | ~5% of home value per year                       |
| Savings rate target        | 15%+ of gross income toward retirement             |
| Life insurance             | 10–12× income, or DIME method                      |
| Safe withdrawal in retirement | ~4% of the portfolio per year                   |
| Expense ratio ceiling      | 0.20%, ideally under 0.10%                         |
| Debt payoff threshold      | Above 8% interest, pay it before investing         |

| Withholding calculator         | irs.gov/individuals/tax-withholding-estimator |

| Check a broker or advisor      | brokercheck.finra.org                   |
| Check an investment advisor    | adviserinfo.sec.gov                     |
| Unclaimed property             | unclaimed.org                           |
| Consumer complaints            | consumerfinance.gov                     |

** Credit freezes

- Equifax: equifax.com/personal/credit-report-services/credit-freeze/
- Experian: experian.com/freeze/
- TransUnion: transunion.com/credit-freeze

** Finding a professional

- Fee-only fiduciary advisors: napfa.org
- Advisors who work with younger clients: xyplanningnetwork.com
- Nonprofit credit counseling: nfcc.org
- Free tax prep (income-limited): search "IRS VITA site locator"

** Books worth the time

- /The Simple Path to Wealth/ — JL Collins. The best single book on investing for a beginner.
  Short, clear, and correct.
- /I Will Teach You to Be Rich/ — Ramit Sethi. Excellent on automation and systems. Ignore the
  title.
- /The Psychology of Money/ — Morgan Housel. Why people behave irrationally with money. The
  best-written book on this list.
- /The Bogleheads' Guide to Investing/ — Comprehensive, practical, slightly dry.
- /Your Money or Your Life/ — Vicki Robin. On the relationship between money and time.

** Online

- bogleheads.org/wiki — the single best free reference on investing and US personal finance.
  Genuinely excellent and utterly non-commercial.
- reddit.com/r/personalfinance — the wiki and the "Prime Directive" flowchart are good.
- levels.fyi — tech compensation data.
- nefeshbnefesh.org — aliyah guidance, including detailed financial material.

** For US–Israel matters

Search for CPAs or Enrolled Agents specializing in "US Israel cross border tax." There are
firms that do only this. It is a genuine specialty and it is worth paying for. Ask
specifically about their experience with PFICs, keren hishtalmut reporting, and the FEIE
versus Foreign Tax Credit decision for families with children.

* APPENDIX D — WORKSHEETS AND CHEAT SHEETS

** D.1 Monthly cash flow worksheet

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
INCOME (net, what actually arrives)
  Spouse 1 take-home                        $________
  Spouse 2 take-home / kollel               $________
  Side income                               $________
  Other (support, benefits)                 $________
                                    TOTAL:  $________

FIXED COSTS
  Rent / mortgage                           $________
  Utilities (elec, gas, water)              $________
  Phone / internet                          $________
  Insurance (health, auto, renters, life)   $________
  Car payment                               $________
  Tuition / childcare                       $________
  Debt payments                             $________
  Subscriptions                             $________
                                    TOTAL:  $________

SAVINGS (pay yourself first)
  Maaser                                    $________
  Emergency fund                            $________
  Sinking funds (from D.2)                  $________
  Retirement                                $________
                                    TOTAL:  $________

SPENDING MONEY (what's left)
  Income − Fixed − Savings =                $________
  ↑ This is the number that goes to the Spending account each month.
#+END_EXAMPLE

                                    OFFER A       OFFER B

  ------------------------------------------------------

  Also weigh, without numbers:

** D.5 The insurance pitch checklist

Print this. Bring it to the meeting.

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] What exactly does this cover, in one sentence?
[ ] What is the premium, and can it ever increase?
[ ] How are YOU compensated on this, versus the alternatives?
[ ] Which numbers in this illustration are GUARANTEED, and which are projected?
[ ] What is this same coverage as plain term insurance? Show me both quotes.
[ ] What are the surrender charges, and for how many years?
[ ] What happens to the cash value when I die?
[ ] What happens if I stop paying in year three? Year ten?
[ ] Can I have all of this in writing to review at home?

RULE: Do not sign anything at this meeting. Not one thing.
#+END_EXAMPLE

** D.6 Before you buy a house

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Down payment saved                              $______
[ ] Closing costs saved (2–5% of price)             $______
[ ] Emergency fund STILL INTACT after both          $______
[ ] Moving and setup budget                         $______

[ ] All-in monthly cost calculated:
      Principal + interest                          $______
      Property tax ÷ 12                             $______
      Insurance ÷ 12                                $______
      PMI (if under 20% down)                       $______
      HOA                                           $______
      Maintenance reserve (1% of value ÷ 12)        $______
                                          TOTAL:    $______
[ ] That total is ≤ 25% of NET monthly income?      Y / N
[ ] With tuition added, does the budget still work? Y / N

[ ] Pre-approved (not just pre-qualified)
[ ] Quotes from at least 3 lenders, within 14 days
[ ] Loan Estimates compared side by side (APR + total closing costs)
[ ] Independent home inspection scheduled — and I will attend
[ ] Wire instructions verified BY PHONE at a number I looked up myself
[ ] Attorney engaged (required in NY/NJ)
[ ] Homeowners insurance bound before closing
#+END_EXAMPLE

** D.7 The new baby checklist

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] Apply for the Social Security Number (do it at the hospital)
[ ] Add the baby to health insurance — THERE IS A DEADLINE (usually 30–60 days)
[ ] Update life insurance beneficiaries and amounts
[ ] Update or create your will and guardianship designation
[ ] Freeze the baby's credit at all three bureaus
[ ] Re-check eligibility for WIC, Medicaid/CHIP, and other programs
[ ] Update the W-4 for the additional Child Tax Credit
[ ] Increase the tuition sinking fund
[ ] Start (or increase) the chasunah fund — $130/month, 20 years, done
[ ] Check whether your employer offers a Dependent Care FSA
#+END_EXAMPLE

** D.8 Buying a car

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
BEFORE YOU LOOK
  Maximum out-the-door price I will pay:        $________
  (Written down, agreed with my wife, before I talk to anyone)

  Down payment available:                       $________
  Loan term, maximum 4 years:                   ___ months
  All-in transportation cost as % of gross income (target ≤10%):  ___%

TOTAL COST OF OWNERSHIP, per year
  Depreciation (estimate)                       $________
  Insurance (get a quote BEFORE buying)         $________
  Fuel                                          $________
  Maintenance + repairs reserve                 $________
  Registration, inspection, tolls, parking      $________
                                       TOTAL:   $________
                                     PER MONTH: $________

CHECKLIST
[ ] Pre-approved financing from a credit union in hand
[ ] Reliability research done on this specific model year
[ ] Out-the-door quotes requested by email from 4–6 dealers
[ ] Vehicle history report (used)
[ ] INDEPENDENT pre-purchase inspection by MY mechanic (used) — non-negotiable
[ ] Open recalls checked at nhtsa.gov
[ ] Car seats physically test-fitted, if this is the family vehicle
[ ] Negotiated out-the-door price only — never answered "what monthly payment?"
[ ] Trade-in negotiated separately, after the price was settled
[ ] Declined everything in the finance office
[ ] Read the contract line by line before signing
[ ] Was genuinely prepared to walk out
#+END_EXAMPLE

** D.9 If the income stops: the first 72 hours

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
[ ] File for unemployment TODAY. Benefits often run from the filing date. (§16.1)
[ ] Do not sign a severance agreement without reading it. You can take a few days.
[ ] Health insurance: compare COBRA vs. marketplace vs. spouse's plan vs. Medicaid.
    Job loss is a qualifying event — there is a deadline. (§6.3)
[ ] Do NOT cash out the 401(k). Leave it or roll it. (§8.11)
[ ] Check equity/option exercise deadlines — often 90 days. (§10.9)
[ ] Triage the budget into MUST PAY / PAUSE / NEGOTIATE. (§16.1)
[ ] Call every creditor BEFORE missing a payment. Ask for hardship options.
    Write down who, when, and what was agreed.
[ ] Re-check benefit eligibility at the new income level. (§5.1)
[ ] Tell your wife everything. Today. (§16.7)
#+END_EXAMPLE

** D.10 The one-page summary

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MONEY, ON ONE PAGE                                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                              │
  │  SPEND less than you earn. Automate it so you can't forget.  │
  │                                                              │
  │  SAVE first: maaser, emergency fund, sinking funds,          │
  │       retirement — all before the spending account is filled.│
  │                                                              │
  │  BORROW almost never. Never carry a card balance.            │
  │                                                              │
  │  INSURE what would ruin you. Refuse the rest. Term, not      │
  │       whole. Ask how they're paid.                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  INVEST in cheap index funds. Then don't touch them.         │
  │       Time is the asset. You are 24. That is the whole edge. │
  │                                                              │
  │  TAXES are rules, not weather. Learn them. Claim your        │
  │       credits. File even when you think you needn't.         │
  │                                                              │
  │  EARN more. It's the biggest lever and the least discussed.  │
  │                                                              │
  │  PROTECT your family: a will, a guardian, a beneficiary      │
  │       form, and a document saying where everything is.       │
  │                                                              │
  │  TALK about it with your wife, once a month, calmly.         │
  │                                                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
#+END_EXAMPLE

* AFTERWORD

Two things to leave you with.

*First:* almost nobody knows this material. Not your neighbors, not most of the people giving
confident financial advice at kiddush, not the guy selling insurance. The bar is low, the
information is public, and you have now read more about how money works than most adults ever
will. That advantage is real, and it compounds the same way the money does.

*Second:* the arithmetic in this document is straightforward and the discipline is not. What
will actually determine your family's financial life over the next forty years is not whether
you chose the optimal fund or shaved a quarter point off a mortgage. It's whether you save
consistently, avoid the few catastrophic mistakes — high-interest debt, an underinsured
disaster, a fraud, cashing out retirement accounts — and keep earning.

You have a baby, a wife who is working, a sugya you're in the middle of, and a degree to
finish. Money should be a solved background process, not a subject that occupies you. That's
the actual goal of everything above: build the system once, automate it, review it briefly
each month, and then go do the things you're actually here to do.

Start with the "this week" list in §18.1. It's three hours. Everything else can follow.
